## 转写原文（修正版）

以下为本期转写全文：英文为已应用校正表的修正版原转写（长近三小时，87 段），每段之下附中文全译（【译】起）。含节目广告口播，一并保留。

[0s - 120s]

The following is a conversation with Preston Stewart and Michael Kofman about the war in Ukraine. Preston is a West Point graduate and Afghanistan veteran who covers the war extensively on his YouTube channel called Preston Stewart, his podcast called War and Conflict, and his newsletter called Defense Tech News. Michael is a world class military analysis expert specializing in the Russian and Ukrainian militaries. He is a senior fellow at the Carnegie Endowment and he hosts the Russia Contingency podcast on the War on the Rocks platform. Please follow and support their work. This is the Lex Friedman podcast. To support it, please check out our sponsors in the description, where you can also find links to contact me, ask questions, give feedback, and so on. And now, dear friends, here's Preston Stewart and Michael Kofman. Gentlemen, thank you for doing this late night conversation. Let's see how long we go. The war in Ukraine is in its fifth year. Let's lay out what is the state of the war in terms of territories, in terms of battles, in terms of everything that you talk about from a military perspective. Mike, do you want to go first? Yeah, sure. I think that the war had probably an inflection point last year, and most people recognize that this year for Ukraine is the best year of combat performance for them on the ground since probably about the summer 2023 offensive. But there's a dichotomy to it because on the one hand, Ukraine is doing really well along the front line, but it faces the challenge of an expansive Russian strike campaign, and the cumul of damage that has been inflicted to Ukrainian infrastructure, both energy and all sorts of civilian types of infrastructure, over the course of several years. And so the winter is quite uncertain on what's going to happen in Ukraine's population centers. Now I know Ukraine has proven.

【译】以下是与普雷斯顿·斯图尔特和迈克尔·科夫曼关于俄乌战争的对话。普雷斯顿是西点军校毕业生、阿富汗战争老兵，他通过自己的 YouTube 频道"普雷斯顿·斯图尔特"、播客《战争与冲突》以及新闻通讯《防务科技新闻》对这场战争做了大量报道。迈克尔是世界一流的军事分析专家，专攻俄罗斯与乌克兰军队。他是卡内基国际和平基金会高级研究员，在 War on the Rocks 平台主持《俄罗斯应急》播客。请关注并支持他们的工作。这里是莱克斯·弗里德曼播客。要支持本节目，请查看节目描述中的赞助商信息，你还可以在那里找到联系我、提问、给出反馈等链接。好了，亲爱的朋友们，有请普雷斯顿·斯图尔特与迈克尔·科夫曼。二位，谢谢你们来参加这场深夜对谈，看看我们能聊多久。俄乌战争已进入第五个年头。我们来铺陈一下战争的现状——就领土、就战役、就你们从军事角度谈论的一切而言。迈克，你先讲？——好，可以。我认为这场战争大概在去年出现了一个拐点，多数人也已认识到：今年是乌克兰自 2023 年夏季攻势以来地面作战表现最好的一年。但这其中存在两重性：一方面，乌克兰在前线打得相当不错；另一方面，它面临俄军大规模打击行动的挑战，以及数年来累积在乌克兰基础设施——既包括能源设施，也包括各类民用设施——上的破坏。因此，这个冬天乌克兰的人口密集城镇会发生什么，相当不确定。我知道乌克兰已经证明……

[120s - 240s]

Resilient and is going to get through this winter, but the level of damage to the economy remains unclear. The Russian military is doing quite poorly this year in the war. For them, this is probably their worst year of combat performance since winter of 2023, and it's clear that the way they've been fighting has reached diminishing returns. Not in 2026, but I would probably peg it as far back as summer of 2025. The increasing will face some manpower issues, and their effort now fixates more on strategic bombardment, which, while inflicting a lot of damage on Ukraine, has historically a challenge in that strategic bombardment does not have a great track record, or even a good track record, of achieving maximums political aims in the war, especially if a ground campaign is unsuccessful or is floundering as Russia's is at this stage of the conflict. So the history of strategic bombardment as a way to try to win the war in the absence of the success of other efforts is not a good one. Although, as an analyst, I'll tell you most political leaders always think that it might work for them, even if you tell them that it doesn't, and so it's unsurprising to see that Russia is now leaning heavily towards strategic bombardment as a key pillar of its strategy. I think it would also be nice to lay out the basics every time you bring them up. Sure. Maybe can you give a quick tangent of the 2023 offensive and then what strategic bombardment is, as opposed to the other mechanisms of war? Sure. So in 2023, there were a series of fairly set-piece offensives and counter-offensives. After a partial mobilization with manpower replenished and some reserves established, Russia attempted a winter offensive, but its forces were unready, and so the winter campaign for Russia in 2023 was largely a failure, perhaps with the exception of the grinding battle over the city of Bakhmut. Then Ukraine launched a much anticipated summer offensive using units that.

【译】了自己是有韧性的，会挺过这个冬天，但对经济的损害程度仍不明朗。俄军今年在战争中的表现相当差。对他们来说，这大概是 2023 年冬季以来作战表现最差的一年，而且很清楚，他们的打法已经到了收益递减的地步——不是 2026 年才如此，我会把时间点往前推到 2025 年夏季。俄方将日益面临兵员问题，他们现在的努力更多聚焦于战略轰炸。战略轰炸固然给乌克兰造成大量破坏，但它在历史上一直有个难题：战略轰炸在实现战争最大政治目标方面没有什么像样的记录，甚至连"不错"都谈不上——尤其是当地面战役不成功、像俄罗斯目前在冲突这个阶段那样陷于困顿的时候。所以说，在其他努力未获成功的情况下，想靠战略轰炸赢下战争，历史上的成绩单并不好看。不过，作为分析师我可以告诉你：大多数政治领导人总是觉得这招到自己手上也许就管用，哪怕你明确告诉他们不管用。因此看到俄罗斯如今把战略轰炸当作其战略的关键支柱重注押上，并不令人意外。——我觉得每当你抛出这些基础概念时，把基本盘铺一下也挺好。——好的。也许可以先简短岔开讲讲 2023 年的那场攻势，再讲讲战略轰炸是什么、与战争的其他机制有何不同？——好。2023 年发生了一连串相当"摆好阵型"式的攻势与反攻。在部分动员之后，俄军补充了兵员、建立了一定的预备队，随后尝试发动冬季攻势，但部队没有准备好，所以俄罗斯 2023 年的冬季战局大体上是失败的，也许唯有巴赫穆特城下的拉锯消耗战算是例外。然后乌克兰发起了备受期待的夏季攻势，投入的部队……

[240s - 360s]

That were freshly trained and equipped by Western countries, and there was a great deal of kind of hope and expectations that were placed upon this offensive, but it too failed. It lasted several months. I think in reality it went from June 2023 to about November of 2023, and then Russia tried to seize the initiative again and started some offensive in fall 2023 with the Battle of Dnipro. But so summer of 2023 is one of the periods where we still see an exchange in terms of initiative between the two sides and fairly clear-cut major offensive operations and counter-offensive operations. In 2023, early on, you have a failed Russian winter offensive. This is probably one of the worst points for the Russian military in the war. Fall to winter, fall 2022 through winter 2023, and then you have the Ukrainian summer 2023 offensive, where Ukraine does gain some ground, but it's not what people hoped for. Um, and since then, for much of the war, you know, you see Russia trying to establish and having an advantage in manpower and material, and for March of 24 and 25, Russia actually tends to have the initiative, even though it's largely pursuing a grinding offensive on a very broad front. And if I sort of fast forward and take us into 2026, you really start to see the inflection point probably around fall of 2025, and then in 2026, the Russian effort is it floundering, and Russian forces face significant reversals, where the battle space a lot more dynamic this year. Ukraine has two of its own localized offensives running, and you see there's actually much greater fluidity in 2026 on the battlefield, certainly at the tactical level, than you would in 25 or 24. There's often a perception that just a positional stalemate, but in fact, even more recently, some of Ukraine's offensive operations had gained more terrain than Ukrainian forces were able to do during the summer 2023 offensive, which was planned to be a much bigger operation. Also.

【译】刚刚由西方国家完成训练和装备。外界对这次攻势寄予了大量希望与期待，但它同样失败了。它持续了好几个月，实际上大概从 2023 年 6 月打到 2023 年 11 月前后。随后俄罗斯试图重新夺取主动权，在 2023 年秋冬依托第聂伯河之战发起了一些攻势。2023 年夏季仍是双方主动权易手、且有相当分明的大规模攻势与反攻行动的时期之一。2023 年早些时候，是俄军冬季攻势的失败——那大概是俄军在这场战争中最糟糕的时段之一，从 2022 年秋季一直延续到 2023 年冬季。接着是乌克兰 2023 年夏季攻势，乌克兰确实夺占了一些土地，但远低于人们的期望。自那以后，在战争的大部分时间里，你会看到俄罗斯努力建立并保有人力与物力优势；在 2024 年和 2025 年，主动权大体上其实在俄罗斯手里，尽管它主要是在极宽的正面上推行一场磨盘式攻势。若把时间快进到 2026 年，拐点真正显现大约是在 2025 年秋季前后；进入 2026 年，俄军的行动陷入困顿，俄军部队遭遇重大挫败，今年的战场空间活跃了许多。乌克兰眼下有两场自己的局部攻势在进行中。你会发现 2026 年战场的流动性明显更大——至少在战术层面——远超 2025 年或 2024 年。人们常常以为这只是一场阵地战的僵局，但实际上，就在不久前，乌克兰的一些攻势行动夺占的地盘，比乌军在 2023 年夏季攻势期间拿到的还要多——而那场攻势原计划是规模大得多的战役。另外……

[360s - 480s]

So it might be useful to kind of state, perhaps the obvious, but the nature of this war. One of the things, especially as it evolved, the role of drones on the front, and maybe this is where you can loop in strategic bombardment as well. Like, what are the main mechanisms of war here? So we look at this war beyond what's happening at the front line. Russia has, from the very beginning of the war, had a series of strikes, an expansive strike campaign, but it really begins involved twenty twenty two, where they begin launching combination strikes of long range air launched cruise missiles and short range ballistic missiles against Ukrainian critical infrastructure. And by critical infrastructure, I mean power plants, substations, various types of heating plants, things that a country needs in order to sustain the economy and the population. And over time, those targets expand. Right? Russia targets various types of civilian infrastructure, and actually, at this point in twenty twenty six, we've seen that campaign really broaden to include warehouses, data centers, schools, medical facilities, malls. Actually, quite a few shopping malls in the last few months. Trains. So it's it's gone far beyond energy infrastructure to include a host of other civilian types of targets, and it changed over time from being primarily done with cruise missile and ballistic missiles to being mostly conducted via saturation strikes using large numbers of one way attack drones. And those drones themselves evolved in terms of the systems, gone now from being largely done with cheaper, easier to shoot down propeller driven drones to ones that are jet powered and are in effect light ground launched cruise missiles and a much larger number of ballistic missiles, some which are more sophisticated. And so Ukraine's been facing both large combination.

【译】——所以，把这场战争的性质说明白也许是有用的，尽管可能显而易见，尤其是随着战争演变，无人机在前线扮演的角色；也许你还可以在这里把战略轰炸也串进来讲。这场战争的主要机制是什么？——我们看这场战争，不能只看前线发生的事。从战争一开始，俄罗斯就实施了一系列打击、一场大规模打击行动，但真正成体系是始于 2022 年：他们开始用远程空射巡航导弹与近程弹道导弹，对乌克兰的关键基础设施发动组合打击。我说的关键基础设施，是指电厂、变电站、各类供热厂——一个国家维持经济运转与民生所必需的东西。随着时间推移，目标清单不断扩大：俄罗斯打击各类民用基础设施，而到了 2026 年的这个时点，我们看到这场打击行动的范围确实大大拓宽，涵盖仓库、数据中心、学校、医疗设施、商场——事实上最近几个月就打了不止一家购物中心——还有火车。所以它早已远远超出能源基础设施，纳入了一大堆其他类型的民用目标。打击手段也随时间演变：从最初主要依靠巡航导弹和弹道导弹，变为如今主要通过大量单向攻击无人机实施的饱和打击。这些无人机本身在系统上也不断演进：从过去大多使用较便宜、易被击落的螺旋桨推进无人机，发展到喷气动力型——实际上就是轻型地面发射的巡航导弹——以及数量大得多的弹道导弹，其中一些更为精良。所以乌克兰面对的既是大规模的组合……

[480s - 600s]

Strikes, but also daily sustained pressure, constant attacks with jet-powered drones that generate alerts and that disrupt economic life and try to exhaust Ukraine's air defense. By the way, I forgot to mention that I'm a big fan of both of yours. It's an honor to have the both of you here, Mike and Preston. Preston, I've been watching you for a long time, so thank you for being here. So, yes, to ask you, do you have to build on what Mike was saying, and maybe can you also speak to some of the geographic areas that are in question here that are most contested? Yeah, absolutely, Lex. Honor to be here. Always a challenge to add anything to what Mike has to say. We'll give it a shot here. I think it's it's good to get an idea of the areas that we're going to be talking about in Ukraine. The fight still is predominantly in the east and the southeast. So, Kiev, kind of in the the middle of the country, is not facing direct Russian attacks in terms of of infantry and short range drones. They, of course, are on the receiving end daily of long range drones and missile strikes. But as you move through the border regions, starting in the north, there are Russian infiltrations in Sumy. There's also fighting in Ukrainian territory in Kharkiv, and then you get down into what's known as the Donbas, Luhansk, and Donetsk, and this is where some of the heaviest fighting throughout the war has taken place. And this is, I'm sure, we'll get into it later, but an area referred to as the Fortress Belt. The Ukraine has turned into just an absolutely brutal area for Russia to try to advance through, and is costing Russia a lot of people day in and day out. Moving from there, we're into Zaporizhia, and then Kherson butts up against the Black Sea, and some of the strategic bombardment that Mike was talking about. So that's beyond the even these areas. So even Kiev is being hit. So a lot of the places throughout Ukraine is being hit, and some in the western portion of the country, not as frequently. Kiev is a a focus. If you look along the front today, you have essentially some.

【译】打击，也是日复一日的持续压力：喷气动力无人机不停来袭，拉响警报、扰乱经济生活，并试图耗竭乌克兰的防空力量。——顺便说一句，我忘了说，我是你们两位的忠实粉丝。迈克、普雷斯顿，能把你们二位请来是我的荣幸。普雷斯顿，我看你的内容很久了，谢谢你来。——好，那想请你接着迈克的话往下讲，也许还能讲讲这里涉及的地理区域，哪些地方争夺最激烈？——没问题，莱克斯，很荣幸来这里。想在迈克讲的内容上再补充什么，始终是个挑战，我们试试吧。我觉得，先对我们接下来要谈的乌克兰各区域建立个概念很有好处。战斗至今仍主要集中在东部和东南部。基辅位于国土中部，就步兵和近程无人机而言，并不面临俄军的直接攻击——当然，它每天都在承受远程无人机和导弹打击。但沿着边境地带走：从北面开始，苏梅州有俄军渗透；哈尔科夫的乌克兰领土上也在交战；再往南就进入所谓顿巴斯——卢甘斯克与顿涅茨克，整场战争中一些最惨烈的战斗就发生在这里。还有一片地区——我确信我们后面会展开讲——叫"堡垒带"：乌克兰把它经营成了俄军推进路上的绝对绞肉区，日复一日地让俄罗斯付出惨重人员代价。从那里再往前就是扎波罗热，然后是紧贴黑海的赫尔松。而迈克刚才讲的部分战略轰炸，则超出了这些区域的范围——连基辅都在挨打。也就是说，乌克兰全境很多地方都在遭打击，包括西部一些地区，只是频次没那么高。基辅是打击的重点。如果你看今天的前线，基本上会有一些……

[600s - 720s]

Some Russian border incursions, trying to establish a small buffer across the border, which are lower intensity areas of fighting in Sumy, Kharkiv. You have an ongoing fighting around city of Kupiansk and Kharkiv region, and then you have the focus of Russian offensive efforts. Preston was saying, still trying to take the remainder of the Donbas, and that fighting ranges all the way down from cities like areas like Luhansk, all the way towards the main conglomeration of cities, and we should mention them now because I think they're going to come up that are remaining in the Donetsk region, which are Sloviansk, Kramatorsk, Dnipro, and Konstantinovka, which troops all colloquially call Konstata because its name's too long. And then further west of that is Dnipro, which is main city now that's been essentially an anchor in Ukrainian defense west of that conglomeration, with another chain of cities behind it. And if we head southwest, we get into the Dnipro region, and then Zaporizhia. And Zaporizhia is an interesting, fairly dynamic area because you have both Russian and Ukrainian forces on the offensive at the same time, where you have Russian forces trying to push west of Dnipro, and you have northeast of that Ukrainian forces having the initiative for much of the year. A tactical grouping of Ukrainian air assault brigades has been running kind of a creeping offensive there. This has been pretty successful in making gains. And the other area which we might get into later around Luhansk was Ukraine's third corps having conducted a successful offensive operation as well, called Bivaldi. So those are two areas where Ukraine's not only contesting the initiative but actually is liberating territory and in many ways wiping out Russian gains from 2025. And that's what I meant when I was saying earlier that this is a much more dynamic battle space than people think. In that territory is a lot more fluid. There's a vast gray zone, in fact, between the two lines. Ukraine does not hold cohesive defensive lines. It often has essentially pickets with small number of infantry.

【译】俄军越境突入的动向，他们试图在边境一侧建立小型缓冲带，这些属于苏梅、哈尔科夫方向强度较低的交战地段。哈尔科夫州库皮扬斯克市周边的战斗仍在持续。然后就是俄军进攻努力的重心所在：正如普雷斯顿所说，俄方仍在试图夺取顿巴斯的剩余部分，战斗从卢甘斯克那样的城市和地区一路延伸到顿涅茨克州剩余的主要城市群。这几个城市我们现在就该点名，因为后面还会不断提到：斯拉维扬斯克、克拉马托尔斯克、第聂伯罗和康斯坦丁诺夫卡——士兵们都俗称它"康斯塔塔"（Konstata），因为名字太长。再往西就是第聂伯罗，这座城市如今实际上已成为该城市群以西乌克兰防御的锚点，其后还依托着另一条城市链。往西南走，就进入第聂伯罗州，然后是扎波罗热州。扎波罗热是个相当活跃的有趣地段，因为俄乌两军在那里同时进攻：俄军试图在第聂伯罗以西向西推进，而在其东北方向，乌军今年大部分时间掌握着主动。由乌克兰空降突击旅组成的一个战术集群一直在那里打一场"蚕食式"攻势，夺取进展的成效相当不错。另一个我们稍后可能会讲到的地区在卢甘斯克附近：乌克兰的第三军团也实施了一场成功的进攻行动，代号为"比瓦尔迪"。所以在那两个地区，乌克兰不只是在争夺主动权，而是实实在在在解放领土，并在相当大程度上抹掉了俄军 2025 年的战果。这就是我先前说这话的意思：战场空间远比人们以为的更有活力，领土的易手频繁得多。事实上，两军战线之间存在一片广袤的灰色地带。乌克兰并不据守连贯的防线，往往只是以少量步兵构成的警戒哨……

[720s - 840s]

That mark positions, but much of the territory is essentially contested, such that in the battle space defined by dense control via drones and a sort of a sizeable engagement zone, which varies from place to place, but you might say roughly it might be 15 kilometers forward and back of the front line. Well, in some areas it's more, in other areas it's less. It depends. What happens is, in reality, that it becomes more of a gray zone, and the specialist is saying Russian forces try to get past this drone engagement zone or defeat zone through infiltration. That's their main challenge, either through individual infantry, through light motorized attacks on motorcycles and ATVs, and they try to essentially get as far back past Ukrainian positions as they can. Whereas Ukraine is largely defending with a combination of prepared defenses, minefields, and a large array of drones and drone units that are effectively engaging Russian forces before they get to their even their own front line, their forward line of troops. And if they get past that point, they get past Ukrainian initial positions, then Ukrainian drone units essentially pick them up there. So it's in many respects kind of a zonal defense. But we should unpack that a bit because people see this as a drone war, and the statistics out there do show that drones inflict 80% of losses. However, it is really a combination of things, and the dynamic is one that involves traditional defense and minefields that make it very hard for mechanized assaults or light motorized assaults to break through. Artillery, which still plays a role in the battlefield, because it forces the enemy forces to disperse. It has a shaping dynamic, even if you don't use it much. The fact that it's present, it makes it hard for troops to concentrate or suppresses them. It slows them down in the assault, making them easy targets for drones. And it's still relevant because drones also have their own limitations. And two periods of of

【译】来标示阵地，但大部分地域实际上处于争夺状态。于是，战场空间就由密集的无人机监视所界定，并伴有一个相当可观的交战区——大小因地而异，但大致可以说位于战线前后各约 15 公里的纵深；有的地方更宽，有的地方更窄，要看具体情况。现实的结果是，它更像一片灰色地带。正如专家们所说，俄军力图通过渗透越过这片无人机交战区、或者说杀伤区，这是他们的核心难题：要么靠单个步兵，要么靠摩托车和全地形车实施的轻型摩托化突击，力求尽可能绕到乌军阵地后方去。而乌克兰方面主要以预设防御工事、雷场，加上庞大的无人机群和无人机分队进行防御，在俄军抵达己方战线——前沿部队线——之前就有效接敌。如果俄军越过了这一线、越过了乌军的初始阵地，乌军无人机分队基本上就会在那里把他们盯上。所以在很多方面，这是一种区域性的带状防御。——不过这一点值得拆开来讲讲，因为大家把这看成一场"无人机战争"，外界的统计也确实显示无人机造成了 80% 的损失。然而它实际上是多种手段的组合：这套动态涉及传统防御和雷场，它们让机械化突击或轻型摩托化突击极难突破；炮兵也仍在战场上占有一席之地，因为它迫使敌军分散。它有一种"塑形"作用，哪怕你并不怎么使用它——它的存在本身就使部队难以集中、或对部队形成压制，令其在突击中放慢速度，从而变成无人机的活靶。炮兵依然重要，还因为无人机自身也有局限。而战争中有两……

[840s - 960s]

The war when they often have the limitations seasonally are spring and fall, begin to the winter because the weather becomes poor. You get high wind, you get rain. Drones don't work nearly as effectively during poor weather, and so artillery is a very important backup. And if you just had drones, you didn't have anything else—no minefields, no artillery. I don't think Ukraine would be able to hold the terrain this effectively with the low density of forces, the men that they currently have deployed. So it's important to describe as a combination of means. Even if drones play such a central role, that folks don't ignore the interaction of of these different capabilities in Ukraine's approach. So sorry to linger on the basics, but sure, can we like lay out so artillery is big guns. What's the role of individual infantry? What's the role of tanks? What is this gray zone? What does it actually look like? How many drones are floating above? What does it look like as Preston was saying for an individual infantry soldier to to run through that? Can you paint a picture of some of the the basic things here? Not good to run through that. It's it's terrifying. There's there's constant eyes in the sky, but to kind of walk this out a little bit, artillery generally speaking, one hundred five or one hundred fifty-five millimeter cannons. That's what we use here in the United States. It's primarily what Ukraine has been using now as they kind of shifted over to a NATO standard. You're talking about twenty-ish kilometer range, which at a time was was big, and there was a phase in this war where that was seemingly to determine the direction that it was going to go is who had more artillery, who could mass fires in certain directions. It's just changed so drastically now to where the drone range has pushed those further and further back. So cannons are on the battlefield. There are some that are dug in, kind of in entrenched positions. Others are mobile on tracks or wheels. They got to get out of there as soon as they fire. There's so many drones overhead watching them. When you get into the drones, then on the battlefield, there's a.

【译】个无人机常受季节性制约的时段，就是春季和秋季，入冬之际也是如此，因为天气转差：起大风、下雨。恶劣天气下无人机的效能会大打折扣，因此炮兵是非常重要的备份手段。如果只有无人机，别的什么都没有——没有雷场、没有炮兵——我不认为乌克兰能以目前部署的这种低兵力密度、这点人手，把地形守得这么有效。所以，重要的是把它描述为多种手段的结合：即便无人机扮演着如此核心的角色，也别忽视这些不同能力在乌克兰战法中的相互作用。——抱歉在基础知识上停留这么久，不过当然，我们是不是可以这样铺开讲：炮兵就是大炮，那单个步兵的作用是什么？坦克的作用是什么？这个灰色地带到底是什么？它实际是什么样子？头顶上飘着多少无人机？就像普雷斯顿说的，一名单兵要从那里穿过去，是什么体验？你能把这些基本图景给我们画个像吗？——从那里跑过去可不好受，那是非常可怕的，天上始终有无数双眼睛。不过还是稍微展开讲讲：一般而言，炮兵指的是 105 毫米或 155 毫米火炮，那是我们美国现在用的，也是乌克兰逐步转向北约标准后主要使用的。射程大约 20 公里上下——这在当年可算是了不得的。这场战争曾有一个阶段，走向似乎就由谁炮更多、谁能向特定方向集中火力来决定。现在情况已经剧变，无人机的作战半径把火炮逼得越退越远。所以战场上还是有火炮的：有的挖好了工事，处于半固守阵地；有的是履带式或轮式的机动火炮，一开火就得赶紧转移，因为头顶上有太多无人机盯着它们。而谈到无人机，战场上则有一……

[960s - 1080s]

Number of them being utilized. Reconnaissance drones is kind of where everything starts, and those are just big fixed-wing drones for the most part that they can stay airborne for quite a while. They're generally low cost, and that's been a dynamic that we've seen with both Russia and Ukraine in this war. Is they've gone from the very few but high cost, maybe larger drones down to a ton of low cost, borderline attritable. They don't want to lose all these drones, but it's not that big of a deal to lose one compared to, say, a U.S. Predator Reaper that costs you know twenty plus million dollars. So they've got these reconnaissance drones up constantly. Both sides have these up when they spot something that is passed along to the appropriate drone unit and is engaged based off of their their what they have available to them. The I would say most common one that we see we see in terms of the media that has the most attention would be the FPV drones. Those are small kind of quadcopters. You see them flying twelve inches, eighteen inches. I think they might get up to twenty inches at times. Generally, there's a a munition attached to the end of that. They crash it into something and it detonates. They can swap that out for anti personnel munitions or go after vehicles or fortifications, and they do that on the spot. So there are mountains of these FPVs. There's a picture of Kim Jong Un actually, North Korea, walking through a hallway of stacked FPVs. So these are the very small, low cost, attritable drones, and they're being used. The numbers are staggering. I'd say for FPVs, it's probably somewhere eight to ten thousand per day, just those, not including other types of tactical drones, and and that would be for one side of the war. So to give you a sense that these are hundreds of thousands of these being employed per month, these strike capabilities exceed all the numbers of personnel and equipment on the battlefield by very substantial. And just to clarify, behind.

【译】大批正在被投入使用。侦察无人机可以说是一切的起点：它们大多是比较大的固定翼无人机，可以长时间留空，而且普遍成本低廉。这其实是俄乌双方在这场战争里共同展现的一个动态：从数量很少但昂贵、个头也许更大的无人机，转向海量、低成本、近乎"可消耗"的无人机。他们当然也不想损失这些无人机，但丢上一架，相比美军那种单价 2000 多万美元的"捕食者""死神"，实在算不上什么大事。所以这类侦察无人机一直在天上挂着，双方皆是如此；一旦发现目标，就转发给相应的无人机分队，由后者根据手头可用之力实施打击。而我要说，我们在媒体上最常见、受关注最多的，是 FPV 无人机：小型四旋翼，常见的尺寸是 12 英寸（约 30 厘米）、18 英寸（约 46 厘米），我想有时能到 20 英寸（约 51 厘米）。一般机头挂一枚弹药，直接撞上目标引爆。弹药还能现场更换：换用杀伤人员的弹药，或转而打击车辆、工事。所以 FPV 多得堆成山。真有这么一张照片：金正恩——朝鲜——走过一条两侧堆满 FPV 的走廊。这些都是极小型、低成本、可消耗的无人机，而且正在被大量使用。数字触目惊心：仅 FPV 一项，我估计每天大约要消耗 8000 到 10000 架——单指这一类，不含其他类型的战术无人机，而且这还只是交战的一方。给你个概念：这等于每月有数十万架被投入战场，其打击能力以巨大幅度超过了战场上全部人员和装备的数量。——还有一点需要澄清……

[1080s - 1200s]

Behind every FPV drone, there's a human being operating it. So there's also from the news or something, there's a sense that autonomy is being utilized. It is not. So this is something you guys talked about. There's usually humans behind the drones. So I'll be frank. Most of this war are systems that are remotely operated, but they are operated by people. It is actually quite manpower intensive. And in fact, it's in many cases, if you look at both Russian Ukrainian military, they had to build out substantial numbers of drone units and channel manpower into the drone component of the fight. It's not even a ratio of one to one, sort of one person to one drone. A typical drone team might require two or three people. You have a pilot, you have a co-pilot navigator. This is exhausting, tiring work. I'm not saying that other traditional missions are easy, but with a drone, you have to be looking at a screen the whole time, all day. You have to be flying, and it's very intensive. Somebody else has to navigate. Another person might be the technician or the person who arms the system. And so almost all systems in this war are remotely operated. Whether they're flying drones, one-way attack strike drones, whether they're UGVs, uncrewed you know ground vehicles, and and they require considerable support. At the end of the day, these are physical objects that require equipping. They require troubleshooting. Like most things in war, doesn't even they don't even necessarily work well or right out out of the box as well. It's what a lot of people don't necessarily appreciate about military affairs that a lot of things don't necessarily work as intended on the battlefield. Somebody's got to be there to fix it. They have to be there to check it. So the bottom line is that is actually quite manpower intensive. There is a degree of semi-autonomy, terminal guidance and targeting at the very last point, or maybe the pilot can hit a box or a vehicle, and then the drone knows that that's what it should go after. These kind of moments and and ways that the drone can overcome enemy electronic warfare jamming with this kind of terminal guidance, but

【译】——每一架 FPV 无人机背后，都有一个人在操控。因为从新闻之类的渠道看，大家有种印象，觉得"自主武器"已经投入使用了。其实并没有。——这就是你们两位谈过的问题：无人机背后通常是人。——坦率地讲，这场战争中的大多数系统属于遥控系统，但它们由人操纵，实际上相当耗费人力。事实上，在很多情况下，你去看俄乌两军，他们不得不组建大量的无人机分队，把人力导入战争的无人机环节。这甚至不是一对一的比例——不是一个人配一架无人机。一个典型的无人机小组可能需要两三个人：一名飞手、一名副手兼领航员。这是非常累人的工作。我不是说其他传统任务就轻松，但飞无人机，你必须整天、全时段盯着屏幕，一直在飞，强度非常大。得有另一个人负责导航，可能还有一名技师，或负责给系统装订弹药的人。所以这场战争里几乎所有的系统都是遥控的——无论是飞行无人机、单向攻击打击无人机，还是 UGV（无人地面车辆）——而且它们都需要相当多的保障。说到底，这些是实实在在的物体，需要配发、需要排障。而且和战争中的大多数东西一样，它们开箱未必就能正常工作。很多不熟悉军事的人未必体会得到：战场上有大量东西并不按预想运转，总得有人在场修、有人在场检查。所以底线是：这件事其实非常耗费人力。确实存在一定程度的半自主——末端制导和最后阶段的目标锁定，比如飞手可以先指定一个箱子或一辆车，之后无人机就知道该追打什么了。正是这类瞬间与手段，让无人机能借这种末端制导克服敌方电子战的干扰，但——

[1200s - 1320s]

Most of these UK use cases are a tiny percentage of the overall fight and the overall number of drones being employed in this war. I think, in some ways, I hate to take away from the autonomy and and artificial intelligence conversation. This war is testing that, and there are plenty areas where I see it's being tested. But to some extent, it's been overhyped in terms of the percentages being the percentage of the fight that's actually being run that way. It is very much a still a human centric war with a lot of manpower involved, running, operating, and maintaining all of these capabilities. So one of the other novel aspects of this war is so much of it is televised, so that you could see videos from the drone perspective on both sides of that drone killing another human being. And one misconception I heard you talk about is that it takes one drone to accomplish that task, and it actually takes a lot more. Can you speak to that in terms of the number of drones it takes to accomplish a particular task, like to destroy a piece of technology or to? I don't know the right way of speaking about this, but to kill another person, yeah, eliminate a target might be a cold way of saying that. We're in an interesting place where there's a lot of propaganda value in footage for anybody using it in any in any military right now in a way that we didn't have access to even just a few years ago. So there's a lot of interest in being able to show. I mean, we we have seen now people will not take reports that something happened unless there is a video of it, which is insane because that you know a few years ago you would never have expected to have video of a missile strike in Crimea on a Russian airfield. Now there's no video; it's maybe it didn't even happen. So the expectation is there, and countries are are meeting that by being able to say, like, look, we penetrated your defenses. Look what we did, or look at all.

【译】这类用例中的绝大多数，在这场战争的整体作战和无人机使用总量中只占极小的比例。从某种意义上说，我并不想给自主性与人工智能这个话题泼冷水——这场战争确实在检验它们，我也看到有不少领域在实测。但就"实际以这种方式运转的作战比例"而言，它被过度炒作了。这依然是一场非常"以人为中心"的战争，有大量人力投入在运转、操作和维护所有这些装备。——这场战争另一个新奇之处，在于它被大量"电视直播"：你能看到双方从无人机视角拍摄的、无人机杀死另一个人类的画面。我听你讲过的一个误区是：以为一架无人机就能完成任务，实际上要多得多。你能就此谈谈吗——完成一项特定任务需要多少架无人机？比如摧毁一件技术装备，或者……我不知道该怎么措辞，比如杀死另一个人？——"消灭一个目标"或许是个冷酷的说法。我们现在处在这样一个有趣的境地：对当下任何使用影像的军队来说，画面都有巨大的宣传价值，这在仅仅几年前是根本无法企及的。所以各方都极有兴趣展示战果。我的意思是，我们已经看到，如今人们若看不到视频，就不肯相信某件事发生过——这很疯狂，因为就在几年前，你根本不会指望能看到导弹打击克里米亚境内俄军机场的视频；而现在要是没有视频，大家就会说"也许压根没发生"。预期已经被抬到这个高度，各国也在迎合这种预期，争相宣称：看，我们穿透了你的防空；看我们干了什么；或者看看我方……

[1320s - 1440s]

The soldiers we killed as they were we were making this approach, so it's out there. There's only a tiny fraction that's made public in the first place, and of those, that's only a tiny fraction of what actually accomplishes the mission. So drones, the batteries run out, they get jammed, and all of a sudden the screen just goes dark. Sometimes they impact and they don't detonate. You know all sorts of little things like that. That footage doesn't make it out. It in turn kind of creates this perception that one $300 drone or one $500 drone is eliminating a tank, and that's not happening. Maybe with the right munition and with a lucky shot, and the tank isn't expecting it. Maybe it's possible. Realistically, we're seeing 10, 20, I think upwards of 30 of these small drones at times being utilized to actually eliminate a tank. So we should be imagining a very large number of FPV drones just hunting humans and tanks and technology. The problem with with understanding what the ratios are is the videos have a strong anchoring effect, and the videos are all videos of successes. There are no videos of misses. So unless you're actually there, you're able to collect the data. You know what percentage of of hits or percentage of drones launched that really is. You might get easily the impression that 100% of the drones launch a hit. As Preston said, that's very far from the case. So you need quite a few drones. For people, even though in videos it looks like a one to one ratio, it's often not. I think it's often a lot closer to maybe five drones or something like that per person. Yeah, in practice, and I've seen. I'll be frank. I've myself seen videos that aren't public. Where in the most extreme case, I saw one on a fuel trip in July where it took 12 on tanks. The most outside example I heard of a well armored, protected Russian tank with electronic warfare. One of these tanks that looks like a large barn, essentially. It's so so heavily protected. Last year was 79. That's an extreme case, but most, as Preston said, is about 30 to 40.

【译】在推进途中所击毙的全部士兵——所以这类影像就在外面流传。首先，公开出来的本来就只是极小一部分；而在这极小一部分里，真正完成任务的又只是其中极小的一部分。无人机会没电、会被干扰，画面会突然一黑；有时撞上了却不引爆，还有各种各样类似的小状况——这些画面根本传不出来。这就反过来制造了一种错觉，好像一架 300 美元或 500 美元的无人机就能干掉一辆坦克。实际并非如此。也许弹药选对了、来个幸运一击、坦克又毫无防备，或许有可能。现实里，我们看到要动用 10 架、20 架，我认为有时甚至 30 架这种小型无人机，才能真正消灭一辆坦克。所以你脑中应该浮现的画面是：极大量的 FPV 无人机，专门猎杀人员、坦克和技术装备。——理解这些比例的难点在于，视频有强烈的"锚定效应"，而所有流传的视频都是成功的视频，没有打偏的视频。除非你人在现场、能收集数据，你才真正知道命中率到底是多少、发射出去的无人机里百分之几命中。否则你很容易得出"发射的无人机百分之百命中"的印象。正如普雷斯顿所说，现实远非如此。所以确实需要相当多的无人机。对人员而言，尽管视频里看起来是一比一，实际往往不是；我认为通常更接近每人约 5 架的样子。——对，实践中——坦率说，我自己看过未公开的视频。最极端的一次，是七月我出行途中看到的一段：打坦克用了 12 架。而我听说过的最离谱的例子，是一辆防护严密、带电子战系统的俄军坦克——就是那种看起来像大谷仓的坦克，防护重到了那种程度——去年用了 79 架。这是极端个案，但正如普雷斯顿所说，多数情况大约是 30 到 40 架。

[1440s - 1560s]

An infantry fighting vehicle, about fifteen, maybe fifteen to twenty, depending. Again, and you have some percentage of drones that are jammed; they'll make it to a target. You have some percentage of drones that may fail to detonate, so it's quite a few. And you know, in in talking to folks who are trying to return mobility and maneuver to the battlefield that specialize in drone enabled mechanized assault, where they have both mechanized units and drones supporting them, a company, the mechanized company that goes on the attack now, expects to take about close to three hundred hits in that assault. They expect vehicles to take quite a few hits, and most to be able to withstand them. So it does take quite a few drones, but the sheer saturation numbers of drones out there is is very difficult to to overstate. So, given that, what does the battle look like? How do you gain territory? Given these drone units, this huge amount of drones, this gray zone, also mentioning artillery, like you mentioned, and tanks, how do they work together in order to be able to do something like gain territory? Well, they're doing it differently now. We're seeing a very different approach from Ukraine than we are from Russia. I can try to speak to the Russian part if you want to maybe take the Ukrainian offensive here in a second. But Russia has defaulted to more of a infiltration type tactic, where rather than large formations that get spotted again ten, twenty, thirty kilometers away from the front and they're then hunted down with long range drones, they don't even have the opportunity to get near the front line, so they stop doing that. We started to see Russia move towards more motorized assaults with just pickup trucks, ATVs, even a lot of motorcycles at a point. Now it's mostly just individual infantrymen. They have checkpoints on a map. They're they're released and they're sent forward five, ten, twenty kilometers, trying to move to a forward location where they can consolidate. And again, the the Ukrainian lines they look.

【译】步兵战车的话，大约 15 架，也许 15 到 20 架，视情况而定。再说，总有一定比例的无人机会被干扰，另一些能打到目标；也有一定比例可能不引爆，所以数量就得相当多。而且，在与那些致力于"把机动性还给战场"、专攻无人机协同机械化突击的人士交谈时——他们既编机械化单位又有无人机配属——如今一个发起进攻的机械化连，预期在一次进攻中要承受接近 300 次命中。他们预计车辆会挨不少打击，而大部分车辆要能扛得住。所以确实需要相当多的无人机，但战场上那种饱和性的无人机数量，怎么高估都不为过。——那么，在这种条件下，战斗是什么样子？怎么夺取地盘？面对这些无人机分队、这么大量的无人机、这片灰色地带，再加上你提到的炮兵和坦克，它们怎么协同才能实现夺取领土这类目标？——现在大家的打法不一样了。乌克兰和俄罗斯采取的思路差异很大。我可以先讲俄方这部分，乌克兰的进攻你稍后再讲。俄罗斯已经退回到一种更偏"渗透"式的战术：与其投入大编队——大编队在前线以外 10 公里、20 公里、30 公里就被发现，然后被远程无人机猎杀，连靠近前线的机会都没有——他们索性不再那么打了。我们先是看到俄罗斯转向更摩托化的突击：只用皮卡、全地形车，有一阵还大量用摩托车；现在则大多只是单个步兵。他们在地图上标好检查点，人员一放出去，就往前送 5 公里、10 公里、20 公里，试图抵达某个可以集结的前方位置。另外要说明，乌克兰的防线在地图上看起来……

[1560s - 1680s]

Clean on a map, they're not. There's a lot of gaps in there because a lot of it is being held with overhead drone coverage to just monitor approaches, and the Russians will get through. And one is a bit of a headache. Three becomes a bigger problem, and all of a sudden they're they're harassing supply lines, and that forces Ukrainian units to move back a little bit, forces Ukrainian drone pilots to move back a little bit, and that opens up the battlefield for more Russian soldiers to come forward just a little. There's a reason that the lines are moving so slowly right now. That infiltration is incredibly costly. We talked with a American volunteer Marine fighting with the First National Guard Corps as of in the Dnipro Peli direction a little over a year ago. He described this as an incredible tactic if you don't care about losing soldiers. Said it's great. You can just try to chew through terrain. You can get there eventually. You're just going to lose ninety ninety five percent of your guys along the way. That's generally what we've seen from Russia over the course of the last at least six months, maybe a full year. Ukraine has been going about it in a different manner, especially with some of their recent counteroffensive stuff. The way I look at it is first that in in 2025, this war really began to be much more of a tug of war over who has superiority in this drone engagement zone, which forces locally call the kill zone, and the side that is able to suppress the other side's drone units through drone strikes, traditional artillery strikes, for Russia also heavy use of glide bombs, standoff glide bombs launched by Russian fixed wing aircraft. That side is then able to push the drone support further away from the front line, and then by displacing it, then be able to actually advance in that way. And so a lot of it's been a tug of war actually over who has superiority in drones and fire support. And increasingly on the Ukrainian side, where we see successful offensive operations, is because.

【译】干净利落，实际并非如此。防线里有大量空隙，因为其中很大一部分是靠头顶的无人机覆盖来监视各条接近路线的，而俄国人总能钻进来。一个俄兵有点头疼，三个就成了大麻烦，转瞬之间他们就开始袭扰补给线，这就迫使乌军部队稍稍后撤、迫使乌军无人机飞手稍稍后撤，从而给更多俄军士兵往前挪一点点打开了空间。现在战线移动得如此之慢，是有原因的。这种渗透的代价高得惊人。一年多以前，我们采访过一名与乌克兰第一国民卫队军团并肩作战的美国志愿海军陆战队员，当时他在第聂伯—佩利方向。他把这套打法形容为"如果你不在乎损失士兵，那就是绝妙的战术"。他说，这招很棒，你可以硬啃地形，最终总能推进到，只不过一路上会损失你 90%、95% 的人。这大体就是过去至少六个月、也许整整一年里我们所看到的俄罗斯的做法。——乌克兰走的是另一条路，尤其是最近的一些反攻。我的看法是：首先，在 2025 年，这场战争真正开始变成一场"拉锯战"——争夺谁在这片无人机交战区（部队当地称之为"杀伤区"）拥有优势。哪一方能通过无人机打击、传统炮击——对俄罗斯来说还包括重型滑翔炸弹、由俄军固定翼战机投射的防区外滑翔炸弹——压制对方的无人机分队，哪一方就能把对方的无人机支援推得离前线更远；把它挤走之后，己方就能以这种方式推进。所以这很大程度上是一场围绕"谁在无人机与火力支援上占优势"的拉锯。而在乌克兰这边，我们看到越来越多的成功进攻作战，其原因恰恰是——

[1680s - 1800s]

You have a very well thought-through plan to set the conditions for the operation. Begin to suppress Russian drone units. Begin to interdict logistics behind the drone units so they don't get sufficient supplies. Begin to figure out how to potentially channel them by essentially pushing them into certain zones of operations where you can then localize, figure out where they're going to be when you're going to do the assault. Then having a coordinated strike plan for how you're going to suppress them during the assault, and then now you've actually established the conditions for your forces to advance with some mobility and to dramatically reduce your losses because Ukraine cannot afford to fight the way Russia does. It doesn't have the manpower, and it doesn't even if it did have the manpower, it's not a question of numbers; it's a question of culture. Ukraine would not treat its forces in the same way; it wouldn't accept those kind of losses of its own troops. And so, you've seen a couple different approaches on the Ukrainian side probably over the past year, and different assault units experimenting with them. You know, you have, I think, some advanced drone assault units like CODE 9.2 and First Separate Assault Regiment that often work together, who are essentially using a combination of drones and very well-equipped and adapted mechanized formations to try to return maneuver to the battlefield and try to take terrain in a more mobile way, do it faster rather than having a kind of slow-moving, grinding, creeping offensive. And you've seen all the way down to maybe the the opposite side of spectrum, air assault brigades very effectively employing three-man fire teams with drone support, systematically clearing tree line by tree line by tree line, slow and steady, but keeping their casualties low. This would be in the south and Zaporizhia, and on a broad front, still over time over the course of the year, taking back a significant.

【译】你有一份深思熟虑的计划，为行动创造条件：开始压制俄军无人机分队；开始遮断无人机分队后方的后勤，让他们得不到充足补给；再设法对他们进行"导流"——本质上是把他们挤进特定作战区域，这样你就能定位，搞清楚他们会在哪里、你何时发起突击；然后为突击期间如何压制他们制定协同打击计划——到这一步，你才真正为部队创造出了带一定机动性向前推进的条件，并大幅降低己方损失。因为乌克兰打不起俄罗斯那种打法：它没有人力；而且就算有，这也不是数字问题，而是文化问题——乌克兰不会那样对待自己的部队，不会接受那种程度的人员损失。——过去大约一年，乌克兰方面出现了几种不同的打法，不同的突击部队在各自试验。比如，有一些先进的无人机突击部队——像"CODE 9.2"第 475 独立突击团——经常协同作战，本质上是以无人机加装备精良、经改装适配的机械化编队的组合，力图把机动性带回战场，以更机动的方式夺占地盘、更快达成，而不是那种缓慢、磨盘式、蚕食式的推进。而在光谱的另一端，你也看到空降突击旅非常有效地运用三人火力小组加无人机支援，一条林带一条林带地系统清剿——缓慢而扎实，但把伤亡保持在低水平。这发生在南线的扎波罗热方向；在宽大正面上，经过一年时间，他们收复了相当……

[1800s - 1920s]

Significant amount of terrain and a bunch of other methods in between. So I won't say that there's been one method, but different units experimenting with different ways of doing it. And on the Russian side, as Preston said, it's a combination of mechanized, light motorized assault sometimes, but increasingly Russians are using mechanized assault simply to consume the attention of Ukrainian drone units, to distract them, or try to basically get them to focus on the mechanized assault, so that light motorized assaults can get through, can get past their initial positions. All these soldiers on motorcycles and ATVs, and even believe it or not, I've seen Ladas and things of that nature for the infiltration side, like literally soldiers on motorcycles. Yes. Trying to gain position. Yes. So they can then attack the supply chains to force Ukrainian forces to push back. Let me maybe take one minute just to explain why that's that can be a challenge for Ukraine because it may sound to someone listening to this a bit preposterous, right? So let me offer why why that can pose a challenge and sometimes light motorized attacks had proven more successful than traditional mechanized attacks because mechanized attacks are a small number of vehicles. Typically, the leading vehicle has a big trawl to clear mines. You know, Ukrainian drones can easily gang up on it and then start isolating the column. And light motorized attacks is a whole number of soldiers. Could be a hundred motorcycles. Could be more than a hundred motorcycles who are trying to use speed to overcome this sort of engagement zone. The reason why that can be an issue sometimes, even though most of them get taken out, I want to be clear, most of them are not successful. But Ukrainian forward positions are often three-man posts. They're not even really firing position or observation posts. They're essentially pickets with considerable separation between them of several hundred meters, right? So Ukraine might have three of these for a kilometer. That means there's considerable space between them, so you could drive through. Then behind them there might be secondary positions, but they're still pretty.

【译】可观的地盘，中间还有一整套形形色色的其他打法。所以不能说存在某一种统一方法，而是不同部队在试验不同的路子。——而在俄罗斯那边，正如普雷斯顿所说，是机械化与轻型摩托化突击的某种组合，但俄军越来越多地只是把机械化突击当作吸引乌军无人机分队注意力的手段——分散其注意力，或者诱使它们聚焦于机械化突击，好让轻型摩托化突击得以通过、越过对方的初始阵地。——就是那些骑摩托车、开全地形车的士兵，甚至——信不信由你——我还见过拉达这类车被用来干渗透的活。——真的就是士兵骑在摩托车上？——对，试图抢占位置。——对，然后他们就能攻击补给链，迫使乌军后撤。——那我用一分钟解释一下，为什么这对乌克兰可能构成挑战，因为听众听起来可能觉得有点匪夷所思。我来解释一下为什么它会构成挑战。有时候轻型摩托化攻击之所以比传统机械化攻击更见效，是因为机械化攻击车辆数量少，通常领头车还挂着大型扫雷犁，乌克兰无人机可以轻易围攻它，进而开始孤立整支纵队。而轻型摩托化攻击是一整群士兵——可能一百辆摩托车，甚至更多——试图用速度硬闯这片交战区。为什么这有时会成问题？我要说清楚：他们中的大多数都被消灭了，大多数并不成功。但乌克兰的前哨阵地通常只是三人哨位，甚至算不上真正的射击阵地或观察哨，本质上是警戒哨，彼此间隔数百米之大。也就是说，乌克兰一公里可能只有三个这样的哨位，哨位之间存在相当大的空间，你可以直接开车穿过去。哨位后面可能有二线阵地，但总体上仍然相当……

[1920s - 2040s]

Sparse in between. Most of the units involved in the fight are essentially supporting units, different type of fire support, drone support, traditional artillery fires, logistics, what have you. Many Ukrainian units don't really have the manpower to merely launch a counterattack if Russian forces break through. Some do. Some have a quick reaction force ready, but for some, the manpower is necessarily there. So it can be a challenge if units do get through and they begin to accumulate. And what can happen? And this is how infiltration works for Russia, even though it's incredibly costly. You can imagine ten soldiers going forward, and seven of them don't make it, but three do, and they found the path in between positions. So now the people that sent them know that this is the gap in Ukrainian positions, and they can then daisy chain send the next group down this path. Now, somewhere behind Ukrainian lines are not three Russian troops, but maybe it's five, six, seven. Now they're accumulating. Well, behind those positions, if you get three to five kilometers back, now you're reaching the first drone reconnaissance units that are flying Mavics. They are not prepared to receive an enemy assault team. They are flying drones. Okay, so for them, this is already an issue because if Russian assault troops, if only three or four or five of them are operating your depth, and Ukrainian units do try to hunt them down, because if they let any number escape, then they get to the drone units. Then you start losing your drone units, your support personnel. It is a real issue in in the battle space. So that's essentially how infiltration works over time. Well, it's incredibly costly for Russia. It's it's a grim calculus, but it's a combination of that and also trying to fight for superiority in drone employment overall. Who has more drones? Who has more drone units in the fight? Who has more fire support? Who's able to suppress whose drone units to then allow their offensive tactics to have some purchase and success. Quick pause. There's coffee downstairs.

【译】稀疏。参战的大多数部队本质上是保障性部队：各类火力支援、无人机支援、传统炮兵火力、后勤保障等等。许多乌军部队其实没有富余的人力，在俄军突破时立刻发起反冲击——有的有，有的保持着快速反应部队待命，但有的确实人力不在那里。所以一旦对方部队真的钻进来了并开始积聚，这就可能构成真正的挑战。而可能发生什么？这正是俄军渗透的运作方式——尽管代价极其高昂。你可以设想：十名士兵往前送，七个没能过去，但三个过去了，而且找到了阵地之间的通路。于是，派他们出来的人现在知道了：这就是乌军阵地的缺口，接下来就可以"链式"地把下一批人沿这条路送进来。现在，乌军防线后面的就不再是三名俄军士兵，也许是五个、六个、七个，他们开始积聚起来。而在这些阵地后面，往后退个三到五公里，你就开始碰到第一批飞大疆 Mavic 的无人机侦察分队。他们可没准备迎接敌军突击队——他们在飞无人机。好，所以对他们来说，这已经是问题了：一旦只有三四个、五个俄军突击兵在你方纵深活动，而乌军部队确实会尽力猎杀他们——因为只要放走任何一小股，他们就会扑向无人机分队。那样你就要开始损失你的无人机分队、你的保障人员了。这在战场上是真实存在的问题。以上就是渗透随时间推移的运作方式。它对俄罗斯而言代价极其高昂——这是一套残酷的算术，但战局就是这种消耗，再加上围绕"总体无人机运用优势"的争夺：谁的无人机更多？谁投入战场的无人机分队更多？谁的火力支援更多？谁能压制谁的无人机分队，从而让自己的进攻战术有着力点、能取得战果。——我们小停一下，楼下有咖啡。

[2040s - 2160s]

For people just listening to this, this is late at night. We're pushing through. This is amazing. Okay, we're back. I I guess I should also ask, what kind of drones are we talking about? So I I just pulled out the what is this? The Mini Pro DJI drones five. How many drones like this are there? And also, I should mention that some of them have a cable from them, right? Fiber optic cable. And I think Preston, you've talked about throughout throughout all this. At first, you were skeptical when you visited initially, but then you kind of saw the the the wisdom, the effectiveness of the of the fiber optic drones. The fiber optics are are terrifying in a lot of ways, but to answer your question on the Mavics, those are still utilized. All things considered, they're relatively expensive compared to some other systems that Ukraine has, and those are not manufactured in Ukraine. And a lot of what they're using right now, they're they're building themselves. So FPVs are a bit bigger than that, but the term drone is so broad right now. I mean, all the way to describe effectively missiles that Russia is firing and Ukraine is firing into Russia that are the size of cars. So we've got everything from very very big to very small and everything in between. The fiber optic one is is interesting. So one of the major defenses against drones, and we use it in the U.S. military, is is jamming electronic warfare, making it to where these munitions or anything doesn't function in a certain area. And I think it was Russia originally that did it. Mike, and correct me here, but they certainly rolled it out relatively quickly, and they attached a very thin fiber optic cable that spooled out of the drone back to the operator. And what that did then was it it brought that signal internal, and the the jammers stopped. Working, so all of the electronic warfare in the world isn't doing anything. That drone flies directly through it. Now, the limitations are that there's a cable behind this drone that, as as thin as it is, it does have some weight to it, which means.

【译】——给只听音频的朋友们说明一下：现在已是深夜，我们还在硬撑，这太精彩了。——好，我们回来了。我想我还该问一句：我们说的是哪种无人机？我刚掏出来的是……这是什么？Mini Pro，大疆的无人机，第五代？这样的无人机有多少？另外我得提一句，有些无人机后面拖着一根线缆，对吧？光纤。我想普雷斯顿，你在节目里讲过这整件事：起初你去实地参观时还是怀疑的，后来才看到光纤无人机的智慧与效力。——光纤无人机在很多方面都令人胆寒。不过先回答你关于 Mavic 的问题：它们仍在使用。但综合考虑，与乌克兰自有的其他系统相比，它们算贵的，而且不是乌克兰制造的——他们现在用的很多装备是自己造的。FPV 比这个稍大一点，但"无人机"这个词如今涵盖太广了：一直到大得像汽车一样的导弹——俄罗斯发射的那种、乌克兰打进俄罗斯的那种，实际上就是那么大。所以我们面对的是从极大到极小、以及中间所有尺度。光纤无人机很有意思。对抗无人机的主要手段之一——我们美军也在用——是干扰，即电子战，让弹药或任何装备在特定区域内失灵。我记得最早是俄罗斯人搞出来的——迈克，我说错了请纠正——他们确实相当快就把这东西铺开了：给无人机接上一根极细的光纤，光纤从无人机上放出线轴、一直连回操作员。这样做等于把信号"内置"了，干扰器随之失效——全世界的电子战都奈何不了它，无人机直接从干扰区穿过去。而它的局限在于无人机后面拖着一根线缆——线再细也有分量，这就意味着……

[2160s - 2280s]

Is a smaller payload or a smaller distance or a shorter distance that the drone can fly? Those can get hung up at times, but we saw Russia roll that out. Ukraine quickly matched it, and that is a serious threat right now. At longer and longer distances, I think we've seen them upwards of around 50 kilometers at this point. The first time I heard this, I thought it didn't it didn't compute in my head. So you just said 50 kilometers, yeah. So that's a very long cable. It's a drone connected by a cable. It didn't make any sense to me. Like that, that that that could be a reality. Maybe this is a good place to kind of ask, like the nature of war, which is figuring out different technologies that are actually effective on the ground. Like figuring out what's effective, even if it seems crazy. Yeah, I mean, as Preston said, this Ukraine has initially experimented with them, didn't adopt them, but the Russians started adopting them pretty heavily after employing them in Kursk when Ukraine had the Kursk offensive. That's when Russian units really started first using fiber optic cables. They saw them as being effective. I'm I'm demonstrating this as a case, as an example of a case at least. And once they saw that was quite effective, impossible to jam, right? Because you literally have a cable attached to the drone. The drone lays behind itself as it flies. Think of it like a very thin fiber optic cable filament, like on a fishing rod, the reel that you would use. About that width, too. It's very thin. It's very thin, and it's a spool like a reel on a fishing rod. That's that's literally what the thing is on. It's a spool, and it's just this cable's coming behind. You have to fly it carefully. You have to fly it slowly. You can't fly it like your the drone you had here, this this Mini Pro. You have to be more cautious because you have to think how the cable's being laid on trees, on roads, on other things. You have to remember that you have a cable, not a radio signal, connecting yourself to it. But nonetheless, it's quite effective. And then it spread from the units using in Kursk, because other units saw it as a successful practice. Once people see something that's effective, they say, "I want to try that."

【译】载荷会更小，或者无人机能飞的距离会更短。线缆有时还会缠挂住。但我们看到俄罗斯把这东西铺开后，乌克兰很快就跟上了，而它如今是实实在在的重大威胁，飞行距离越来越远——我记得目前已经看到接近 50 公里上下了。——我第一次听到这个的时候，脑子完全转不过来。你刚说 50 公里？——对。——那可是一根非常长的线。一架靠线缆连接的无人机。我当初觉得完全说不通，这怎么会是现实。——也许这是个好地方，可以问问战争的本质：本质之一就是去发现那些在实战中真正有效的技术——哪怕它看起来很疯狂。——对。正如普雷斯顿所说，乌克兰其实最初试验过光纤无人机，但没有采用；而俄罗斯人是在库尔斯克——乌克兰发起库尔斯克攻势期间——大量使用之后，才真正开始推广的。那正是俄军部队最早真正用上光纤线缆的时候，他们看到了它的效果。我拿它当个案例来讲，至少是个例子。一旦他们看到它相当有效、无法干扰——因为无人机后面真的拖着一根实体线缆——线缆随无人机飞行一路铺在身后。想象一根非常细的光纤丝，就像钓鱼竿上用的绕线轮，宽度也差不多那么细——非常细。它就是一个线轴，跟钓鱼竿的绕线轮一样，这就是那东西的实际形态：一个线轴，线缆就拖在后面。飞它必须小心，必须飞得慢。你不能像飞你手里那台 Mini Pro 那样飞它。你得更加谨慎，因为你得琢磨线缆会铺在树上、路上还是别的东西上；你必须记住，你和无人机之间连接的是一根线缆，而不是无线电信号。但它确实非常有效。后来它从在库尔斯克使用的部队扩散开去，因为其他部队看到这是一套成功的实践——人们一旦看到什么东西有效，就会说"我也想试试"。

[2280s - 2400s]

Then spreads to other units. Then, as Russians reason against Ukrainian units, Ukrainian units begin adopting it as well, not at the same rate. Fiber optic cable drones are still a fairly small percentage of the overall tactical strike drones used, and I'm not sure—I hate to say—I'm making this number up, but it's 15% or less, I think, overall. That said, they play a very important role because you can't jam them, and also they're much more difficult to detect. Ukrainian units, everyone commonly has systems of detection force drones that are flown via an analog radio signal because it's open and it's easy to intercept. So you often have a device, for example, it's nicknamed the Chuika, one type of it. I've always had one in the vehicle when driving. There's three antennas coming out of it, which receive signal on three different frequency spectrums. And if there's a drone coming towards you, the way things work is, once a drone that's using an analog radio signal is closer to you than to the operator, the signal strength can be stronger to you, so you can start to receive the signal from it, and you have like a little screen that you can see the video feed of an enemy drone coming. And with a fiber optic cable drone, you have nothing in terms of warning because you can't receive that signal. But the the way I look at is that there's been about a three to four month cycle of evolution of technology and tactics in this war between Russian and Ukrainian forces, and they iterate fairly quickly. So both sides have enough defense industrial capacity, enough capital, enough folks working on this that if one side is using something and it's demonstrated to be effective, they assume and anticipate that the other side is going to begin to imitate them and adopt those technology capabilities. And usually, the interaction between the two—and I'm very broad-brushing this—so when Ukraine has often led in terms of tactical development.

【译】接着就扩散到其他部队。再往后，俄军拿它对付乌军部队，乌军部队也开始采用，只是节奏没跟上。光纤无人机在战术打击无人机总量中仍占相当小的比例——我不确定，我不想随口报数，但我认为总体在 15% 或以下。话虽如此，它们扮演着非常重要的角色，因为你无法干扰它们，而且它们也难侦测得多。乌军部队人人普遍装备针对模拟无线电信号无人机的侦测系统，因为模拟信号是开放的、易于截收。所以你常常会有一种设备，比如绰号叫"楚伊卡"（Chuika）的，其中一种。我开车时车里总放着一台。它伸出三根天线，在三个不同频段上接收信号。如果有一架无人机朝你飞来，原理是这样的：一旦一架使用模拟无线电信号的无人机离你比离它的操作员更近，你收到的信号强度就可能更强，于是你就能开始接收它的信号，你面前有个小屏幕，能看到敌方无人机传来的实时画面。而面对光纤无人机，你在预警方面一无所有，因为你根本收不到那个信号。——但我看待这件事的方式是：这场战争中俄乌两军之间技术与战术的演进周期大约是三到四个月，双方迭代得相当快。两边都有足够的国防工业产能、足够的资金、足够多的人在捣鼓这些，所以一旦某一方用上了什么东西并被证明有效，另一方就会假定并预期对手即将模仿、采用这些技术能力。而通常，双方之间的互动——我这里说得很粗线条——是乌克兰往往在战术发展……

[2400s - 2520s]

And bottom-up innovation, because of the way Ukrainian units all experiment on their own, and all have—they're kind of all defense startups. They all have kind of their own R&D section. They have their own defense shop that's or company that's building drones for them. So technology tends to, you know, bloom. Or like I remember when EW was really exploding in twenty twenty three, twenty twenty four, and folks were calling it like an electronic warfare jungle because all these different systems were appearing. And so Ukraine tended to innovate faster. Russian military was slower, but Russia had, in some ways, more capacity and tended to be better at scaling a solution once they identified it. And this was Ukraine's frustration that once the Russian military identified something as effective, and they saw that Ukraine is doing something well, they would then allocate capital, industrial capacity, scale, deploy it across the force in a standardized way, a bit faster. If I can add something to this, Lex, because it's an aspect of this that we just kind of and Mike did it here, and I do it all the time too, is you just rattle it off like it's normal, and it's so far from normal. Yeah. The Chuika drone detector. What that means is, it looks like an old Game Boy. It has a screen on it, so you're driving down the road in Ukraine, watching the screen. It turns on, and it is the enemy's view of the drone. Is it your vehicle, or is it a different vehicle? And you will watch. You can watch as the enemy drone comes in at you. For soldiers in tree lines, they might be watching and trying to decide: Does that drone see us? Do we need to move? It doesn't see us. It's looking the other direction. It's literally watching the feed of the Russian soldiers that are hunting you. That is just about everybody along the front has that right now. It's just a weird dynamic that brand new to war. It beeps, and when it picks up the signal, and so when you're holding it, the it's not a pleasant feeling for everybody else in the vehicle that you.

【译】和自下而上的创新方面领先，因为乌克兰部队都各自做试验，他们有点像一堆"国防初创公司"：各有自己的研发部门，有自己的国防作坊或公司给他们造无人机。所以技术容易"开花"。比如我记得电子战在 2023、2024 年大爆发的时候，大家把它称作"电子战丛林"，因为各种系统层出不穷。所以乌克兰倾向于创新更快；俄军较慢，但俄罗斯在某些方面产能更强，而且一旦认定某个方案有效，更擅长把它规模化。这正是乌克兰的痛点：俄军一旦认定某样东西有效、看到乌克兰某方面做得好，就会调拨资金和工业产能，规模化、标准化地推广到全军，速度甚至还更快。——莱克斯，我能补充一点吗？因为这里有个我们俩都容易一带而过的问题——迈克刚才就是这么讲的，我自己也总这样——你顺口讲出来，好像这一切稀松平常，而它其实离"平常"差得太远了。——对。——"楚伊卡"无人机探测器。它意味着什么？它长得像一台老式 Game Boy，带一块屏幕。所以你在乌克兰开车时，是一边开一边盯着那块屏幕。它一亮，显示的就是敌人那架无人机的视角：它看的是你这辆车，还是别的车？你能眼睁睁看着敌机朝你扑过来。躲在林带里的士兵，可能正盯着屏幕盘算：那架无人机看到我们了吗？要不要转移？——它没看到我们，它正朝别的方向看。你等于是实时看着那些要猎杀你的俄军士兵眼中的画面。前线几乎人手一台这样的东西。这是一种战争史上全新的诡异动态。它会"哔哔"响，一拾到信号就响；所以你拿着它的时候……对车上其他人来说，那不是种愉快的……

[2520s - 2640s]

Can see it's beeping. It's picking up the signal on the drone, and I have had times where folks see me looking at the screen. It's a small screen. Looking at the screen of the enemy drone, and then looking, you know, like to your right, kind of looking your surroundings to see is this, you know, is this like close to me as a kind of looking in the same area or not. So it is, it is exactly as Preston describes it. So there's a surreal aspect to this. It feels almost like video game like with real life, life and death consequences. The soldiers on both sides are looking at screens, and then the whole world is looking at the screens that the soldiers filming the thing. It just feels like the nature of war is completely different. Can you speak to the degree this war represents modern warfare and the future of modern warfare, and to what degree does it represents leftover from old way of fighting wars? I think we're at a transition point right now. This war represents Russia and Ukraine. It is not representative of what every war in the world would look like should it spark today, tomorrow, or next year. There's just very unique aspects to this. The lack of air superiority on Russia's part plays a major role in how the battlefield looks today. That may or may not be the situation that the U.S. military or NATO per se would be in in another conflict. Maybe it would, but there's a lot of dynamics at play here. So it's we we haven't seen this in our lifetime, where we have, you know, one of the one of the aspects of warfare is when people are fighting for their life day in and day out, they tend to come up with really good ways to stay alive, and alternatively, really good ways to kill the enemy. Ways that you don't come up with when you're just between wars and thinking about what might be a good solution. So we are seeing rapid innovation in both Russia and Ukraine, and these are modern economies with modern technology. We've never we haven't seen this in in my lifetime at least. You know the the.

【译】感觉。你能看见它在响，它在接收无人机的信号。我有过这样的经历：别人看见我盯着屏幕——那是块小屏幕——先看敌方无人机的画面，然后往右边瞟，环顾四周，判断：这画面里的地方离我近不近？它看的是不是和我所在的是同一片区域？——所以，它和普雷斯顿描述的一模一样。——这有一种超现实感。它几乎像电子游戏，只不过带来的是现实中的生死后果。两军的士兵都在盯屏幕，而全世界又在看士兵们拍下的这些屏幕画面。感觉战争的性质已经完全不同了。——能否谈谈：这场战争在多大程度上代表着现代战争与未来战争，又在多大程度上是旧式战争打法的残留？——我认为我们正处在一个过渡点上。这场战争代表的是俄罗斯与乌克兰，它并不代表今天、明天或明年世界上爆发的每场战争都会是这幅样子。它有非常独特的层面：俄方未能掌握制空权，这对今天战场的样貌影响巨大。美军或北约在另一场冲突中未必处于同样的处境——也许会，也许不会，这里的变量太多。我们此生从未见过这种局面。战争的特点之一就是：当人们日复一日为活命而战时，他们会想出非常好的活命办法，同时也会想出非常好的杀敌办法——这些办法是你坐在两次战争之间空想"什么方案好"时想不出来的。所以我们看到俄乌双方都在快速创新，而这是拥有现代技术的现代经济体。至少在我此生……

[2640s - 2760s]

Taliban were innovating, Al Qaeda was innovating, ISIS they were innovating. The enemies that we fought were innovating. They didn't have this scale to do it. They didn't have this technologies to do it. So we, I think we jumped decades in terms of what warfare will be in a very short period of time because of where it's playing out. As a thought experiment, how would the United States military fight this differently? Say there was no nuclear weapons on the table. You said something air superiority. What does that mean? So air superiority is the ability to conduct operations in an airspace pretty pretty much at will. So very similar to Iraq and Afghanistan as we were fighting there, we had air power overhead at all times. They were really at no risk of being shot down or even suppressed to the point of not being able to carry out operations. What you're seeing in Ukraine right now is air denial. Neither side is flying aircraft into enemy territory, and that's been the case for a long time. The U.S. military is very built, very much built around air power and delivering long-range precision munitions and a lot of munitions from B-1 bombers, B-2 bombers, things like that, onto onto enemy positions. But by the way, small tangent: which branch of the military is responsible for drones? I would love to know. Like you were at West Point, is there like set of courses now on drones on drone warfare? So is that part of the Air Force? Is that part of air power? Does that count as air power? Is that not? We're figuring it out. I think is the best way to put it. And it's this is the thing that it's it's happening so fast, right? They haven't had the opportunity to create new courses and and put cadets through this for three or four years at a point because there's new technology right now that wasn't necessarily being utilized just 18 months ago. So the the Pentagon is working on this. We've seen a lot of kind of press releases and positive statements about the direction we're headed. There's a drone dominance program right now that the military is spearheading, trying to identify small manufacturers that can be agile and come.

【译】从未见过。你知道，塔利班在创新，基地组织在创新，ISIS 也在创新。我们打过的敌人一直在创新，只是他们没有这样的规模，也没有这样的技术。所以我认为，正因为战争在这片土地上展开，战争的形态在极短时间内向前跳跃了几十年。——做个思想实验：美军会怎么把这场战争打得不一样？假设核武器不在考虑之列。你刚提到了制空权，那是什么意思？——制空权指能够基本随心所欲地在一片空域实施行动的能力。很像我们在伊拉克和阿富汗作战时的情形：头顶上始终有空中力量，几乎不存在被击落、或被压制到无法执行任务的风险。而你现在在乌克兰看到的是"空中拒止"：双方都长期不派飞机进入敌方领土。美军的建制很大程度上就是围绕空中力量构建的——用 B-1 轰炸机、B-2 轰炸机等向敌方阵地投送远程精确弹药和大量弹药。——顺便岔开一句：军队哪个军种管无人机？我特别想知道。你在西点待过，现在有没有成套的无人机、无人机战争课程？它算空军的吗？算空中力量吗？算还是不算？——我们还在摸索，我想这是最贴切的说法。事情变化太快了：他们还没来得及开设新课程、让学员完整学上三四年，因为现在出现的新技术，就在 18 个月前还不一定投入使用。所以五角大楼正在抓这件事，我们看到了不少新闻稿和积极表态，说明方向是对的。军方眼下正在主推一个"无人机优势"计划，试图找出那些灵活、能够……

[2760s - 2880s]

With new solutions to field to the broader force, we're trying to catch up in a lot of ways. In terms of who owns it, I don't know that that answer is there yet. It seems like everybody's got a little piece of it, but that's part of it. We're trying to work our way through it, right? But the U.S. military is embracing this this new small drone. Like to what degree is this an intermediate stage of having these kind of ad hoc drones? Is this an intermediate stage of something else that's coming, or is this going to be the new way of fighting these kinds of wars? That's a good question. There's debate whether an analogy is maybe like aviation in World War One, where we see the Russia-Ukraine war as a transitional war. We press and described it. It starts off as a actually fairly traditional 20th century war with two forces that are principally artillery armies that are both successor armies to to the Soviet military, which was an artillery army, and much is decided in the wars with their interaction artillery, armor, anti-tank guided missiles, and a lot of modernized Soviet equipment. If you were around in 70s and 1980s, you would have recognized most of the things on the battlefield in 2022 through 2024. In fact, most of the equipment NATO sent was also what NATO had made all the way from 60s to you know the the 90s. Um, and then drones evolve quite rapidly and really start to mature over the course of this war. And the question is, are we close to this final stage of maturity, or will we see a substantial reduction of how much manpower is involved? Would drones become much more autonomous to the benefit of both a different set of technology and artificial intelligence being used for navigation, guidance, what have you? Um, and how relevant is that to the wars we plan to fight, and also the way we want to fight? Every military has its own conception of its contingency.

【译】拿出新方案、推广到全军的中小制造商。在很多方面我们是在追赶。至于归谁管，我认为答案还没有揭晓，看起来人人手里都攥着一小块，而这本身就是过程的一部分——我们正在摸索出路。——但美军正在拥抱这种新型小型无人机。这算不算一个中间阶段——这种临时拼凑的无人机？是别的什么东西到来之前的过渡阶段，还是说这就是今后打这类战争的新方式？——这个问题很好。现在有争论：一个类比也许是第一次世界大战中的航空。我们认为俄乌战争是一场过渡性战争——正如有人已经描述过的那样。它开局其实是一场相当传统的 20 世纪战争：两支本质上以炮兵立军的军队，都是苏联军队的后继者，而苏军本身就是一支炮兵军队。战争的大部分内容是由炮兵、装甲、反坦克导弹的交互，加上大量现代化改型的苏制装备决定的。如果你经历过 70 年代、80 年代，你能认出 2022 到 2024 年战场上出现的大部分东西。事实上，北约援助的大部分装备，也是北约从 60 年代一直用到 90 年代的那些。随后无人机迅速演进，并真正在这场战争中走向成熟。问题是：我们离最终成熟阶段还有多远？我们是否会看到人力投入的大幅下降？无人机会不会借助另一套技术、借助用于导航与制导等环节的人工智能而变得自主得多？而这一切，对我们计划打的战争、对我们偏好的打法又有多大关联？每支军队都有自己对"应急想定"……

[2880s - 3000s]

The scenarios that it has in mind, and also an idea of how it wants to fight. And here is a challenge because first, how do we know when we're looking at a war that what we see is generalizable to other wars? Every war has its own context, right? So, this is a principally land war with an air and a sea component. Second, there's always an issue of when you're looking at prolonged conventional war, what part of the war do you want to draw lessons from? Because most wars of this type, they start out with this dynamic, intense phase, the clash of forces, reconciliation of military plans, but then they often do become positional, attritional in nature, right? Forces dig in. There's way more troops on the battlefield. Everything's mined. It's very hard to break through. And drones really came into their own more in these latter phases, right? So sometimes people make the mistake of they look at the latter phase, the late parts of of a war, and think, okay, this is how the next war will start. But the next opening phase of a war is often different than kind of the middle phase where both sides enter this dynamic that they're trying to break out of. I think for the United States, part of the challenge is that we actually are tactically an incredibly skilled force, right? We spend a lot of money on our military, on the training for our military, and the quality of our personnel. I think Preston's absolutely right. We spend a tremendous amount of funding on the expeditionary air component of our force because it's the best combination of mobility, firepower, on the battlefield, which then enables the rest of the force to conduct combined arms maneuver. And what I mean by that is, if you think of the different combat arms, right, infantry, armor, artillery, if you think of aviation in combination with that, that force works together, right? All those pieces are interacting with each other, supporting each other dynamically on the battlefield. And one of the big advantages of our forces, not just technology, not just training of personnel, but the fact that we can run a complex.

【译】的构想——即它脑中预演的种种情景，以及一套自己想怎么打的思路。这里的挑战在于：首先，当我们看一场战争时，怎么知道所见能否推广到其他战争？每场战争都有自身的语境。这场战争本质上是一场陆战，附带空与海的成分。其次，观察一场长期常规战争时，你到底想从哪个阶段汲取教训？因为这类战争大多以一个动态、激烈的阶段开局——两军相撞、各自战争计划的相互调适——但随后往往转为阵地性、消耗性的形态：部队掘壕固守，战场上兵力剧增，遍地雷场，极难突破。而无人机恰恰更多是在这些后期阶段大放异彩的。所以人们有时会犯这样的错误：看到一场战争的后期、尾声阶段，就想"下一场战争开头也会是这样"。但下一场战争的开局阶段，往往不同于双方陷入并试图挣脱的那种中段僵局。——我认为对美国来说，部分挑战在于：我们在战术上确实是一支极其精锐的部队。我们在军队、训练和人员素质上投入巨大。普雷斯顿说得完全对：我们把巨额经费投在部队的远征空中组成部分上，因为它集机动与火力之大成，进而让其余部队能够实施联合兵种机动。我的意思是：想想各个战斗兵种——步兵、装甲兵、炮兵——再把航空兵与之结合起来，这支力量是协同运作的：所有这些板块在战场上动态地互动、相互支援。而我们军队的一大优势——不只是技术、不只是人员训练——恰恰在于我们能够驾驭复杂的……

[3000s - 3120s]

Operation have these pieces working together, at least ideally, and be able to achieve a much greater effect from the fact that these pieces are interacting; they're much better coordinated. And lastly, one of the biggest separators of modern militaries is whether or not you can do the things you're doing in exercise or in a small scenario against a permissive environment. Like, let's say you're fighting the Taliban. I'm not saying that wasn't a challenge, but nonetheless, it's quite different having a fight like that than facing the largest conventional war Europe has seen since World War II. Right? The ability to scale operations, complex operations, is a big limiter for other militaries. In fact, the Russian military really struggled with this extensively at the beginning of this war. To maybe make an analogy to folks not military affairs, it's one thing to run a business with you know 10 employees. It's another thing to run a corporation with 10 or 20,000. Right? The complexity scales, and I don't know anything about running corporations, but I imagine there's a great deal more complexity to it than something like a small business, even though in principle you're you're still running a business operation. So I use this kind of analogy to explain that this is, in some ways, how the United States is very much advantage. But if if you're interested, there are some open-ended questions based on what we saw in this war. If we need to adapt, if we need to change the way we operate, there's a degree of uncertainty. If our traditional advantages will offer the same benefits in a future war, if that war features this technology in the battlefield, one thing we do know is that our opponents, like Russia, do plan to fight in this way. We already have a sense of how they plan to fight because we see how they're fighting in this war, right? As you know, William Gibson used to say, "The future is already here. It's now evenly distributed, right? " So we already see that this you know this is already here on the battlefield in Europe. An absurdly large question, but who is winning this war right now? And maybe a better version of that question is what is a good measure.

【译】作战行动，让这些板块协同运转（至少在理想状态下），并因它们彼此互动、协同顺畅而达成大得多的效果。最后，区分现代军队高下的最大分野之一，是你能否把演习中、小规模想定中、在宽松环境里做到的事情搬到实战。比如跟塔利班作战——我不是说那不算挑战——但那种仗，和面对欧洲自二战以来最大规模的常规战争，是完全不同的两回事。把复杂作战行动"规模化"的能力，是其他军队的一大限制因素。事实上，俄军在战争初期就为此吃尽了苦头。给不熟悉军事的人打个比方：经营一家 10 名员工的公司是一回事，经营一家一两万人的大公司是另一回事。复杂度是会翻倍的；我对经营公司一窍不通，但可以想见它比小企业复杂得多——尽管原则上你经营的仍是同一门生意。我用这个类比想说明：在某些方面，这正是美国的巨大优势所在。——不过如果你感兴趣，基于这场战争的所见，还有一些开放性的问题：我们是否需要调整、需要改变运作方式？如果未来的战争以这种技术为战场特征，我们那些传统优势是否还能带来同样的红利？这存在不确定性。但有一点我们确知：像俄罗斯这样的对手，确实计划以这种方式作战。我们已经大致知道他们打算怎么打，因为我们正在这场战争中看着他们怎么打。正如威廉·吉布森说过的："未来已在这里，只是分布不均。"所以我们已经看到：这一切已经在欧洲的战场上发生了。——一个荒诞级别的大问题：现在谁在赢？或者这个问题的更好版本是：怎么给这场战争计分才算好的衡量标准……

[3120s - 3240s]

Of how to score the war, in terms of the military balance of power and effectiveness. So, territory gain, casualties, quality of each army, maybe other measures. Who's winning this war right now? I'll tackle it. Honestly, Lex, it's a it's a question I generally avoid. I understand where the question comes from. People want to have a very clear and concise: where do things stand right now? But the challenge is, this isn't like a football game where both teams you're looking at the scoreboard, who has more points at the end of 60 minutes, and that's the winner. You can say whoever has more points at 55 minutes, they're that much closer. Because Russia and Ukraine in this case are fighting for different things. They have different objectives. You could overly simplify it and say that Russia is trying to take Ukraine, and that Ukraine is trying to maintain their sovereignty. But then you just go down a rabbit hole of what is success, or what is what is the fact that Ukraine still exists today to a degree is winning. But they have lost some territory that they don't appear on the verge of getting back. That is not winning. So it's difficult. The other thing I would say with that is when you look at historical conflicts, say World War II, Nazi Germany was winning that war for the majority of it until they weren't, and then they quickly lost. Imperial Japan was winning much of World War II in the Pacific. They were unstoppable. If you looked at, if you took a snapshot in time, and then all of a sudden things turned, they were losing and they lost. So the winning, losing in the middle of a conflict, I feel like you could paint that honestly something like this in whichever direction you would prefer. Is it fair to say that either side can collapse quickly, so ties can turn dramatically? Historically, we have seen that. I don't know that there's a lot of evidence right now. I think there was a lot of expectations early on that Ukraine would be the the country to collapse, and there's been countless analysts just you know they're on the verge. This winter is going to be it. This summer is going to be it.

【译】——就军事力量对比与作战效能而言。比如夺占领土、伤亡、两军素质，或许还有别的指标。现在谁在赢？——我来接这题。老实说，莱克斯，这个问题我一向回避。我理解问题的来路：人们想要一个非常清晰简洁的答案——现在局势到底如何？但难就难在，这不是橄榄球赛：盯住记分牌，60 分钟结束时谁分多谁赢，你还可以说 55 分钟时分多的那队离胜利更近。而俄罗斯与乌克兰在这场战争里追求的东西不同，目标不同。你可以过度简化地说：俄罗斯想拿下乌克兰，乌克兰想保住主权。但接下来你就掉进"什么算成功"的兔子洞：某种意义上，乌克兰今天仍然存在就是赢；可它丢掉了一些看起来短期内收不回的领土，这又不算赢。所以很难。另一点是，回看历史上的冲突，比如二战：纳粹德国在大部分时间里都在赢——直到赢不了，然后迅速败亡。日本帝国在太平洋战争的大部分时间里也在赢，势不可挡；你若在某个时点拍张快照，会得出那样的结论，然后局势突变，他们开始输，最终输掉了。所以冲突中段的"输赢"，我觉得你几乎可以按自己偏好的任意方向去描绘。——那这样说是否公平：任何一方都可能迅速崩溃，所以战局可能急转直下？——历史上我们见过这种事。但我不认为眼下有很多证据。战争早期确实有大量预期认为乌克兰会是崩溃的一方，此后无数分析师宣称"就这个冬天了""就这个夏天了"……

[3240s - 3360s]

And and and they're still holding. There's no indications that either one is on the verge of collapse, but some sort of large change to dynamically shift the conflict. You know, the the the Prigozhin Wagner march on Moscow. There was the the possibility there. Maybe that would have been the thing that would have brought about not necessarily a Russian collapse, but a Russian inability to continue to project power into Ukraine. Black Swan event is probably a little too much, but it's that type of thing that could precipitate a a major change in the direction the war is going. Yeah, I don't see either side right now being able to impose the kind of collapse I think you're thinking of, and and part of the reason is just the way they're fighting and the interaction between the two forces isn't really conducive to producing that. It's very hard to generate the level momentum and exploit battlefield conditions to really to really kind of force a collapse on a side. Although, as I often say, you know, we have to be mindful wars unfold in phases, right? So there are these periods where one side has the initiative. Russia held the initiative for a long period of time. Now, in 2026, we see actually Ukraine increasingly has the initiative on parts of the front, as imposing real challenges on the Russian armed forces. You could say that for a time, Ukraine has been advantaged this year on the battlefield. The debate we have as analysts is: is that going to be a lasting advantage? Is that a cyclical trend, or is that a window of opportunity for Ukraine? Because just because you're advantaged, like Russia might have been in 24 and 25, it doesn't mean that that will hold. That will last. You you see that the way one side fights can reach diminishing returns and become cost ineffective. That's what happened to Russia in 2025. You see much of it is an interaction between the two forces, adaptation, new technology comes along. Both sides are experimenting with different ways to fight, and so this is you know I think for neither of us is this not a cop out. I think as analysts, there is always a tendency to admire the problem.

【译】——可他们至今仍在坚守。没有任何迹象表明哪一方濒临崩溃，但某种重大变故可能让冲突急剧转向。比如普里戈任率瓦格纳进军莫斯科那件事，当时确实存在那种可能性——也许那本可成为导火索，带来的未必是俄罗斯的整体崩溃，而是俄罗斯丧失继续向乌克兰投送力量的能力。"黑天鹅事件"这说法也许有点过，但正是那类事件，可能让战争的方向发生剧变。——对，我看不出眼下任何一方能把对方逼到你说的那种崩溃，部分原因就在于双方的打法以及两军的互动方式，并不容易催生这种结果。要攒足那种势头、并利用战场条件硬生生压垮一方，非常困难。不过，正如我常说的，我们必须意识到战争是分阶段展开的。会有某一方长期掌握主动权的时期：俄罗斯曾长期握有主动权；而现在到了 2026 年，我们看到乌克兰在前线多个地段日益掌握主动，给俄军造成了实实在在的难题。可以说，今年在战场上乌克兰一度占优。我们作为分析师争论的是：这会是持久的优势吗？是周期性趋势，还是乌克兰的机会窗口？因为占优——就像俄罗斯在 2024、2025 年可能的那样——并不意味着它能保持、能延续。你会看到一方的打法会走到收益递减、变得不划算：2025 年的俄罗斯就是这样。很大程度上这是两军互动、相互适应、新技术出现的过程，双方都在试验不同的打法。对我们俩来说，这并不是在回避问题；作为分析师，我们这个行业总有一种"欣赏问题本身"的倾向……

[3360s - 3480s]

But if you say somebody's winning, it often sets for people and certainly any audience expectations. Most people think if you say someone's winning, that side is going to win.
