But Ukraine also faces a very, very significant challenge from the Russian strike campaign. None of us are sure how this winter is going to go. We're not. We're not sure how cold the winter is going to be. That's a big factor too. And other factors are at play. What other wars will happen? The U.S. war with Iran had a material and political impact on this war. Right. So we don't know what other things will take place around the world that may shape this war, because there are all sorts of countries that are de facto material parties to the war or very significantly engaged in supporting the countries fighting this war. So it's just a great deal of factors to consider. When you analyze the war, maybe you can speak to the levels of analysis that are most important to you that we should be thinking about. So I've spoken to a bunch of historians, like James Holland, who analyzes, for example, World War II, the Western Front, the Eastern Front, and focused on the Western Front from all the different levels. You know, tactical, strategic, operational. There's, of course, the political, geopolitical. There's, you know, the supply chain, all this kind of down to geography and weather, like you mentioned, winter. So what what are some of the levels of analysis that are important here in this particular war? I mean, I think for military analysts, you know, there's at least three levels, and then there's the central political level that's also essential to understand. But the way I look at first, tactical level of analysis, right? That's the nitty gritty battlefield details. What's happening on the ground in these specific fights? How are the two sides fighting at this tactical level? How are forces employed? We covered quite a bit of that in the conversation already. How are how those infantry advance? How are drones being?

【译】但如果你说某一方在赢，往往就会给人们、给任何受众设定预期。多数人会以为，你说谁在赢，谁最终就会赢。而在常规战争中，往前看清哪怕几个月都非常困难，因为变数太多，你只能说：乌克兰眼下在地面占优。但乌克兰同样面临俄军打击行动带来的极其严峻的挑战。这个冬天会怎样，我们谁都不确定。我们不确定这个冬天会有多冷——这也是个大变量。还有别的因素在起作用：世界上还会发生哪些战争？美国与伊朗的那场战争，就对这场战争产生了物质与政治层面的影响。对。所以我们不知道世界上还会发生什么、会怎样塑造这场战争，因为有各种国家实际上是这场战争的事实参战方，或者深度介入、支撑着交战国。所以要考虑的因素实在太多了。——分析这场战争时，也许你可以谈谈对你而言最重要、也值得我们思考的分析层次。我采访过不少历史学家，比如詹姆斯·霍兰德，他研究二战、西线、东线，并从各个层次剖析西线：战术层、战略层、作战层，当然还有政治、地缘政治层面，还有供应链等等，一直到地理和天气——比如你刚提到的冬天。那么就这场战争而言，哪些分析层次是重要的？——对军事分析人士而言，我认为至少有三个层次，另外还有一个核心的政治层面，对理解战争同样必不可少。我看待它的方式是：首先是战术层面的分析——那些最具体的战场细节：这些具体战斗中地面上发生了什么？双方在战术层面怎么打？部队如何运用？这些我们刚才已经谈了不少：步兵怎么推进？无人机是怎么……

[3480s - 3600s]

Employed, you know, correlation of forces and day-to-day battlefield changes, they inform your understanding of how the war is being fought, and also they help set your expectations for what's likely to happen in the months ahead. They're also relevant because even though the war is not going to be decided by who advances the next five or ten kilometers, that this tactical level war very much informs the strategic. Why it sets expectations among military and political leaders for who has greater bargaining power, who has leverage, who is succeeding on the battlefield, provides information to the negotiations between the two sides as well. Right. So, to some extent, we can't overly fixate on the map, but the map is important, and you can see who is adapting, who is not adapting, and actually is increasingly suffering greater casualties and battlefield reversals. Which, in this case, twenty twenty six is Russia. Now, let's take the operational level. And first, I'll say this is a thorny subject in military circles because I know plenty of people, military side, that don't think there's a discrete operational level of war. They think that there's operational art, but there isn't such thing as a separate operational level of war. We should not get into that discussion here. I see you saying that some people listening to this are very angry hearing you even say operational, like it's this discrete layer. I would not say they were angry, but I can imagine a host of army majors getting ready to debate this as I bring the topic up. So let's just stop it and say, look, there are different ways of looking at operational war, but there is such a thing as operations and the conduct of operations, right? Which is important, and this is much more the organization of intelligence, logistics, fire support, the coordination of different units in a coherent action with a clear objective, right? And ideally, the operations then link what you're trying to achieve in terms of the objectives of the operations with the political goals of the actual war. Like ideally, the military strategy takes the operations and connects them to the.

【译】……如何运用。要知道，力量对比和战场上的每日变化，能帮助你理解这场战争是怎么打的，也有助于你形成对未来几个月可能发生什么的预期。这些之所以重要，还因为：尽管这场战争不会由谁多推进下一个五公里或十公里来决定，但这个战术层面的较量在很大程度上影响着战略层面——它决定了军队领导人和政治领导人心中的预期：谁的谈判筹码更多、谁占据主动、谁在战场上占上风——也为双方之间的谈判提供信息。所以，在某种程度上，我们不能过度盯着地图，但地图确实重要，你可以从中看出谁在适应、谁没有适应、谁实际上正蒙受越来越大的伤亡和战场挫败——在这个案例里，即 2026 年，这就是俄罗斯。好，接下来谈作战层次。首先我要说，这在军界是个有争议的话题，因为我认识不少军方人士认为并不存在一个独立的"作战层次"：他们认为存在的是战役法（operational art），但不存在一个单独的战争作战层次。我们不该在这里展开那场讨论。我看你这是在说，有些听众光是听到你把"作战"讲成这么一个独立层次就已经非常愤怒了。我不会说他们愤怒，但我能想象，我一提起这个话题，就有一大群陆军少校摩拳擦掌准备辩论了。那我们就此打住，这么说吧：看待作战问题可以有不同方式，但"战役行动"及其组织实施是确实存在的，对吧？这一点很重要，它更多是指情报、后勤、火力支援的组织，是不同部队在一个有明确目标的连贯行动中的协同。理想状态下，战役行动把你试图达成的战役目标，与这场战争的实际政治目标连接起来。理想情况下，军事战略把这些战役行动接过来，把它们连接到……

[3600s - 3720s]

Political goals of the war, so you're not just conducting successful military operations, but they have a clear purpose in terms of the political objectives you're trying to achieve. And operations are things that are phased; they are planned, they involve coordinating a whole host of units, but they have a clear objective behind them. They have a sequencing, you know, they thought through how they're going to set the conditions, how the breaching force is going on, who's the second echelon. If you meet with success, who's going to exploit that success? If you don't meet with success, what are the different alternative courses of actions you're going to take? And then ultimately, how are you going to consolidate? Right. So it has a lot of moving pieces. This is what staffs usually plan, and often they're looking at bigger size formations. If we were talking earlier about infantrymen, squads, platoons, companies, this is more at the size of armies, you know, or corps. And then above that, we have military strategy. It's at its core level, it's a theory of success, right? There's some coherence to the logic of what you're doing, why you're doing it. There are choices being made. It reveals itself often best actually in what you're choosing not to do rather than what you're choosing to do. That's often the most interesting part for me as an analyst. There's some sequencing involved. You've thought through the sequencing of actions, and ideally, it links the actual operations you're doing and planning with the political goals of the war that you receive from your political leaders, right? You know, Preston, you want to add a bit more to that, but military strategy to me is always one of the more interesting topics, at least intellectually. It's, and and it's often kind of debated, you know, what to what to what extent, what is the military strategy, how you understand the military strategy, but this level is important too. So I always wonder at the at every layer, but the strategy layer, how much does strategy look like? I apologize, but Pinky and the Brain, there's that cartoon where you know what are we going to do today? Is we're going to take over the world? You kind of assume everything goes flawlessly, and it's kind of this dramatic picture of success.

【译】……战争的政治目标上——也就是说，你不只是在成功地实施军事行动，这些行动还要在你试图达成的政治目标上具有明确的意义。战役行动是分阶段的，是经过计划的，涉及协调一大堆部队，但背后都有明确的目标。它们有先后顺序：要考虑清楚怎么创造条件、破障突破兵力怎么上、谁是第二梯队；如果行动顺利，谁来扩张战果；如果不顺利，有哪些不同的备选行动方案；最后，如何巩固战果。所以说这里面有很多环节。这通常是参谋机关负责计划的东西，而且他们看的往往是更大规模的建制——如果我们前面说的是步兵、班、排、连，那这一层就是集团军乃至军一级的尺度。再往上，就是军事战略。就其核心而言，它是一套制胜理论，对吧？你做什么、为什么做，其中的逻辑要有某种连贯性。这里面有各种选择。而它往往最清楚地体现为你选择"不做什么"，而不是你选择做什么——这对我来说，作为分析师，常常是最有意思的部分。战略里也包含时序安排：你把行动的先后顺序想清楚了，而理想状态下，它把你正在做和正在计划的战役行动，与从政治领导人那里接到的战争政治目标连接起来。普雷斯顿，你想不想就此再补充一点？不过军事战略对我来说一直是最有意思的话题之一，至少在智识上如此。这个层面也常常引起争论：到底什么是军事战略、如何理解军事战略、到什么程度。所以我每一层都会琢磨，但在战略这一层：战略在多大程度上像——不好意思——像《Pinky and the Brain》（那部动画片）？就是"我们今天干什么？""我们去征服世界！"那种：你默认一切都完美进行，是一幅戏剧性的成功图景……

[3720s - 3840s]

Versus how much planning is there for all the different contingencies, levels of including complete failure? It's like how much of the different trajectories are considered. Strategy and theory first. It's a plan that you're not going to change quickly or often because otherwise it's not much of a strategy. But any good strategy must have core planning assumptions, and if those assumptions are proven wrong, you should expect to adjust. Actually, a lot of times strategy goes poorly is when people are trying to execute a strategy even though their assumptions are clearly wrong. I can think of some wars we've been in that looked a bit like that. Second, the strategy is iterative, so it's interacting with the strategy of your opponent, right? And so ideally, you're iterating. You're looking at what your opponent's strategy is, how your strategy is performing against what they're trying to do, and then you're changing it over time, right? But unlike tactics, which change quickly and have to keep pace with technology, and tactics informs strategy. If strategy is divorced from tactics, then it's a great staff planning exercise. It may be very well written, but the force can't execute that strategy, right? And alternatively, if you just have tactics, you're just fighting battles, then you don't have a campaign. Okay, you have maybe good ways of fighting a war, but you haven't thought through how you're going to win it. You don't have a campaign thought through. It's not obvious how the battles you're fighting are going to aggregate to military success. Maybe we could take, given everything you laid out now, as a case study, the invasion of 2022, February 24th. What were the failures and what were the effective decisions on the Russian side and the Ukrainian side in the early days in the early months of the war? Maybe Mikey could also talk about what you predicted and what turned out to be a correct prediction and what turned out to be not a correct prediction. Yeah, absolutely. So first, I think amongst community of military analysts and folks who are looking.

【译】……与这一点相对的，是对各种不同意外情况、各种局面——包括彻底失败——做了多少计划？也就是考虑到了多少种不同的轨迹。先说战略和战略理论。战略是一份你不会很快或频繁更改的计划，否则就算不上战略。但任何好的战略都必须有核心规划假设，如果这些假设被证明错了，就应当预期要调整。实际上，很多时候战略搞砸，就是因为在假设明显已经错了的情况下，人们还在硬着头皮执行。我可以想到我们打过的几场战争就有点像那样。第二，战略是迭代式的，它在与对手的战略互动。理想状态下，你在不断迭代：观察对手的战略是什么、你的战略在对抗对方的图谋时表现如何，然后随时间调整。但与战术不同——战术变化快、必须跟上技术，而且战术反哺战略——如果战略脱离了战术，那它就只是一场漂亮的参谋作业，也许写得非常好，但部队根本执行不了。反过来，如果你只有战术、只是在打一场场战斗，那你就没有战局（campaign）：你可能有一套不错的作战方式，却没想清楚怎么赢下这场战争，没有把整个战局想透——你现在打的这些战斗如何累积成军事上的成功，并不明了。既然你把这些都讲清楚了，也许我们可以拿 2022 年 2 月 24 日的入侵作为案例研究：开战初期、最初几个月，俄方和乌方各自的失败决策与有效决策是什么？也许迈基还可以谈谈你当时的预测：哪些被证明是对的，哪些被证明是错的。——当然可以。首先，我认为在军事分析界、在关注这场战争的人当中……

[3840s - 3960s]

In 2021, I was definitely one of the people that thought Russia was preparing a full-scale military invasion of Ukraine. And even though we weren't certain, and I wasn't certain of exactly how they would do it or what it would look like, so there were definitely gaps in information. It's rare where you have perfect knowledge of of the adversary's plans. Although the U.S. intelligence community had great knowledge of Russia's plans, and we're releasing that information throughout the Russian military buildup, it was a very difficult time because first, many folks did not want to believe that what they were seeing on the ground—the Russian military buildup and the rhetoric leading up to it—was truly built up to a full-scale invasion. In fact, I learned as an analyst that even if all the pieces kind of look right, people naturally want to believe it's anything other than that. This is a very inconvenient truth, and there were a host of countries and allies the United States had that had a different interpretation of the evidence, like Germany and others in Europe. And to be frank, Ukrainian political leadership also had real misgivings about the likelihood of a full-scale invasion. People thought if there was going to be a breakout of conflict again, that it would just be narrowly focused on the Donbas region, and that much of the Russian buildup was meant to coerce Ukraine. It was meant to be a part of course of diplomacy or to distract Ukrainian forces. So there were competing interpretations. I want to be clear about this, and and people had different contingencies in mind. Some thought it might be just another Russian attempt to seize the Donbas. Some thought it might be something larger, trying to capture parts of Eastern Ukraine. And some did think it was going to be a Russian attempt to take most of the country. So I think I was amongst the folks that predicted the invasion early on, Russian intentions, and and a scope of the operation, but not the way they were going to do it, because there, I think we over-rationalized.

【译】2021 年，我确实是认为俄罗斯正在准备对乌克兰发动全面军事入侵的人之一。尽管我们并不确定，我也不确定他们具体会怎么做、会是什么样子，所以信息上明显存在空白。你对敌方计划的了解很少能做到完美无缺。虽然美国情报界对俄罗斯的计划掌握得非常清楚，并且在整个俄军集结期间不断公开这些信息，那仍是一段非常艰难的时期。因为首先，很多人不愿意相信他们在地面上看到的东西——俄军的集结以及随之而来的战争叫嚣——真的是在为一场全面入侵做准备。事实上，我作为分析师学到的一点是：即使所有拼图看起来都对得上，人们也天生愿意相信它是别的任何东西。这是一个非常令人不愿面对的真相。美国当时有一批国家和盟友对证据持不同解读，比如德国和欧洲其他国家。坦率地说，乌克兰政治领导层对全面入侵的可能性也确实心存疑虑。人们认为，如果冲突再次爆发，也只会局限于顿巴斯地区；俄罗斯的集结在很大程度上是为了胁迫乌克兰，是外交博弈的一部分，或者是为了牵制乌军兵力。所以当时存在相互竞争的解读——我要把这点说清楚——人们心里有不同的预案。有人认为这可能只是俄罗斯又一次夺取顿巴斯的尝试；有人认为规模可能更大，是想占领乌克兰东部部分地区；也确实有人认为这将是一次俄罗斯夺取该国大部分地区的行动。所以我属于较早预测到入侵、预测到俄罗斯意图和行动规模的那批人，但没有预测到他们动手的方式，因为在那方面，我认为我们过度理性化了……

[3960s - 4080s]

The way Russian military would actually conduct the operation, not seeing how the military strategy and the plan, the concept of operations, would be tied to Russian political assumptions. And the Russian political assumptions were that Russian intelligence had set the conditions in Ukraine for a speedy takeover. The Russian troops would be greeted as liberators. They could conduct essentially a coup de main, that they could very rapidly advance into Ukraine, encircle the capital, either capture or isolate the Ukrainian political leadership, and that even though there would be an initial phase of combat operations, it would be brief, and that they could get Ukrainian forces to to a large extent surrender or isolate them. These are the Russian assumptions. I'm I'm being clear about that, and that then they could quickly move to a stabilization occupation phase. And all that stemmed from the fact that at the end of the day, Vladimir Putin did not see Ukraine as a real independent country with a separate national identity, and really had not learned or updated his views at all from the real beginning of the war, which was 2014. Right, and and we know this because he actually wrote many of them in a July article in 2021. If we go back to that, folks who are interested, it was published, and he laid out a whole series of views of how he saw Ukraine, how he saw the sort of ongoing frozen conflict between Russia and Ukraine, and his assumptions were fairly were fairly clear. I don't think we did a good enough job of tying those assumptions to thinking through how Russia might actually do this kind of this kind of operation. And secondarily, the toughest thing to predict the interaction between the two forces. Here, it's not a case of overestimating the Russian military, just underestimating the Ukrainian military. Although I think there's a fair argument to be said that we overestimated how Russian military would perform in this case, we underestimated how the Ukrainian military would perform in the initial opening of the war.

【译】……即俄军实际实施这次行动的方式：没有看出军事战略和计划——也就是作战构想——会与俄罗斯的政治假设绑得有多紧。而俄罗斯的政治假设是：俄罗斯情报机构已经在乌克兰铺好了条件，可以实现速战速决的接管；俄军部队会被当作解放者夹道欢迎；他们基本上可以来一场"闪电夺权"（coup de main），可以极迅速地突入乌克兰、包围首都，抓捕或孤立乌克兰政治领导层；虽然会有一段初期的战斗阶段，但会很短暂，而且他们能让乌军大部分投降或被孤立。这些就是俄方的假设，我在此明确地说——然后他们就能迅速转入稳固占领阶段。而这一切的根源在于：归根结底，弗拉基米尔·普京并不把乌克兰视为一个真正独立、拥有单独民族认同的国家，而且从这场战争真正的起点——2014 年——以来，他根本没有吸取教训或更新过自己的看法。我们之所以知道这些，是因为他在 2021 年 7 月的一篇文章里亲笔写下了其中很多观点。有兴趣的听众可以回头去看，那篇文章是公开发表的，他系统阐述了一整套他如何看待乌克兰、如何看待俄乌之间这种持续中的冻结冲突的观点，他的假设写得相当清楚。我认为我们没有做好的一项功课，是把这些假设与"俄罗斯实际会如何实施这种行动"的推演绑在一起。其次，最难预测的是两军之间的互动。这里的问题不在于高估了俄军，而只是低估了乌军——尽管可以说，我们确实高估了俄军在这场战争中的表现，但我们对乌军在开战阶段表现的低估更甚。

[4080s - 4200s]

In part because we knew far less about the Ukrainian military. I'm going to be very frank about that. Most expertise is usually against adversary forces. We don't have nearly as much expertise amongst allies for allies and partners. There's just the nature of the field and the profession we work in. And secondarily, I think actually more was known about Russian plans to attack than Ukrainian plans to defend. So the degree of anchoring bias that information has when you do analysis, but also much was contingent. So Ukrainian forces perform not only did they perform much better, but Ukrainian volunteers show up to fight in those early moments. But it's a very chaotic early period of the war: the initial clash and reconciliation of the Russian invasion and the Ukrainian defense. I think one challenge that analysts have and folks who look at analysis is in understanding that military power by itself cannot be abstracted. I often see it abstracted in conversations: the sense that the United States is number one, and China is number two, and Russia is maybe number three or four or five, wherever we want to put it. But there's no such thing. Military power requires a context to express itself. Right. So if you're thinking of a potential, let's say, battle between the United States and China over Taiwan, you have one scenario in mind. Okay. If you're thinking of maybe a war between China and India, you have a different scenario in mind. So this the subtraction isn't helpful. It actually really matters what the scenario is because the geography matters, the political objectives matter. They will drive the military strategy and the concept of operations, and that will then heavily decide how the forces will be employed, which is the most important thing here. And so sometimes what happens is you may know something about a military, or you may even know quite a bit about both militaries, but knowing something about militaries is not the same as knowing how the two of them will interact in a particular fight, and that's subject to a lot of individual choices, political choices, military choices, and also a great.

【译】部分原因是我们对乌军的了解要少得多。这一点我要非常坦率地承认。大多数专业经验通常都对着敌军；对盟友和伙伴，我们掌握的专业知识要少得多。这就是我们这个行业和这一职业的天性。其次，我认为关于俄军进攻计划的已知信息，实际上多于关于乌军防御计划的已知信息——这就是这类信息在你做分析时带来的锚定偏差的程度。但也有很多东西是偶然的。乌军不仅表现得远为出色，乌克兰的志愿者也在最初时刻就现身参战。但那是战争中一段极其混乱的早期：俄军入侵与乌军防御的初次碰撞与相互磨合。我认为分析师和分析观察者面临的一个挑战，是理解军事力量本身无法被抽象化。我经常看到谈话里把它抽象化：美国第一、中国第二、俄罗斯大概第三第四第五，随便往哪儿放。但这种排名并不存在。军事力量需要一个表达自身的情境。如果你设想的是美国与中国之间围绕台湾的潜在冲突，你脑子里是一种想定；如果你设想的是中国与印度之间的战争，你脑子里就是另一种想定。所以这种排名并不管用。想定是什么其实至关重要，因为地理重要，政治目标重要，它们会驱动军事战略和作战构想，进而很大程度上决定兵力如何运用——而这才是这里最重要的东西。有时候的情况是：你可能对一支军队有所了解，甚至对两支军队都相当了解，但"了解军队"不等于知道它们在某场具体战斗中会如何互动，后者受大量个体选择、政治选择、军事选择左右，还有很大的……

[4200s - 4320s]

Deal of serendipity and chance, things that are contingent. I'll say this about the opening of the war: as poorly thought through the Russian invasion was, and as unworkable as their assumptions were, you know, in many cases the Ukrainian defense itself was improvised, and so that first clash was, in my view, contingent on a whole series of choices and and early battles that went a certain way. And we'll continue to debate this military history for a long time. You know, I think I and other folks who are interested in military history, we still debate what happened in World War II, what happened in World War One, what happened in Napoleonic Wars. The reason I bring this up is for the degree of humility. This war is hardly over. We still have a long ways to go to piece together what happened exactly and why. And often we don't have a great understanding of of the probability of a timeline, because people naturally assume that whatever timeline they're living in, whatever happened, was the likeliest series of events to take place. They rarely ever assume that they're living in in in a fortunate version of of that timeline. So I'll stop there in a sort of discussion of of what we thought and and the initial reconciliation of the Russian plan to invade and Ukrainian plan to defense. I learned a great deal myself professionally, as an analyst. I'll be honest, Washington, particularly from the things that not from the things necessarily they got right, but from things they got wrong. I think you end up learning far more from what you get wrong professionally. What surprised you the most? What surprised me the most first, how the Russians actually conceived of this operation, the fact that they had fairly leverage and fairly brittle force without any real plan B. There's no hedge in their invasion plan. Second, how poorly they performed at the tactical level, just in terms of capability, quality of the force, and it's difficult to.

【译】……偶然与运气成分，很多事情全凭际会。关于战争的开局我想说：俄军的入侵想得有多么不周、他们的假设有多么行不通，但同样，在很多方面乌军的防御本身就是临机拼凑的，所以那第一次碰撞在我看来取决于一连串的选择和一系列恰好那样发展的早期战斗。这段军事史我们会继续争论很久。我和其他对军事史感兴趣的人，到现在还在争论二战到底发生了什么、一战到底发生了什么、拿破仑战争到底发生了什么。我提这个，是为了说明需要保持多大的谦逊。这场战争远未结束。要拼凑出究竟发生了什么、为什么，我们还有很长的路要走。而且我们往往对"时间线的概率"没有很好的理解，因为人们天然会假设自己正身处的那条时间线、已经发生的一切，就是最可能发生的序列；很少有人会假设自己活在一条幸运版本的时间线里。关于我们的判断，以及俄军入侵计划与乌军防御计划的最初磨合，我就说到这里。作为分析师，我自己从这段经历中学到了很多。老实说，华盛顿也一样——尤其是从做错的事情里学到的，而不是从做对的事情里；我认为在职业上，你从错误中学到的远多于从正确中学到的。——最让你惊讶的是什么？——最让我惊讶的：第一，是俄国人实际上如何构设这场行动——他们手里是一支相当有限、相当脆弱的部队，没有真正的 B 计划，入侵计划里没有任何对冲。第二，是他们在战术层面表现得有多差，就能力和部队素质而言；而这很难……

[4320s - 4440s]

Judge seeing a military going through a series of military forms modernization, how they might perform in a large scale conventional war if you haven't seen a large scale conventional war. But I think there was definitely intervening period where we overrated them, which is a tendency when looking at adversaries because we tend to worst case them. I'll be very honest; it's often a professional assumption that it's better to worst case and assume that adversaries can do the things they they would like to do and the things they talk about doing, and maybe get that wrong. In which case, your own forces have a good day; they perform much better than the opposite version. Right, strategic surprise, Pearl Harbor, where you underestimate your opponent, and the result of you underestimating your opponent is is disastrous. Right, yeah, and I guess there's a bunch of things when you speak into the reality of war just went wrong. Like there's the columns of tanks going towards Kiev that weren't effective. There's when you take a military force and it connects with the reality of the geography, the situation, then you actually get to see which were the effective tactical decisions, and that was the illustration of that. Well, ultimately, you have the interaction between the two forces and the decisions that both sides make, and I think that definitely the initial prediction that Russia would succeed in this conventional phase of the war because it looked like you know they had superiority in deployed forces. Ukrainian military was fairly late to mobilize, and much of the Ukrainian military assumptions seemed to be that the focus of the Russian invasion would come in the Donbas, and that's where a lot of units were deployed. Many units were not there available to either defend in the south to even check the Russian advance from Crimea, or in the north. In fact, there were almost no regular military units north of Kiev to stop the advance from Belarus. So it was difficultness to assess the interaction of those plans. I I look back on that, and I think I learned a tremendous amount, so did so did other analysts, and it's it's.

【译】……很难评判：看着一支军队经历了一系列"军事形态现代化"，如果你从未见过大规模常规战争，就很难判断它在这样一场战争中会有什么表现。但我认为确实有那么一段中间时期我们高估了他们，这是研究对手时的通病，因为我们倾向于按最坏情况估他们。我很坦白地说，把对手按最坏情况估计、假定对手能做到他们想做的事和他们宣称要做的事，往往是一种职业惯例，宁可在这方面搞错——那样的话，自己的部队落得个轻松日子，表现远好于反过来的版本。反过来就是战略突袭、珍珠港：你低估对手，而低估对手的后果是灾难性的。——对。我想，一旦把军事力量放进战争的现实中，就有一堆事情出了问题。比如那些开向基辅的坦克纵队毫无成效。当一支军事力量与地理和态势的现实相接触时，你才真正看得出哪些是有效的战术决策——那就是一个例证。归根结底，这是两军之间的互动和双方做出的决策。而我认为，最初"俄罗斯会赢下这场战争常规阶段"的预测肯定是有依据的，因为看起来他们在已部署兵力上占优势。乌军动员相当晚，而且乌军的很多假设似乎都认为俄军入侵的重点会放在顿巴斯，大量部队也就部署在那里。许多部队不在相应位置上，既无法在南边防御、连迟滞俄军从克里米亚方向的推进都做不到，北边也一样。事实上，基辅以北几乎没有正规部队去阻挡从白俄罗斯方向而来的推进。所以很难评估那两套计划之间的互动。我回头看那段经历，认为我学到了极多东西，其他分析师也一样，这是……

[4440s - 4560s]

It's a case that I think we're going to continue to study, but this was just the very initial period of the war. What's interesting is, I think military now has got much better in correcting expectations, and then predictions for follow-on offensives and counter-offensives became much more accurate once we saw the two forces in the fight. Preston, what surprised you most about the beginning of the war as you look back now, especially as that war has evolved? Well, there's something right out the gate, Lex, that I think is still there today, and it shows this difference between, we'll call it, the Soviet military mindset and the Western mindset, and we hadn't really seen this in play in my experience. Again, fighting the global war on terror, it's not you weren't running up against a conventional force that was operating in any way similar to how we were. We push push authority down and allow junior leaders to make decisions in the moment. The whole idea is you have a a commander's intent. This is what we're trying to accomplish and and why the task and purpose and all of that. And down the chain of command, the soldiers, the individuals have the authority to make a decision on the spot to decide I need to go left, or I need to dig in, or I need to call for reinforcements, or I need to retrograde. That is pushed down to the lowest possible level to where we've got you know 18, 19 year old kids making these incredible decisions in combat. We should say for a little bit of context that you served in Afghanistan, yes, and maybe you can connect some of the experiences you remember from that. What things you've seen in terms of examples of how Western force operates? Well, that was very dispersed. We were were spread across a lot of small bases in Afghanistan. So you had captains and below, sometimes 24, 25 year olds making daily life and death decisions for a lot of people, and we would push that down to 20 year old staff sergeants or or sergeants that were making a decision that could cost the lives of their men. So you're saying like a 19 year old, 20 year old, if they have a good idea, if they have a good decision.

【译】……这是一个我们将会持续研究的案例，但这还只是战争最初期。有意思的是，我认为军界此后在修正预期方面做得好多了，一旦我们看到两支军队真正交手，对后续进攻和反攻的预测就变得准确得多。——普雷斯顿，如今回头再看，尤其是这场战争已经演变到现在，开战时最让你惊讶的是什么？——有一件事从第一刻起就存在，莱克斯，我认为到今天依然存在：它展示了所谓苏联式军事思维与西方思维的差别，而以我的经历，我们此前并没有真正在实战中见识过这种对比。再说了，打全球反恐战争时，你面对的并不是一支以任何方式与我们相似的常规力量。我们把权限往下压，允许初级领导者在当下临机决断。整个理念就是"指挥官意图"：我们要达成什么、为什么，任务与目的，等等。沿着指挥链往下，士兵、个人有权限当场决定：我需要向左，或者我需要就地挖壕固守，或者我需要呼叫增援，或者我需要撤退。这种权限被下放到尽可能低的层级，以至于我们有十八九岁的孩子在战斗中做着这些了不起的决策。——我们也许该交代一点背景：你在阿富汗服役过。——是的。——也许你可以讲讲你记得的一些经历，你见过哪些体现西方军队如何运作的例子？——那是高度分散的。我们在阿富汗散布在很多小基地里。所以上尉及以下军官——有时是二十四五岁的年轻人——每天为很多人做生死攸关的决定；我们还会把这种决定权下放给二十岁的上士或中士，他们做的决定可能让自己的人送命。所以你是说，一个十九岁、二十岁的人，如果他们有好想法、有好判断……

[4560s - 4680s]

That has power. Yeah, there's a lot of leadership involved with that because you can't just grab a random guy and say we're going to let you make whatever decision you want. But through the training and through the kind of briefings from the top down, from brigade to battalion to company level commanders to the squad leader, making sure that your people know this is what we're doing, this is the mission, and this is what we're trying to accomplish. So they have a full understanding. So then, when something inevitably goes sideways, they're not stuck like the Russian army was trying to advance on Kiev, where when they got stuck, they stopped. There was a shocking number of senior Russian officers killed in the opening few weeks of the war because they kept having to go closer and closer to the front to essentially move traffic jams because they didn't have the as they went down the ranks. Whereas we have, and and you know, again, this was a Ukrainian victory to be clear. The U.S. and NATO had been doing some work in in training Ukraine for some time, and in some units had kind of instilled this idea of junior leadership and empowering your junior soldiers to where, if they don't have an order, they still know what the mission is. And they're able to make the best decision on the spot. We didn't see that from Russia, and I would argue that we're still seeing that today on the Ukrainian side. There is a crazy amount of innovation. I know we talked about it with drones, but even the tactics on how they're going to advance in certain areas—it's different from one unit to the next. A lot of that is driven from the bottom up, which is a very Western military type thing. Whereas on the Russian side, still to today, is still top-down driven. They are given their orders. They are given their place on a map. Go there. So that was surprising to see for me right out the gate, and I think it's pretty notable. I think as the war, you know, hopefully wraps up before too long here, I think that's going to be a big takeaway: is that that method is preferred of pushing the authority as far down the chain of command as you possibly can. Yeah, absolutely, and but that's both a conversation about.

【译】……那这就有力量了。——对，这里面涉及大量领导力工作，因为你不能随便抓一个人来说"你想怎么决定就怎么决定"。而是要通过训练、通过自上而下的各类简报——从旅到营到连级指挥员再到班长——确保你的人知道：我们在做什么，任务是什么，我们要达成什么，让他们有完整的理解。这样，当某件事不可避免地出岔子时，他们就不会像向基辅推进的俄军那样卡死——他们一卡住，就停在那里不动了。开战头几周里阵亡的俄军高级军官数量惊人，因为他们不得不一次比一次更靠近前线去疏导堵死的车队，因为他们沿军衔往下没有这种（决断）能力。而我们这边——再说一遍，明确讲这是一场乌克兰的胜利——美国和北约在训练乌军上已经做了一段时间的工作，在一些部队里确实灌输进了这种初级领导力、授权初级士兵的理念，以至于他们即使没有接到命令，也知道任务是什么，能够当场做出最好的决定。我们没有从俄军身上看到这一点，而且我认为今天在乌军一方仍然看得到（这种特质）。那边有惊人的创新量。我知道我们谈无人机时说过，就连在某些地段怎么推进的战术，也是一支部队一个样，很多是自下而上驱动的，这是非常西方的军事特质。而俄方直到今天仍是自上而下驱动：命令下来，地图上给你位置，"到那里去"。所以这是我一开始就感到惊讶、而且我认为相当值得注意的一点。我想随着这场战争——希望不久后——落幕，这会是一条重要教训：把权限沿指挥链尽可能向下下放，这种方法是更可取的。——对，完全同意。不过这同时也是一场关于……

[4680s - 4800s]

Leadership in military culture, and I think, in looking in the Russian military before this war, the gaps of knowledge we may have had on how they would fight were filled with what we knew about doctrine, operational concepts, and more should have been done to fill the gaps. Looking at military culture and some of the real deficits and shortcomings in terms of military culture and leadership style that's preferred in the Russian armed forces, and one could easily say that when looking at the Ukrainian military, we could have done a much better job learning more about the Ukrainian forces before the war to get better estimation on how they would fight. But in many cases, talking to Ukrainian colleagues at the end of the day, you find that a lot of these battles, a lot of these interactions, were the result of initiative of units of commanders in those early days, and it was quite chaotic and and the success of volunteers, of troops, and people who essentially mobilized themselves to fight, join the forces, and filled the ranks early on, swelled the ranks in order to to muster that defense. You've written about Prigozhin's mutiny in twenty three. What does that teach us about the structure of the Russian military? And going to perplexity here, the Prigozhin mutiny was a short lived armed rebellion led by the Wagner mercenary chief Yevgeny Prigozhin in Russia on June twenty third and twenty fourth, twenty twenty three. Yeah, well known character from well before this war to people who followed Russia and particularly the role of Wagner and other wars like the like the Syrian civil war and their deployments to to Africa and parts of the Middle East, but Prigozhin rose to prominence.

【译】……军事文化与领导力的对话。我认为，回看战前的俄军，我们在"他们会怎么打"上的知识空白，是用我们对其条令和作战概念的已知认知去填补的，而本应做更多功课去补上这些空白——去看军事文化，去看俄军中占主导的军事文化与领导风格上那些真实的缺陷和短板。同样可以说，回看乌军，我们本可以在战前下更大力气了解乌克兰部队，以便更好地估计他们会怎么打。但很多时候，归根结底，与乌克兰同行交谈你会发现，那些战斗、那些交手中的很多，是早期那些部队、那些指挥员的主动性带来的结果，当时相当混乱；还有志愿者的成功——那些战士、那些基本上自我动员起来参战的人，加入了部队，早早充实了队伍、扩充了编制，才撑起那道防御。——你写过 2023 年的普里戈任兵变。它对我们理解俄军的结构有什么启示？——这里我去查了一下 Perplexity：普里戈任兵变是 2023 年 6 月 23 日至 24 日在俄罗斯发生的一场短命的武装叛乱，由瓦格纳雇佣军首领叶夫根尼·普里戈任领导。——对。早在战争之前，普里戈任对关注俄罗斯的人来说就是个知名人物，尤其是瓦格纳在其他战争中的角色，比如叙利亚内战，以及他们在非洲和中东部分地区的部署，但普里戈任声名鹊起……

[4800s - 4920s]

Certainly, amongst Western audience, from outspokenness and public criticism, and the conflict that he developed with the the Minister of Defense at the time, Sergey Shoigu, and the Chief of General Staff, Valery Gerasimov. Maybe to quickly track at least how I remember the role Wagner was. Wagner comes in early on in 2022. They get paired up with the Russian airborne. They show some success in May of 2022 at a town battle called Popasna. They then are expanded in terms of the role in this war, and Prigozhin is given authority by the Russian government to go recruit from Russian prisons. He starts hiring convicts to build out special convict-staffed assault formations, assault groups within Wagner. So the size of Wagner grows in assault force, and Wagner is thrown then into a fight for a city called Bakhmut, and enters a grinding fight for the city. And I think you could periodize the battle. Technically, maybe starts August or so, and it runs through fall of 2022, winter, all the way till May. We should say that's a that's one of the most important battles in the war. It is one of the most important battles, particularly in the early period of the war. Certainly. Can you actually take a small attention and just what are your memories of that? Can you describe what that that battle was and what it what did it teach you about war? What did it teach you about this this particular war? So the fight around Bakhmut was, in some respects, a siege, a classical siege, because you had both a battle for the city, and you had a decisive battle around the city to try to control the roads, the ground lines of communications into the city. And what was happening to Bakhmut? The reason why at that time period, this was going back to February, March of 2023. The reason why Ukrainian defense of Bakhmut increasingly looked precarious.

【译】……尤其是在西方受众当中，靠的是他的直言不讳和公开批评，以及他与当时的国防部长谢尔盖·绍伊古、总参谋长瓦列里·格拉西莫夫之间发展出的冲突。也许我可以快速梳理一下，至少按我的记忆，瓦格纳当时的角色是这样的。瓦格纳 2022 年初入场，与俄空降兵配对使用。2022 年 5 月，他们在一个叫波帕斯纳的城镇战斗中打出了些成绩。随后他们在战争中的角色被扩大，普里戈任获得俄罗斯政府授权，可以去俄罗斯监狱招募。他开始雇用囚犯，在瓦格纳内部组建专门的囚犯突击编队、突击群。于是瓦格纳的规模和突击力量膨胀起来，接着瓦格纳被投入对一座叫巴赫穆特的城市的争夺，陷入一场消耗战。我认为这场战役可以分期：严格说大约从八月前后开始，贯穿 2022 年秋季、冬季，一直打到（次年）五月。——我们该说一句：这是这场战争中最重要的战役之一。——它确实是最重要的战役之一，尤其是在战争早期。——当然。——你能不能稍稍岔开一笔，讲讲你对它的记忆？你能描述一下那场战役是什么样的吗？它让你对战争本身学到了什么？对这场特定的战争又学到了什么？——围绕巴赫穆特的战斗，在某些方面是一场围城战，一场经典围城：因为你既有城内的城市争夺战，也有城外一场决定性的战斗，争夺的是通往城里的道路、地面交通线。巴赫穆特到底发生了什么？为什么在那个时段——这要回到 2023 年的二三月——乌克兰对巴赫穆特的防御看起来越来越岌岌可危——

[4920s - 5040s]

It wasn't the urban battle for the city. In the city, you had essentially a city that was divided by a small river running through it, and there was a kind of residential eastern side to it, and there's a more urban and industrial side to the city. And let's see. Actually, I want to be clear. This is a nice city. I have a friend who was in the Peace Corps in the city. He knows it much better than I ever did. But Bakhmut is a sort of medium-sized city, but not an unknown one in Ukraine. So you had industrial zones, you had nine-story apartment buildings. You know, those of us who originally were born or grew up in the Soviet Union can remember them well. And you had a kind of eastern residential area on the eastern side of the river, and then you had terrain that was raised above the city. The city itself was kind of on the punch bowl. It was in lower terrain. It was not an easy place to defend because the terrain was higher both in front, east of it, and west of it, right? And so what happens? Russian forces end up occupying the positions east of it, and then you have essentially two battles to take place. One is for Bakhmut, as Russian forces steadily enter Bakhmut from the eastern side, make progress from east to west, and the other one is the siege around the city. Because most sieges, if you want to take a city, you have to interdict, you have to control your enemy's ability to reinforce the city, and you have to take control of the logistics, right? Otherwise, they'll keep reinforcing it, and it's going to be a very long fight and a slog. And so Ukrainian forces were essentially fighting two two battles, and in some ways, the more important one was the battle to prevent encirclement of the city. And at that time, very briefly, where I happened to be there with Ukrainian forces, Russian military had gone around the northern side of the city and was really close to interdicting one of the main roads running down from Chasiv Yar, the Khromove road, and in part because another town fell north of Bakhmut called Soledar. This this smaller city had fallen, and so the flank, essentially the left flank, began to give right in this battle, and on the right flank, so.

【译】——原因并不在城内的巷战。城内的情况，基本上是一座被一条穿城而过的小河一分为二的城市：东侧偏居住区，另一侧则更城市化、更工业化。让我想想。其实我要说明一点：这是一座不错的城市。我有朋友在和平队服役时就在这座城市，他比我了解得多。巴赫穆特算一座中等城市，但在乌克兰并非无名之地。所以那里有工业区，有九层高的公寓楼——我们这些在苏联出生或长大的人对这种楼再熟悉不过。河的东侧是一片东部居住区，而城市外围有地势高于城区的高地。城市本身就坐落在"碗底"，处在低地上。它不是一个便于防守的地方，因为东面和西面的地势都比它高，对吧？于是发生了什么？俄军占据了城东的阵地，然后就打响了实质上的两场战役。一场是巴赫穆特之战：俄军从东侧持续突入城内，自东向西推进；另一场是外围的围困战。因为在大多数围城战里，你要拿下城市，就必须遮断、必须控制敌方增援城市的能力，必须拿下后勤的控制权，对吧？否则对方就会不断增援，那将是一场旷日持久、无比磨人的战斗。所以乌军实际上在同时打两场仗，某种意义上更重要的是防止城市被合围的那一仗。当时——简单说一句——我恰好在现场与乌军在一起，俄军已经绕到城市北侧，非常接近切断从恰西夫亚尔延伸下来的主干道之一，也就是赫罗莫韦公路；部分原因是巴赫穆特以北的另一座城镇索列达尔陷落了。这座小一些的城市失守之后，这一仗里（乌军）的左翼——本质上的左翼——开始让位；而右翼也……

[5040s - 5160s]

Began to give as well as Russian forces entered the eastern part and then the southeastern part, and the city, like many cities that face a battle like this, essentially saw systematic destruction. This was still very much an artillery and infantry and tank fight. There were infantry fighting vehicles, BMP two s directly firing thirty millimeter cannon in the city. There were tanks that came into the city would be direct firing down the street at buildings and positions, and you had close quarters combat between infantry fighting from buildings to buildings. You could picture this as a fairly, in some ways, a traditional urban fight. But on top of that, you also had drones. You had light bomber drones. At this time, FPVs were just being experimented with. In winter of 2023, you didn't really see them at this point of the fight. But the battle was a grinding fight. Wagner was throwing constant assault groups, exhausting the Ukrainian defense with convict infantry, and many of them would get killed. But Ukrainian units would get tired; they'd run out of ammo, and then Wagner would send more experienced troops. And Russian airborne essentially secured the flanks, and they were providing artillery support to them as well. So it wasn't just Wagner fighting, although Prigozhin liked to take all the credit for the fight. In reality, it was Wagner, a large amount of artillery support, because the Russian military had a significant fires advantage, artillery advantage in this fight as well. And this is what was leveling a lot of city, and it also had elements of Russian airborne securing the flanks. That way, Wagner could focus on the fight for the actual city itself. And it was a grinding battle. It was very much a traditional battle that ultimately saw the destruction of the city. By the time Russian forces took it, there was very little of the city left. If you see images of it now, I will say it's from my point of view. Not only is it a tragedy, but this is the fate that many other cities have have suffered in Ukraine. Essentially, in some ways, Russian forces at the end of the battle took nothing because there was very little of.

【译】……也开始让位，因为俄军打进了城东部分，然后是东南部分。这座城和许多经历这种战斗的城市一样，遭受的实质是系统性的毁灭。这在很大程度上仍是一场炮兵、步兵加坦克的战斗。城里有步兵战车——BMP-2 在城里用三十毫米炮直射；有坦克开进城，沿街道对建筑物和阵地直瞄射击；还有步兵在建筑物之间逐楼争夺的近距离战斗。你可以把它想象成一场相当传统的城市战。但在这之上还有无人机：有轻型轰炸（投弹）无人机。当时 FPV 无人机才刚进入试验阶段——2023 年冬天，在这个阶段的战斗中你还见不到它们。但这是一场磨盘般的消耗战。瓦格纳不断投入突击群，用囚犯步兵去耗尽乌军的防御，其中很多人被打死。而乌军部队会疲惫、弹药耗尽，瓦格纳再投入更有经验的部队。俄空降兵基本上负责锁住侧翼，同时也为瓦格纳提供炮兵支援。所以打仗的不只是瓦格纳，虽然普里戈任喜欢把这场仗的功劳全揽到自己身上。现实是：这是瓦格纳加上大量的炮兵支援——因为俄军在这场仗里拥有显著的火力优势、炮兵优势——城里的很大一部分就是被这些夷平的；此外还有俄空降兵的部分兵力锁定侧翼，这样瓦格纳才能专注于城市本身的争夺。这是一场磨人的战役，是一场非常传统的战役，最终以城市的毁灭告终。等到俄军拿下它时，城市所剩无几。如果你现在去看它的影像——我这么说完全是出于我自己的视角——这不仅是悲剧，也是乌克兰许多其他城市已经遭受的命运。某种意义上，俄军在战役结束时几乎一无所获，因为几乎已经没有多少……

[5160s - 5280s]

The city left to take, and you've referred to the Bakhmut syndrome. So Bakhmut syndrome is Bakhmut became a fixing point in the war at a critical period because what was happening the war around this fight is in the fall Ukraine had two remarkably successful offensive operations. One was in Kharkiv, where Russian military literally routed, and the other one was in Kherson, which was much more slow going and plodding, but nonetheless forced the Russian military to withdraw and retreat from the western side of the Dnieper River bank. They were both successful, and they began to set the expectation that actually this could be a shorter war that Ukraine could achieve a military victory in this war. And the Russian military then tried to use Bakhmut as essentially a fixing fight, but employing Wagner, which were kind of the expendables in this battle. Prigozhin may not have seen him as forces that way, but certainly that's the way they were being used, and it became a grinding fight. And as became a grinding grinding fight over time, it started to sap morale. But battles like this take on political significance. I personally think that the loss of Bakhmut wasn't all that important or meaningful. In fact, I don't just think that; I know that because it took the Russian military a whole year to get from Bakhmut to Chasiv Yar, the next town over, on higher terrain, still fighting for it, and it took them even another year to take a large part of Chasiv Yar. Okay, and if you look at where they are from Bakhmut, distance wise, the map it's not far. So one can safely now argue, I think, the evidence is in incontrovertibly that the loss of Bakhmut wasn't nearly wasn't nearly that critical at the end of the day. Critical for the broader strategy of the war. So why why over fixate on Bakhmut? Well, the challenge is that battles become politically significant even if tactically they might not.

【译】……城市剩下的东西可拿了。你提到过"巴赫穆特综合征"。所谓巴赫穆特综合征就是：巴赫穆特在关键时期成了这场战争的牵制点，因为围绕这场战斗的战争态势是：头年秋天，乌克兰打出了两场极为成功的进攻战役。一场在哈尔科夫，俄军在那里彻底溃败；另一场在赫尔松，推进缓慢而笨重得多，但仍然迫使俄军从第聂伯河西岸撤出、退却。两场都成功了，于是开始形成一种预期：这场战争也许能速决，乌克兰有可能在这场战争中取得军事胜利。俄军随后就试图把巴赫穆特用作一场实质上的牵制战，用的是瓦格纳——在这场仗里算是"消耗品"。普里戈任未必把自己的部队看成消耗品，但他们被使用的确就是那种方式——于是它变成了一场消耗战。随着它旷日持久地磨下去，它开始消磨士气。但这样的战役会获得政治意义。我个人认为，丢掉巴赫穆特并没有那么重要、那么有分量。事实上，我不只是"认为"——我是知道——因为俄军花了整整一年才从巴赫穆特打到恰西夫亚尔——旁边那座地处更高地形的城镇——而且还在为它争夺；又花了整整一年才拿下恰西夫亚尔的大部分。如果你看地图上他们从巴赫穆特出发走了多远，距离并不远。所以现在可以有把握地论证——我认为证据无可辩驳——丢掉巴赫穆特对战争的大战略而言，归根结底并没有那么关键。那么，为什么在巴赫穆特上过度执着？问题在于：战役会获得政治意义，哪怕在战术上它们也许……

[5280s - 5400s]

Be justifiably freighted with that significance, and political. When you mean, is it is it like Stalingrad in in the World War Two? Because certain leaders up at top, there's certain fixation on a particular city as a symbol. As a symbol, yes. The battle for Bakhmut to me was one freighted with political significance because it was a contest, right? And wars, by their nature, are fundamentally contests of wills, and they're contests of systems between states. And these then become bellwether events. And what happens is political leaders often see the battle as significant. Maybe the city itself is not critical, but they can't afford to lose it. Why? It became very important for Ukraine because Ukraine heavily dependent on external support, on Western support, and wanted to maintain the perception after successful offensives in the fall that the war was going their way, that they had the momentum. Okay, and and believe that further expectations of support, the equipment they wanted to receive, the additional capabilities and training they want to receive, would be tied to the sense in Western countries that Ukraine is winning. And so they were very concerned. This happens all the time. Wars are full of sunk cost fallacies and this pathology of thinking. Naturally, very concerned that the loss of Bakhmut would lead to a turn in sort of Western public sentiment and the views of political decision makers in Western countries. And so there was this whole campaign organized called Fortress Bakhmut. I always worry when any city, especially a fairly small city, is declared a fortress, because then it means that forces there will not be able to withdraw or retrograde in a timely manner. Most people who end up in those battles, I know from Ukrainian troops, also always worry when you know something's declared a fortress, because then they're then they know that's going to be a pretty tough, pretty tough and bitter fight. But essentially, what happened was this became a prolonged, attritional fight over Bakhmut. Ukraine was able to draw the fight out.

【译】……未必当得起这种意义——以及政治（意义）。——你的意思是，它像二战中的斯大林格勒吗？因为顶层某些领导人对某座作为象征的城市有某种执念。——作为象征，是的。在我看来说，巴赫穆特之战是一场满载政治意义的战役，因为它是一场较量，对吧？而战争就其本性而言，根本上是意志的较量，是国家之间体系的较量。这些战役于是成了风向标事件。而实际情况往往是：政治领导人把战役看得意义重大。也许城市本身并不关键，但他们输不起。为什么？它对乌克兰变得非常重要，因为乌克兰严重依赖外部支持、西方支持，并且希望在秋季攻势成功之后维持这样的认知：战争正朝他们有利的方向走，势头在他们这边——并且相信，后续的支持、想获得的装备、想获得的额外能力与训练，都将与西方国家"乌克兰正在赢"的感觉绑定。所以他们非常担心。这种事一直在发生，战争里到处是沉没成本谬误和这种病态思维。他们自然非常担心，巴赫穆特的陷落会导致西方民意和西方国家政治决策者的态度转向。于是就组织起了这样一场整建制的运动，叫"堡垒巴赫穆特"。任何城市——尤其是相当小的城市——被宣布为堡垒时，我总会担心，因为那就意味着那里的部队将无法及时撤出或退却。我通过与乌军的接触也知道，大多数最终陷入那种战斗的人，也总是害怕某地被宣布为"堡垒"，因为他们知道，那将是一场相当难打、相当惨烈的战斗。但实质上发生的情况是：围绕巴赫穆特形成了一场长期的消耗战。乌克兰成功把这一仗拉长了……

[5400s - 5520s]

But I'm not sure necessarily to their benefit. And what happened was it tied down quite a few units in the fight for Bakhmut. And this is an ongoing debate: to what extent did the battle for Bakhmut expend very experienced troops in exchange for holding onto a town for some extra period of time? But on the other side, the Russian military was fighting with these expendable Wagner forces. A lot of them were convicts. Second, to what extent it pulled in forces that then could have been used and trained that were very experienced that could have been used and actually trained on Western equipment and could have led the summer offensive in June of 2023 instead of having green, new, inexperienced troops with the better Western equipment, but nonetheless lacking experience, having to conduct a very difficult breaching operation. So all these questions remain. I know we'll continue to debate them. But the bottom line was Bakhmut syndrome. It was essentially choosing to defend the city past the point when it made sense, and that would often happen because it's very difficult to withdraw from a population center. Right, population centers often fix fights. That tends to be at least the character of this war. And also, defense is often anchored around population centers, around cities. They serve as a key part of defense, right? So once the fight actually becomes a siege of a population center, you then get into the situation where it's very hard to withdraw, and you'll see Russians expend an immense amount of troops and personnel to try to take it, destroy the city in the process. But Ukraine also bitterly hold on to the city in effort to try to exhaust the Russian offensive there. Before we return to Prigozhin and Wagner and the Prigozhins and the mutiny, Preston, what do you think about this? Maybe even in the modern context, over fixation on particular symbolic regions, the territories or cities versus the territorial gains in the broader context.

【译】……但我不确定这是否真的对他们有利。实际发生的是，相当多的部队被拖在巴赫穆特之战里。这是一场仍在进行的辩论：巴赫穆特之战在多大程度上，是用非常经验丰富的部队去换取多守一座城镇一段时间？而在另一边，俄军是用这些可消耗的瓦格纳部队在打，其中很多是囚犯。第二，这场仗在多大程度上吸走了那些本来就非常有经验的部队——他们本可被使用和训练、本可在西方装备上受训、本可领头打 2023 年 6 月的夏季反攻——而不是让生嫩、全新、缺乏经验的部队拿着更好的西方装备、却终究缺少经验，去执行一场非常艰难的破障突破作业。这些问题都还悬而未决，我知道我们会继续争论下去。但底线就是"巴赫穆特综合征"：本质上，就是在已经不合算的时间点之后仍选择守城。这种事经常发生，因为从人口聚居点撤出非常困难。对，人口聚居点往往会"钉住"战斗，这至少是这场战争的一个特征。而且防御也常常依托人口聚居点、依托城市展开，它们构成防御的关键部分，对吧？所以一旦战斗真的变成对一座人口聚居点的围困，你就进入了很难撤离的处境，然后你会看到俄方投入海量部队和人员去夺城、在过程中摧毁城市；而乌克兰也同样死死苦守城市，试图耗尽俄军在那里的攻势。在回到普里戈任、瓦格纳和兵变之前——普雷斯顿，你怎么看这个问题？放到现代语境下：对特定象征性地区、领土或城市的过度执着，与更宏观战局中的领土得失之间……

Of the war, it's difficult, and I think it's case by case because, as Mike mentioned, sometimes it gets this this added value just because it's held for a period of time, and it was hard to take for a month, and then two months, and then three months, and all of a sudden it's this fortress city when maybe it shouldn't be that. 【译】……这很难说，我认为要具体案例具体分析。因为正如迈克所说，有时候一个地方获得这种附加价值，只是因为它被守了一段时间：难攻，一个月、两个月、三个月，突然间它就成了"堡垒城市"，而它也许本不该如此。而且两边都是如此——这正是我们在巴赫穆特看到的：双方都这样。俄罗斯高度聚焦于夺取该镇，聚焦到必须拿下的程度，如果期限内拿不下，那就是失败。我们很早就看到他们开始宣称拥有该城，他们也许夸大了自己的推进，但这恰恰说明此事对莫斯科有多重要。它在乌克兰内部也获得了同等的重要性。事先我想没有人会预料到这一点。正如迈克所说，回头看，围绕它有一些争论，但我认为，这种事情就是一个国家、一支军队会"撞上"的东西。当时乌克兰一些军事领导人受到了大量批评，我们在另外几个方向也见过类似情况——库尔斯克攻势、阿夫迪夫卡，我相信围绕那些也有过一些争论。而现在，我想围绕康斯坦丁诺夫卡你会开始看到更多。那个方向已经开始呈现出其他那些被合围地区的样子。这对军事领导层是一个很高的要求：要能认识到——没有人愿意从那种地区撤出，尤其是乌克兰的处境：他们等于是把自己国家的土地让给推进中的俄军。但到了某个时点，守下去不再值得，你是在拿前置的部队冒险。这是一个非常艰难的决定，我认为，它比我们从外部所能体察的更情绪化。——对，而那个时点就是一个判断题：什么时候你在城市战中不再是在以非常有利的消耗比消耗对手——因为你是守方、对方是攻方，对方大概率伤亡更大——什么时候你面对的是可能被合围……

There's a threat to your force. The lines of communication are being interdicted, so you're losing personnel and equipment trying to sustain your positions. And then, lastly, usually when you can really see it is when you're forced to counterattack in order to maintain your positions, because now you're taking casualties because you're the one going on the offensive in order to try to even maintain the position. And we've seen that time and time again in a number of fights. In some cases, justifiable; in other cases, you can see it may be politically motivated. At the end of the day, military strategy flows from decisions of political leaders. Political decision making holds supremacy, and they decide what is a critical objective and which battle to them is essential in the war. Even if analysts or somebody else or planners looking at might say this is not a critical fight, that doesn't matter. When political leaders decide that's a critical fight, and then when they raise political stakes and say that they're going to try to hold on to the city, then it now becomes important. Can you speak to what does it take in the modern context to gain control of a territory or to quote unquote take a territory? There's something that I've heard both you speak about, which is that on the Russian side, the Russian military claims more territory than they have actually taken, and that and the the difference between how much territory is actually taken and the claimed taken territory has has an effect on the actual operation of the military. Can you just speak to what it takes to gain control of a territory? I think that's that's up for debate, and we see Russian analysts getting frustrated and debating this day in and day out because it kind of happens in phases. If you look at a just a town in Ukraine, there's a period of time where it's it's normal, and there's a population there, and the businesses are open. But then, as the front gets closer, you start to see the population leave and the businesses close, and the next thing you know, it's just military. And then at some point, there's no military because it's in the gray zone, and you've got Russians in one side, Ukrainians on the other.

【译】……你部队面临威胁、交通线被遮断，于是你在维持阵地的人员和装备上不断损失。而最后，通常最能看清问题的时刻，是你被迫发起反击才能守住阵地的时刻——因为这时你是主动出击的一方，正在为"维持住阵地"本身而承受伤亡。这样的情形，我们在多场战斗中一次又一次看到。有些情况下是正当的；另一些情况下，你可以看出它可能是政治驱动的。归根结底，军事战略源自政治领导人的决策。政治决策居于最高地位，由他们决定什么是关键目标、哪场战役对他们是战争中必不可少的。哪怕分析师、其他什么人或者参谋人员看了会说"这不是关键战斗"，那也无关紧要。一旦政治领导人认定那是关键战斗，一旦他们抬高政治赌注、宣布要守住这座城市，它就变得重要了。——你们能否谈谈：在现代语境下，要"控制"一块领土，或者所谓"拿下"一块领土，需要什么？我听你们两位都谈过一点：俄方声称占领的领土多于实际占领的，而"实际占领"与"声称占领"之间的落差，会实际影响军事行动。你能具体讲讲"控制一块领土"需要什么吗？——我认为这本身就有争议，我们看到俄罗斯分析师们为此挠头、日复一日地争论，因为它是分阶段发生的。你随便挑乌克兰的一座城镇：有一段时间它是正常的，那里有居民，商铺开着门。然后，随着前线逼近，你开始看到人口离开、商铺关门，不知不觉间，那里只剩下军人。再往后某个时点，连军人都没有了——它已进入灰色地带，俄国人在这边，乌克兰人在那边……

What Russia has done a lot, this I feel like it's increased. Is they will have one soldier forward; they'll wave a flag for a drone to pick up. This last week we saw some AI being utilized, where it was a like a fake video. Yeah. It's a fake video. Called out by the Russians themselves, saying, "Guys, what are we doing here? This is not good." But even if it is a real soldier, that's the PR victory of saying, "Look how far we've advanced. We now control this territory." The Russian pushback there is one soldier in a basement is not control of territory. Now, is it a platoon of soldiers? Is it is it a regiment pushing forward beyond that town? Control of the village, you don't really have it. So it's tricky in that sense. And I think if you're talking about full blown control, as in you can move, you can come and go from that town as see fit, you're probably talking about 20 plus kilometers back from the front line. And even then, you're still at risk of getting hit every single day by drones. But these forward areas, it's it's been interesting, man. Russia has a concern that I have is we're starting to see Putin more and more reference some of these claims that are not in line with reality. And I I really wonder where up that chain of command they understand that they're sending a false report. The AI video is a great example. Somebody made that. So where up that chain does somebody know this is not an accurate report? We're not there, and they send it anyways, or is it going all the way up and everybody genuinely believes that to be true because it's.

【译】俄罗斯大量玩的一套——我感觉这还在变本加厉——是让一名士兵前出，向无人机挥旗让它拍到。然后他们把那个点标在地图上，宣称："以他为界，往回我们全控制了。"——对，就是那个士兵在那片区域举旗的画面。——通常是视频。上周我们还看到有人用上了 AI，做的是一个像假视频的东西。——对，是假视频。连俄国人自己都出来揭穿，说："伙计们，我们在干什么？这可不妙。"但就算士兵是真的，那也是一场公关胜利："看我们推进了多远，我们现在控制了这片土地。"俄方的反驳是：一个蹲在地下室里的士兵，不叫控制领土。那么，是一个排的士兵？还是一个团越过那座城镇继续前推？这就众说纷纭了。因为就算村里有你一大股部队，如果你无法给他们补给，或者乌克兰在整个区域掌握无人机优势、你谁也进不去出不来，那我们在谈什么？控制村子——你其实并没有。所以这个意义上很难界定。而如果你说的是完全意义上的控制——你能机动、能随意进出那座城镇——那你大概说的是距前线 20 多公里的纵深。即便那样，你仍然每天都有被无人机打击的风险。但这些前沿区域，说实话，很有意思。俄罗斯这边——这是我的一个担忧——我们开始看到普京越来越多地引用这些与实际不符的宣称。我真的很想知道，沿那条指挥链向上，到哪一层他们其实知道自己在提交假报告。AI 视频就是绝佳例子：总有人制作了它。那么沿指挥链向上，到哪一层有人知道"这不是准确的报告，我们并不在那里"，却照样往上送？还是说它一路通达顶端、所有人都真心相信那是事实，因为……

[5880s - 6000s]

It paints a very different picture of how the war is playing out. I think on the Russian maps, they consistently claim themselves to be something like 15 kilometers, 10 kilometers at least forward to where they actually are, and they regularly cite positions they have from soldiers that they might have in place somewhere that infiltrated, but they don't have them anymore; they've been lost, or as Preston says, they took pictures of some flags, and it actually very negatively shapes Russian operations. In some ways, we are fortunate that they have such a cultural falsification of battlefield data, because they then organize fire support around lines and positions they don't actually hold. Their troops will often advance, or units that rotate into an area will be given the wrong information, and what they think are actually their forward line of troops is contested territory. Right, so they'll they'll end up having their own soldiers killed because the map they were given is not correct. I think Preston is also very right that clearest information trickles up to political decision making and to Putin, and that that may be part of the reason why he holds the view the Russian forces are still advancing in the Donbas because, in fact, the maps is being supplied lie in a very literal sense, and also, believe it or not, there is a pattern to Russian offensive operations where often we see them really intensify offensive efforts right now during this time period from October to November, and and their casualties go up, and it is often because they find themselves pressed to actually take the territory that they've been claiming to control in reports for months, because now they're in a point where senior commanders are basically saying, "Now show it to me," and they don't actually control the terrain, and now they have to really throw men at it to try.

【译】……因为它描绘出的战争图景与实际天差地别。我认为在俄方地图上，他们始终把自己标在比实际位置靠前大约 15 公里、至少 10 公里的地方；他们经常引用一些阵地，说是他们渗透进某处、可能有士兵就位的地方——其实那些早就没有了、已经丢失，或者如普雷斯顿所说，只是拍了些旗帜的照片。而这对俄军行动的塑造是非常负面的。某种程度上说，他们有这样一种战场数据造假的文化，我们算是幸运的：因为他们会围绕自己并不实际控制的战线和阵地去组织火力支援。他们的部队推进时，或换防进入某区域的部队，常被交给错误的信息：他们以为是自己前沿部队线的位置，其实是争议地带。对，于是他们自己的士兵会因此丧命，因为发给他们的地图是不对的。我认为普雷斯顿也说得非常对：这类信息会一路渗透上传到政治决策层、到普京那里，而这可能是他坚持认为俄军仍在顿巴斯推进的部分原因——因为事实上，送到他手里的地图在非常字面的意义上就是在撒谎。另外，信不信由你，俄军的攻势行动有一个模式：我们经常看到他们在现在这个时段——十月至十一月——真正加强进攻力度，然后伤亡上升。这常常是因为他们发现自己被逼到墙角，必须实际去夺取那些在报告里"已控制"了好几个月的地盘——因为现在到了这样一个节点：高级指挥官基本上在说"现在把它拿给我看"，而他们并没有控制那片地形，于是只能真的往里大量砸人，试图……

[6000s - 6120s]

To realize the lie that they've been telling for some time, and I completely agree with Preston said, we have a very hard time mapping it. It is a gray zone for some substantial distance, and to me, probably the best way to describe control is you're able to use terrain for your purposes. That is, not only you have you denied it to your enemy, not only do you have presence there, but you are able to largely use this terrain, and the enemy does not have substantial amount of fire control over it. Because at this stage of the war, yes, you can go fifteen, ten kilometers from the front line; those are technically your positions, but you can't freely move around in them. You can't do much of what a a ground force would expect to do. You have presence, but you don't really have control. You're not able to use it for your own purposes. And many places, Ukrainian Russian positions in cities, or sometimes in tree lines, are so commingled that they're marbled, and it's very hard to map control at all. Because if there's hundreds of Russian troops in Konstantinovka in basements, commingled with hundreds of Ukrainian troops in basements, who controls Konstantinovka? How would you know that? How would you empirically judge it? It's crazy when you really think about it, Lex. Like I could color a map on the screen, and nothing matters doesn't really change anything. The fact that leaders are making those changes and then sending their troops forward with bad information is crazy. It's costing lives. Like at the very least, you know, when they say there was a Kupiansk a little over a year ago, Russia claimed ownership of it. This was one that Putin said that they had control over. He famously invited international journalists. He said, "Go in and check it out for yourselves." And they and they didn't. And what that did then was you could see Russian forces were able to relocate assets to other areas, air power and fire support that might be needed in certain areas was either pushed forward or pulled back because it wasn't needed there anymore. So you've got these soldiers out there that.

【译】……把谎了很长时间的谎圆成真。我完全同意普雷斯顿刚才说的：我们很难把它标到地图上。在相当大的纵深上它是灰色地带。在我看来，对"控制"最好的定义大概是：你能为你的目的使用这片地形。也就是说，你不仅把它屏蔽给了敌方、不仅在那里有存在，而且你能在很大程度上使用这片地形，敌方对它没有实质性的火力控制。因为在战争的现阶段，是的，你可以抵达距前线十五公里、十公里的地方——那些在技术上算你的阵地——但你在其中无法自由机动，做不成一支地面部队理应做的大部分事情。你有存在，但没有真正的控制，你无法把它用于自己的目的。而且在很多地方，乌克兰与俄罗斯的阵地——在城市里，有时在树带里——交织到"大理石纹"般的地步，根本没法在地图上标出控制。如果康斯坦丁诺夫卡有几百名俄军士兵蹲在地下室里，与几百名乌军士兵的地下室交织在一起，那谁控制康斯坦丁诺夫卡？你怎么知道？你凭什么凭经验判定？认真想想这很疯狂，莱克斯。我可以在屏幕上给地图涂个颜色，那什么都不是，不改变任何东西。而领导人们做着这种改动，然后拿着错误的信息把部队派上去，这才疯狂。这在要人命。至少，比如一年多前的库皮扬斯克，俄罗斯宣称占领了它——这是普京亲自说他们已控制的地方。他出了名地邀请国际记者，说："你们自己进去看看。"而他们没有去。那之后你能看到的效果是：俄军得以把兵力兵器转移到其他地区，某些地段的空中力量和火力支援或被前推、或被后撤，因为那里不再需要了。于是就出现了那些士兵，他们……

[6120s - 6240s]

Now supposedly are ten kilometers forward. They can't get air strikes on the Ukrainian positions because if they call that up, somebody is going to have to say, "Yeah, okay, the lines have changed a little bit." So they're they're genuinely making these changes, making these false reports, and it is it is it's hard to fully understand to be hard to grasp. It's costing lives. If you want an anecdote, I was just west of Kupiansk at that time last year when Gerasimov declared to Putin in the meeting that they had all the Ukrainian units in Kupiansk encircled. And I was there with a colleague from one of the Ukrainian brigades, basically reading this news, and it it it's remarkable. I mean, when you're actually right by there and you see the situation, these things don't don't pass the giggle test, but nonetheless, this is what you see Russian senior military leadership tell their political leadership. To me, this is fascinating because maybe you can educate me on this, but my guess would be throughout human history, throughout all the wars, whether you look at Napoleon, Hitler, Churchill, Stalin, maybe even Genghis Khan, although the interesting dynamic there, there's always going to be some distortion of information as it travels up the chain of command, and you can study where the distortion is greatest depending on the culture. Who is a soldier lying to their commander? Is it the general lying to the commander in chief? There's some distortion happening somewhere. So maybe can you speak to the distortions happening on both sides here? From what I can see, Ukraine is getting a much better picture of the current status along the front. Very hard to say where and on both sides. Very hard to say if and where there's a breakdown. And you're absolutely right. Commanders want to say that they're doing a good job. It takes a lot of integrity to say we lost positions, and we've seen that in the Ukrainian military where commanders have been relieved because they've not reported that up correctly. It seems to be much more much more of a culture issue in the Russian military, top to bottom.

【译】……现在"号称"前出了十公里。他们无法呼叫对乌军阵位的空袭，因为一旦往上申请，就得有人说："呃，好吧，战线其实是变了一点。"所以他们确实在持续做这种改动、提交这些假报告，而这很难完全理解、很难消化，它在要人命。如果你想要个轶事：去年那个时节，我正在库皮扬斯克以西，格拉西莫夫在会上向普京宣称，他们已把库皮扬斯克内的所有乌军部队合围。我当时和一位来自某个乌克兰旅的同事在一起，基本就是现场读着这条新闻，那真是……我的意思是，当你真的就在旁边、亲眼看到态势时，这些说法连"引人发笑测试"都过不了（荒谬得可笑），但尽管如此，你看到俄军高层领导就是这么对他们的政治领导讲的。——在我看来这非常迷人——也许你能给我上一课——我猜，贯穿人类历史、贯穿所有战争，无论你看拿破仑、希特勒、丘吉尔、斯大林，甚至成吉思汗——那里面的动力关系很有意思——信息沿指挥链上行时总会有某种失真，而你可以研究在哪种文化里失真最大。是士兵在向他的指挥官撒谎？还是将军在向总司令撒谎？总有什么地方在发生失真。那么，也许你们可以谈谈双方各自的失真？——以我所见，乌克兰对前线当前态势的图景掌握要好得多。双方哪里失真非常难说。很难说有没有、在哪里出现断裂。而你说得完全对：指挥官都想说自己干得不错，说"我们丢了阵地"需要极大的正直，我们在乌军中也见过这种事——有指挥官因为没有如实上报而被解职。在俄军那里，这似乎更是一个自上而下的文化问题……

[6240s - 6360s]

But yeah, it's definitely happening on both sides. Definitely, definitely happening to a degree. Hard to tell where that line is. I think it's worse on the Russian side. It's because of the military culture. First, there is a real lack of trust in the Russian military between soldiers and their commanders, and that breeds a lack of cohesion. Second, the military runs on a very coarse of culture, so you expect that for any mistake, you are going to be punished, right? And so, there is a strong incentive to to lie, and and then lastly, there is a centralization of command and control, where rather than the junior officer who's actually at the position commanding the troops who has greater authority, it's the senior officer who has greater authority because they decide everything, and then they kind of give the scheme of maneuver the orders for junior folks to execute. And for the Russian military, the effects are worse because it the way they plan military decision making can often lead to you know what in data is called a garbage in garbage out problem, where if the information on what's happening to tactical level is is not correct, it's false, and the planning is based on that, and people are executing combat tasks on the basis of things that aren't true, then it's very problematic for that military, especially because they don't have the same culture of having freedom to adjust, adapt, make decisions at a more junior level on the ground, right? They they're waiting for information to reach the senior commander. I don't want to make it sound this ridiculously regimented, but nonetheless, it's a bigger problem for the Russian military as we see consistently. Even though the Ukrainian forces have also had issues with falsification information and commanders, plain commanders getting relieved, especially.

【译】但确实，双方都在发生，程度确实存在，只是那条线画在哪里很难说。我认为俄方更严重，原因在军事文化。第一，俄军中士兵与其指挥官之间严重缺乏信任，而那会滋生凝聚力的缺失。第二，这支军队运转在一种非常严苛的惩罚文化上——你要预期任何失误都会受罚，对吧——于是撒谎的激励非常强。最后，是指挥控制的集中化：本该是真正在阵地上带兵的初级军官拥有更大权限，实际上却是高级军官权限更大，因为他们决定一切，然后把机动方案、把命令发给下级人员去执行。而对俄军来说，后果更糟，因为他们那套军事决策的规划方式，往往导致数据领域所说的"垃圾进、垃圾出"问题：如果战术层面发生的情况信息不正确、是假的，而计划又基于它制定，人们基于不实之情执行战斗任务，那对这支军队就是大麻烦——尤其因为他们没有那种在更初级的地面上层级临机调整、适应、决断的自由文化，对吧？他们在等信息送达高级指挥官。我不想把它说得僵化到可笑的地步，但无论如何，这是我们反复看到的：这对俄军是更大的问题。尽管乌克兰部队也有信息造假的问题，也有指挥官——普通指挥官——被解职的情况，尤其是在……

[6360s - 6480s]

Under the previous commander in chief Syrskyi, and and sometimes also because they were placed in untenable position, because they wouldn't have enough men, they had to defend the position. They too were told that they can't withdraw a position without approval from the general staff, and if they lost the position, they reported it. They would be told they have to take it back, but they didn't have the forces to take it back. And then a commander is sometimes faced with an ethical dilemma: Do I sacrifice men to take a position that I know is untenable, or I can't take back? Or do I face the consequences of failing to report it, but I preserve my own men? What do I do? These are not the easiest choices. You know, it's not clean cut. It seems, from my just observational history, there seems to be, especially like at the general level, a kind of optimism that there's a a path towards winning this war at a strategic level soon. It seems like United States and other nations have been engaged in, you know, in in case Afghanistan and Iraq is like something like forever wars. How does that at which level of distortion of information do you keep going with this kind of momentum of a war going on for prolonged period of time without any strategic victory, or is that not attributable to distortion of information and something else? Oh, I think Afghanistan is a very interesting case study of how we ended up for twenty years in that war. I would, Preston, I think you should talk a bit about that to see where in that fight. Well, there's a pride aspect to it. Of can you get it done? Yes, I can, and it's not necessarily overstating what you're able to do, but understanding you there are constraints on your resources and and what you have available to to accomplish the mission, and nobody wants to be the one to say I can't do that. And there's a yeah, there's just some pride in in finding a way to make it work, and I think a lot of that comes down to bad advice at some level of setting those conditions.

【译】……前任总司令瑟尔斯基任内。有时也是因为他们被放到了守不住的位置上：兵力不够，却必须守阵地。他们同样被告知，不经总参谋部批准不得放弃阵地；如果丢了阵地，他们上报了，就会被命令必须夺回来，可他们根本没有夺回来的兵力。于是指挥官有时面临伦理困境：我是不是要牺牲士兵，去夺一个我知道守不住、或者我夺不回的阵地？还是承担瞒报的后果，但保全自己的士兵？我该怎么办？这些不是最轻松的抉择，你知道，这不是非黑即白的事。——从我的纯属观察的历史来看，似乎存在——尤其是在将官层面——一种乐观主义，认为在战略层面上，不久后就有一条赢得这场战争的路径。看起来，美国和其他国家曾陷入——比如阿富汗和伊拉克——那种可称为"永远的战争"的境地。在信息失真的哪个层面，你会让一场战争在没有任何战略胜利的情况下，凭着这种势头长期打下去？还是说这根本不能归因于信息失真，而是别的东西？——哦，我认为阿富汗是一个非常有趣的案例，研究我们是怎么在那场战争里待了二十年的。普雷斯顿，我觉得你应该讲讲这一点，看看你们在那种战争里的处境。——这里面有自尊心的成分。"你能办到吗？""能。"这不一定是夸大自己的能力，而是明知你的资源、你完成任务所能用的东西是有约束的，但没有人想当那个说"我做不到"的人。是的，这里面就是有一种把事情办成的自豪感，而我认为这在很大程度上，要归结为某个层级给出的糟糕建议、归结为那些条件的设定……

[6480s - 6600s]

You should not be put in that position if you cannot accomplish the task. Afghanistan, great example would be: we were we had combat units flooded across the entirety of the country. Great for security, not necessarily great for nation building, not necessarily great for developing, changing a culture if that was even possible in Afghanistan to get the people out in Zhari District in Kandahar Province to understand that the person in Kabul that doesn't speak their language and they've never seen before represents them. That's not that's not a soldier's job. We don't know how to do that. We're not very good at it, but we don't want to say we can't. So we say we got it. We can figure it out. We'll learn counterinsurgency operations. We will have we will send people to language courses. When in reality, somewhere along the line, somebody should have said, "You know what? This isn't what we can do. We need State Department. We need USAID. We need X, Y, Z to be able to step in and fill this." It's I think it's a military mindset, and I don't think that's unique to the United States. I imagine most countries. The higher you get up the ranks, the generals are very much, "We got this." I want to add to Preston's great perspective the point of view from the other side, having had a bit of experience dealing with political leadership. Sometimes the information that the military is passing up is overly optimistic. It's you know this mindset. I don't want to say I can't get done, but there are also definitely times where the political leadership has clear assumptions and is very resistant to information that is adverse to those assumptions. First, one of the basic inputs into wars when leaders think they are going to go to war is war optimism. They have a clear set of assumptions. Often it's that the war will be sharp and short; that it'll be a quick war. Good example: Russian full-scale invasion in twenty twenty two, or our war with Iran this year. They are resistant to information that actually it's quite risky.

【译】……如果你完成不了任务，你就不该被放到那个位置上。阿富汗就是个绝佳例子：我们的作战部队铺满了整个国家。这对治安很好，但对国家建设未必好，对发展、改变一种文化未必好——就算那在阿富汗是可能的话——比如让坎大哈省扎里县的百姓理解：喀布尔那个不说他们的语言、他们从未见过的人"代表"他们。那不是士兵的工作。我们不知道怎么做，我们也不擅长，但我们不想说我们做不到。于是我们说：没问题，我们能搞定，我们能想出办法，我们会学反叛乱作战，我们会送人去上语言课程。而现实是，这条线上的某个地方，本该有人说："你知道吗？这不是我们能做到的事。我们需要国务院，需要美国国际开发署，需要 X、Y、Z 各种机构介入来填补这个空缺。"我认为这是一种军队式思维，而且我不认为这是美国独有的，我猜大多数国家都一样：军衔越高，将军们越是一口"我们搞得定"。——我想在普雷斯顿的精彩视角之外，补上另一方的视角，我在与政治领导层打交道上也有过一些经验。有时军队向上传递的信息过于乐观——就是这种"我不想说我办不到"的心态。但也确实有一些时候，是政治领导层带着一整套明确的假设，非常抗拒与这些假设相悖的信息。首先，当领导人认为自己要开战时，战争的一个基本输入就是"战争乐观主义"。他们有一套明确的假设，往往假设战争将是速决而短暂的、将是快仗。好例子就是 2022 年俄罗斯的全面入侵，或者我们今年对伊朗的那场战争。他们抗拒那些"其实风险很大"的信息……

[6600s - 6720s]

What will happen if it becomes a prolonged war? When military leaders tell them that it might be this, it might be that, then you know these forces, they face the problem that if they basically tell them this can't be done, the political leader will tell them, no, you're just the wrong person for the job. I'll find somebody who'll tell me they can do it. You think you need 300,000 troops for Iraq? I'll find someone who will do it for a lot less. Okay, and and lastly, there are often cases also. Let's say you know, prolonged conventional war like Afghanistan, and political leadership might ask, not would you need to win the war? It's not clear that victory defined in their terms is necessarily possible. They'll ask, really, what is the amount of troops we need for me to kick this can down the road so that we don't lose the war on my watch, and then it goes to the next election, and then maybe becomes somebody else's problem. Okay, so there are many times where I've seen that it's not the military leadership that's to blame. It's that the political leadership, which at the end of the day makes key decisions on the war, sets constraints on the military of how they can fight, what forces they have available to fight, have certain assumptions, have certain ideas, and they're not necessarily amenable to military advice. Where they take they're listening to the military advice, but they're also considering domestic politics, they're considering international politics as other things that predominate in their calculus, other than strict kind of operational planning or military analysis. And I think that's important to consider because wars, at the end of the day, are started by political leaders. They're the ones who decide to go to war and have very strong views and assumptions, and some extent also emotions and fears that play into that. How often is it that military leadership, like the generals, try to say, don't do this? In terms of starting wars, this is this comes to a bigger theme. Is I would love to see a long distance future of human civilization.

【译】……如果打成长期战争会怎么样？当军方领导人告诉他们，可能是这样、可能是那样时，这些人就面临一个问题：如果他们基本明说"这办不到"，政治领导人就会对他们说：不，你只是干这份工作的错误人选，我会找一个说能办到的人来。你觉得打伊拉克需要 30 万部队？我能找到一个用少得多的兵力就办成的人。好。最后还有一种常见情形：比如阿富汗这样的长期常规战争，政治领导层问的可能根本不是"打赢这场战争你需要什么"——按他们定义的胜利是否可能，根本不清楚——他们会问的其实是：我们真正需要多少兵力，才能把这个问题往后踢，让战争别在我的任内输掉，然后拖到下一次选举，也许就变成了别人题。好，所以有很多次我看到，该受责难的并不是军方领导层，而是政治领导层——他们归根结底对战争做关键决策，给军队设下约束：怎么打、有哪些部队可用；他们带着某些假设、某些成见，未必听得进军事建议。他们在听军事建议，但同时也在考虑国内政治、国际政治，考虑其算计中占主导的其他因素，而不是严格的作战规划或军事分析。我认为这一点很重要，必须考虑进去，因为战争归根结底是政治领导人发动的。是他们决定开战，带着非常强烈的观点和假设，某种程度上还有掺杂其中的情绪与恐惧。——军方领导层、比如将军们，试图说"别这么干"的情况有多常见？——关于发动战争，这就引向一个更大的主题：我希望能看到人类文明在一个遥远的未来……

[6720s - 6840s]

That doesn't have wars, and it seems like we start wars. We broadly humanity, and I would love to know which are the humans amongst us that can stop them from starting. I think there are plenty of times where military leaders give the advice that a war could be a risky or a costly misadventure. They don't necessarily say don't do this, but the advice they give and they lay out right has clear implications to it. And there are plenty of times where military leaders, or not military leaders, let's say civilian leaders, in our case, what was the Department of Defense, will advise against a war. Right. Yes, there are plenty of times where they don't give that advice, but I I'd be open to Preston's views on this. But if there's some public sense of the military profession as folks that are actually eager to go to a war, that's often not the case, and that's not been my experience. People who have commanded other folks in combat and who have lost lives and who understand the implications of that endeavor are very rarely eager to pursue it. Right, especially if they're not sure that the cause is right, or that it's been well thought through, or that the strategy makes sense, or that this is very much a necessary war. So it's not been my my impression. One of the challenges there, Lex, is it's not at least the way we operate in the United States. It's not the military's job to make the decision to go to war or not. It's their job to advise on what the possible what the capabilities are, what the options are for the president to eventually the commander in chief to eventually make that decision. So I think we have to be careful with that that bleeding into this war is a political endeavor.

【译】……不再有战争。而我们似乎总在开启战争——广义上的人类——我很想知道，我们中间究竟是哪些人，能够阻止战争的开启。我认为，有很多次，军方领导人给出的建议都是：这场战争可能是一场高风险、高代价的鲁莽冒险。他们不一定会说"别这么干"，但他们给出和摆出的建议带有明确的含义。也有很多次——或者说不是军方领导人，让我们说文职领导人，在我们的体制里，也就是当时的国防部——会反对一场战争。对。是的，也有很多次他们不给那种建议。我很想听听普雷斯顿在这点上的看法。但如果公众有某种印象，觉得军人这个职业的人是真心渴望打仗的，那往往并非如此，至少不是我的经历。那些在战斗中指挥过他人、失去过生命、理解这番事业意味着什么的人，极少热衷于此。对——尤其是当他们不确定事业是否正义、这事是否经过深思熟虑、战略是否说得通、这是否真是一场必要的战争的时候。所以至少我的印象不是那样。——这里的一个挑战，莱克斯，是至少在我们美国的运作方式下，决定打不打仗不是军队的职责。军队的职责是提供咨询：可能的能力是什么、选项有哪些，供总统——最终的三军统帅——来做那个决定。所以我认为我们必须小心，别让这种越界渗透进来：战争是一项政治事业……

[6840s - 6960s]

So when the when the military starts advising on we should or we shouldn't go to war, I think that's a very slippery slope. They can say this will or will not accomplish our objectives. You know, if we drop a couple bombs, it might not get us to the point where we actually can say that we we got what we wanted out of this. But broadly speaking, yes or no to war, I think is is is probably not best reserved for the military. Absolutely, war is a social political phenomenon, and there's political leadership that has to not just make these decisions, but holds the supremacy, making and the accountability for it. And in our system, specifically the United States, the senior most military officer, the Chairman Joint Chiefs of Staff, does not command forces. They are the top advisor on military affairs to the president. Actually, quite different than other systems where you might have commander in chief or some someone else that actually directly, for example, in the case of Ukraine's or the Russian military, where you have a chief of general staff or a commander in chief that's a military position that actually can directly command forces. Speaking of structure and hierarchy of the different organizations of the military, getting back to Prigozhin, yes, yes, we forgot about him. We talked about 17 hours ago. So what happened? What happened there? You talked about the Wagner Group and Bakhmut. What was with the mutiny? What does it represent? What does it show about the Russian military? Okay, so Prigozhin had this ongoing conflict with Gerasimov. Why? He was basically positioning Wagner as a more successful alternative. Okay, and I think he was allowed to position Wagner in this way. And I think there were elites in Russia who were backing him as well. So in some extent, you had an internecine battle between different elite clans. One associated with the Ministry of Defense, the military; another one backing Prigozhin, and Prigozhin was given the sleight. He would be constantly complaining that he wasn't being given enough artillery ammo and so on and so forth. Even actually, his effort was a priority effort, and he was.

【译】……所以当军队开始就"我们应该"还是"我们不应该"开战提出建议时，我认为那是一条非常滑的斜坡。他们可以说：这样做能或不能达成我们的目标。比如我们扔几颗炸弹，也许到不了那个我们真能说"我们从中得到了想要的东西"的地步。但笼统地讲，对战争说"是"或"否"，恐怕最好不要留给军队。——绝对如此。战争是一种社会政治现象，必须由政治领导层来做这些决定：他们不仅握有决策的最高权威，也承担相应的问责。而在我们的体制里——具体说是美国——最高级的军官，参谋长联席会议主席，并不指挥部队。他是总统在军事事务上的最高顾问。这实际上与其他一些体制很不一样：别的体制里可能有总司令或别的什么人实际直接（指挥部队），比如乌克兰或俄军的情况——那里有总参谋长或总司令，那是真正可以直接指挥部队的军职。——说到军队各种组织的结构与层级——回到普里戈任吧。——对，对，我们把他给忘了。——我们大概"十七个小时前"聊过他。那么到底发生了什么？那里发生了什么？你讲了瓦格纳集团和巴赫穆特。那次兵变是怎么回事？它意味着什么？它反映了俄军的什么？——好。普里戈任与格拉西莫夫之间有这场持续的冲突。为什么？他基本上是在把瓦格纳定位成一个更成功的替代选项。而且我认为，他是被允许以这种方式定位瓦格纳的；我认为俄罗斯也有精英在背后支持他。所以在某种程度上，这是不同精英派系之间的内斗：一派与国防部、与军方关联；另一派 backing 普里戈任。而普里戈任受了冷遇：他会没完没了地抱怨，说给他的炮弹不够等等；可实际上他的行动是被优先保障的行动，而且他……

[6960s - 7080s]

Given a lot of artillery ammo to sustain it, and he was also building out this kind of popular following, and to some extent, trying to see if he could expand his political cachet. Ultimately, that competition between him and the military, which played out at the leadership level, as he actively attacked the Ministry of Defense and the Chief of General Staff, but also Wagner forces looked down on the regular Russian military, seeing them as incompetent and ineffective, and the regular Russian military looked down on Wagner forces, seeing them as either hired mercenaries or convicts. Right. So there was these clashes between them, and they competed with each other during the Battle of Bakhmut because even in the fight for Soledar and later in the Battle of Bakhmut, Wagner and the Airborne competed for who would take credit. Right, and later on in the battles, we got to April May. Prigozhin started saying the regular military spooled artillery and fire support from him because they don't want Wagner to win. They want Wagner to lose more personnel, and then they want to come in at the end and take the credit. He basically advanced this conspiracy theory. But the mutiny really kicks off because at a certain point, the Russian Ministry of Defense has had it, and Shoigu, essentially clearly much more powerful player at the end of the day, has an ultimatum issued that after the Battle of Bakhmut is decided, that all Wagner personnel have to sign contracts with the military. Right. For a long time, Wagner was essentially an autonomous force supported by the Russian military, and generally integrated into an operational command, right, and logistically also supported by the the regular Russian forces, but with a huge amount of autonomy where Prigozhin was essentially running his own army and his own fight, in coordination with the Russian Airborne, and Shoigu's ultimatum trapped Prigozhin essentially, and it put them in a position Wagner had withdrawn from Bakhmut and was replenishing personnel.

【译】（瓦格纳）手里有大量炮弹弹药来维持作战，他也在打造自己的民众追随基础，并且在某种程度上试探能否扩大自己的政治影响力。归根结底，他与军方之间的这种竞争在领导层层面展开——他公开攻击国防部和总参谋长；与此同时，瓦格纳部队看不起俄正规军，认为他们无能、低效；正规军也看不起瓦格纳部队，把他们视为拿钱办事的雇佣兵或囚徒。双方就是在这种摩擦中冲突不断，并且在巴赫穆特战役期间彼此竞争：早在争夺索列达尔的战斗以及随后的巴赫穆特战役中，瓦格纳与空降兵就在争抢功劳归属。到了战役后期的四五月间，普里戈任开始声称，正规军把炮兵和火力支援从他那里抽走了，因为他们不想让瓦格纳赢；他们想让瓦格纳损失更多人员，然后自己最后下场收割功劳。他基本上抛出了这套阴谋论。但兵变真正爆发的原因是：到了某个节点，俄罗斯国防部忍无可忍，而绍伊古——归根到底显然是更强势的一方——发出了最后通牒：巴赫穆特战役尘埃落定之后，所有瓦格纳人员必须与军方签订合同。要知道，长期以来瓦格纳本质上是一支由俄军支持的自主力量，通常被纳入作战指挥体系，后勤也由正规部队提供，但拥有极大的自主权——普里戈任实际上是在与俄空降兵协同的情况下，指挥着自己的军队、打着自己的仗。绍伊古的最后通牒实际上把普里戈任逼入了绝境，使瓦格纳处于这样的境地：此时瓦格纳已撤出巴赫穆特，正在补充兵员。

[7080s - 7200s]
