And was essentially in rest in Luhansk, and Prigozhin sensed that he was trapped—that either Shoigu was going to force the complete surrender of Wagner's autonomy and he would lose control, command and control his personnel, or he had to go on this reckless adventure. And I think he may have conceived of this not as any attempted regime change, but some some analysts that I know saw the mutiny as essentially a petition. He then tries to advance on Rostov in Russia, which is where the headquarters of the Russian military effort for the war is. Wagner, if you remember those scenes, the footage actually encircles and occupies the headquarters. There's a standoff. I mean, it does look from the outside as a an attempt to take over power. Well, absolutely, it is it is a mutiny. It's a mutiny in the midst of a war, and he wants Shoigu and Gerasimov. He's essentially stuck though because they are not in the Rostov headquarters, and Putin comes out very vocally, seeing this and declaring this as a betrayal in the midst of a war, and a whole set of Wagner columns. There are two Wagner columns that are advancing past Rostov down the road towards Moscow. And by the way, at the same time, they're shooting down Russian helicopters, they're shooting down Russian planes. Actually, Wagner's mutiny is quite damaging for the Russian air force. It kills a lot, quite a few personnel, and they're advancing towards Moscow. There's some degree of panic because elites in Moscow themselves are not sure because all the Russian forces are deployed in around Ukraine and they're not prepared to face these Wagner troops who are actually fairly experienced. And and I want to be clear: they have tanks, they have air defense with them, they have armored fighting vehicles. They brought a lot of equipment with them to the fight. They're I want to be.

【译】（瓦格纳）当时基本在卢甘斯克休整。普里戈任意识到自己被困住了：要么绍伊古将迫使瓦格纳彻底交出自主权，他将失去对人员的控制和指挥权；要么他就得踏上这场鲁莽的冒险。我认为他也许并未把这构想成任何夺权企图——我认识的一些分析人士其实把这场兵变看作本质上是一份"请愿书"。随后他挥师向俄罗斯的罗斯托夫进发，那里是俄军对乌作战总部的所在地。如果你还记得那些画面：瓦格纳发布的画面显示，他们实际上包围并占领了总部，形成对峙。从外部看，这确实像一次夺权企图。——没错，这绝对是一场兵变，是战争进行当中发生的兵变，他要的是绍伊古和格拉西莫夫。但他实际上陷入了僵局，因为两人都不在罗斯托夫的总部里。普京随即高调出面，将此定性为战争中的背叛行径。与此同时，多支瓦格纳纵队开动——有两支瓦格纳纵队越过罗斯托夫，沿公路向莫斯科推进。顺便说一句，与此同时他们还在击落俄军直升机、击落俄军战机。事实上，瓦格纳兵变对俄罗斯空军造成了相当大的损害，打死了不少人员。他们一路向莫斯科推进，莫斯科出现了一定程度的恐慌，因为莫斯科的精英们自己也心里没底：所有俄军都部署在乌克兰周边，没有准备好去迎击这些其实相当有作战经验的瓦格纳部队。而且我想说清楚：他们带着坦克、带着防空系统、带着装甲战斗车辆，携带了大量装备投入这场行动。他们……我想说明白——

[7200s - 7320s]

Clear. They're not advancing in trucks and pickups. This is actually mechanized formations. So, how close did that come to overthrowing power in Moscow? Oh, I don't think very close. I don't think that they had any any chance of doing it. And Moscow is a massive city. So, if they had gotten in there, I don't know what they could have hoped to achieve. Was there some degree to which that was the goal? Look, I think what the goal was: once they got to Rostov and had no chance of either getting their hands on Sergey Gerasimov, and Putin had essentially ruled against them, their only leverage was the advance on Moscow. They used the advance on Moscow. Prigozhin used it to then negotiate a stand down and amnesty, and the and then a handover of assets and control of Wagner operations over time. That's what happened. He used that as bargaining leverage. I think he also early on saw that he did not have support from the Russian elite. The Russian elite did not see him as an alternative to Putin. So there's no chance of success in regime change. And the reason for that is straightforward: in personalist authoritarian regimes, the first thing they do is eliminate alternatives. And most coups you never even hear of; they fail. They take; they never take off the ground. This is even a rare case of a real mutiny that becomes this phenomenal 24-hour event. Most attempts at coups in personalist authoritarian regimes are stopped by intelligence; they never take off the ground because typically the number of elites and people you have to involve in organizing a coup makes it very difficult to keep it secret. So, if that authoritarian system is at all effective, it's going to discover it. But that's what I think ultimately happened. And of course, lastly, I remember having these discussions at time. Folks thought that Prigozhin somehow got off easy because he negotiated a handoff or a sell off of his assets. He was still alive after the coup. But it was clear to those of us who followed Russia that there was no way he was going to survive this. Speaking of which, going to perplexity.

【译】——这一点：他们不是坐卡车和皮卡推进的，这是真正的机械化编队。——那么，这次兵变离推翻莫斯科的政权有多近？——哦，我认为并不很近。我认为他们没有任何机会成功。而且莫斯科是一座巨型城市，就算他们真打进去了，我也不知道他们能指望达成什么。——那这是否在某种程度上就是目标所在？——听着，我认为实际的目标是：一旦他们到了罗斯托夫、既没有机会抓到谢尔盖·格拉西莫夫，而普京又已经明确表态反对他们，他们唯一的筹码就是向莫斯科进军。他们动用了这个筹码。普里戈任利用向莫斯科进军，谈成了停手与赦免，以及随后逐步移交资产和瓦格纳业务控制权的安排。事情就是这样。他把它当作了讨价还价的筹码。我认为他也很早就看到，自己得不到俄罗斯精英阶层的支持——精英们不把他视为普京的替代者，所以政权更迭没有任何成功可能。原因很简单：在个人独裁政权里，统治者要做的第一件事就是消灭替代选项。大多数政变你根本从未听说过——它们失败了，从未离地起飞。而这甚至是一桩罕见的案例：一场真正的兵变演变成了这样一场惊人的 24 小时事件。在个人独裁政权中，大多数政变图谋都被情报部门掐灭在萌芽里、从未成事，因为组织一场政变通常需要拉拢的精英人数之多，使保密极其困难。所以，只要那个威权体系还算有效，它就会察觉。但我认为最终发生的就是这么回事。当然，最后还有一点：我记得当时我们讨论过，有人觉得普里戈任算是全身而退了，因为他谈成了资产的交接或出售，政变后还活着。但我们这些长期跟踪俄罗斯的人当时就很清楚，他绝无可能在这场兵变后活下来。说到这里，我去用 Perplexity 查一下。

[7320s - 7440s]

Yevgeny Prigozhin died on August twenty third, twenty twenty three, aged sixty two, when his private jet crashed in Russia's Tver region while flying from Moscow to Saint Petersburg. Do you think he was killed? It exploded midair. It was very obvious that he was killed, and he wasn't killed alone. He was killed along with several senior commanders of Wagner. And to me, the only explanation is this plane exploded in midair on video. Um, it was clear that he was never going to survive this mutiny, um, and that very likely top folks involved in Wagner weren't going to survive it either. And he was killed very demonstratively as a demonstration to others. And so that outcome to me and many other folks who watch Russia for years was was not surprising. This is a fascinating chapter in the war. Like I mean, there was a period of time there that forty eight seventy two hours where it was it was unknown like what was going to happen. There was a real consideration that this this might be the end of the war. A funny aspect here is Prigozhin was one of his many companies, very wealthy, ran the Internet Research Agency, which is kind of the the bot farms in Russia. And Prigozhin and and Wagner was so popular at the time, as as Mike was talking about. One of the issues here was they were the ones being celebrated. If you go back to this time period around Bakhmut, the biggest, most significant battle of the war, likely today, nobody can tell you the other Russian units that were operating anywhere else. But people know Wagner. They know that name. They've probably seen pictures of Prigozhin. He was becoming famous. So when this march happened, you had these these bots. They didn't know which direction to go. Are they still on Team Wagner because Prigozhin's their guy and he's he's capturing all of these these cities? Or wait a minute, is he against Putin now? Should we sit this one out? And another interesting aspect here is this, in my opinion, was something that Russia was doing very well.

【译】（查询结果）叶夫根尼·普里戈任于 2023 年 8 月 23 日去世，终年 62 岁；他的私人飞机在从莫斯科飞往圣彼得堡途中，坠毁于俄罗斯特维尔州。——你认为他是被杀的吗？——飞机在半空中爆炸。很明显他是被杀的，而且被杀的不止他一个：几名瓦格纳高级指挥官与他一同丧命。在我看来，唯一的解释就是：这架飞机在半空中爆炸，而且有视频为证。很清楚，他本来就不可能在这场兵变后幸存，瓦格纳的核心高层也很可能活不下来。他被杀得非常有"示范性"，是杀给其他人看的。所以对于我和许多观察俄罗斯多年的人来说，这个结局毫不意外。——这是战争中引人入胜的一章。我的意思是，有那么一段时间——大概四十八到七十二小时——完全不知道接下来会发生什么，人们确实认真考虑过这可能就是战争的终点。这里有个有趣的侧面：普里戈任名下有众多公司，非常富有，其中一家经营着"互联网研究机构"——也就是俄罗斯的那些机器人水军农场。而正如迈克刚才所说，普里戈任和瓦格纳当时人气极高。问题之一在于：被颂扬的正是他们。如果你回看巴赫穆特前后的那个时期——那很可能是迄今为止这场战争中规模最大、最重要的战役——没有人说得出在其他任何地方作战的其他俄军部队番号，但人们都知道瓦格纳，知道这个名字，多半还见过普里戈任的照片。他正在成名。所以进军发生时，那些水军机器人懵了，不知道该往哪个方向带节奏：是继续站队瓦格纳，因为普里戈任是自己人、正在攻占一座座城市？还是等一等——他现在是跟普京作对了？这一波要不要置身事外？另一个有意思的地方在于，在我看来，这（信息操纵）本是俄罗斯做得非常出色的一件事。

[7440s - 7560s]

And it almost worked out too well, and they didn't know how to kind of direct it in the right way. Wagner was a private military company, private military group, kind of. They were not really a part of the Russian government. This gave them this weird autonomy to operate in places like Syria. I mean, they went head to head. Wagner forces went head to head with U.S. forces in Syria. It went very badly for them. Did not go well for Russia. As I think it's twenty eighteen, might be February twenty eighteen, outside of Deir ez-Zor. Yeah, but it gave Russia this ability to have deniability. Say these. This is a private company. This isn't our military, but they effectively operate. I mean, they would fly in on Ministry of Defense aircraft. They would train at Ministry of Defense locations, and it was all things considered a pretty effective mechanism of state power that Russia had put together. They were pretty innovative in the war. the The concept of pulling these these convicts in, which I just interviewed a soldier that fought in Bakhmut, talked to him a couple weeks ago, and he talked about how exhausting it was that these guys just kept coming. He said one after the other, after the other, you couldn't you couldn't stop. They were innovative in that sense, but Wagner wasn't just prisoners. They had very lethal, well trained assault groups that were kind of leading the way in some of these new tactics. Because, in a sense, it was like this again privatized army. Prigozhin was competing to try to make them better, and I don't know the different directions that could have gone. Whether they branched out or became a a kind of elite assault force type thing, but they became too popular, and and Prigo and then Shoygu and Gerasimov didn't really know. It seems like there was a bit of a panic and just shut it shut it all down, and now it doesn't exist. They did. There's a the Africa Corps is operating in Africa. It's got some of the old Wagner leadership, but they effectively did away with all of that private military stuff, and said we got to bring this all in house. We can't do that again. But again.

【译】（接上）而它几乎运转得太成功了，以至于他们不知道该怎么把它引向正确的方向。瓦格纳是一家私营军事公司，算是私人军事团体，并不真正隶属于俄罗斯政府。这赋予了他们一种奇特的自主性，可以在叙利亚之类的地方活动。我是说，他们正面交过手——瓦格纳部队在叙利亚与美军正面交锋，结果对他们来说非常糟糕，对俄罗斯也没有好处。我想那是 2018 年，可能是 2018 年 2 月，在代尔祖尔城外。——对。但这给了俄罗斯"可否认性"：可以说，这是一家私人公司，不是我们的军队；但他们实际上照常运作——他们乘坐国防部的飞机飞进去，在国防部的场地上训练。综合来看，这是俄罗斯拼装出来的一套相当有效的国家权力工具。他们在战争中相当有创新性。比如把囚徒吸纳进来的这套思路——我前几周刚采访过一名在巴赫穆特作过战的士兵，他谈到那些人一波接一波地涌上来，令人筋疲力尽。他说，一个接一个、再接一个，你根本挡不住。在这个意义上他们是创新的。但瓦格纳不仅仅是囚犯：他们还有一些非常致命、训练有素的突击群，在某些新战术上走在前列。因为从某种意义上说，这又像是一支私有化的军队，普里戈任在竞争压力下设法把他们变得更好。我不知道这本可能走向哪些不同方向——是四处扩张，还是变成某种精锐突击部队之类的东西——但他们变得太有名了，于是普里戈任……随后绍伊古和格拉西莫夫其实也不知所措，看起来是有几分慌乱，干脆把它整个关停了，如今它已不复存在。——确实关停了。现在有"非洲军团"在非洲活动，里面有一些原瓦格纳的领导层，但他们实际上把私营军事那一套全部废掉了，说：这一切必须收归体制内，我们不能再让这种事重演。但话说回来——

[7560s - 7680s]

The interesting piece there is, I I do think Wagner was onto something. They were operating brutal, to be clear, incredibly brutal organization. I mean, there was a a awful video of one of their guys, I believe, surrendered, and they they got a hold of him, or he wouldn't go on an assault or something. They they smashed his head with a hammer against a cinder block, their own guy. So that's the group we're talking about, to be clear. But they they were effective, and Russia destroyed the organization. There's an effect that Wagner did have, and like referred to as the Wagnerization of the Russian military, which is what happens after Wagner is dismantled. The Russian military widely adopts Wagner tactics. It begins to contract convicts in very large numbers. It creates assault companies called Storm-Z and Storm-V. It begins to deploy assault companies of convicts structured as assault groups across the battalions and regular formations. It basically takes much of what made Wagner successful and begins to spread this across the Russian military, and then the Russian military begins to actually fight, having seen how Wagner fought, primarily with assault groups and assault detachments, using them as a constantly replenishable assault force that they throw at the Ukrainian forces. And for a period of time, believe it or not, in in 23 and 24, many of these assault units are convict convict staff, and many others are not convict staff, but people take special contracts that pay more, essentially taking on the risk of being an assault group. And so the Russian Ministry of Defense and the military actually begins to copy and imitate a lot of Wagner tactics. The Russian airborne also builds out assault groups and assault detachments, and following this for.

【译】——有意思的地方在于，我确实认为瓦格纳摸到了某种门道。先说清楚：他们行事残忍，是一个极端残忍的组织。我是说，有过一段可怕的视频：他们自己的一个人，我记得是投降了被他们抓住，或者是不肯参加突击之类的，他们用铁锤把他的头砸向混凝土砌块——砸的是自己人。所以说清楚，我们谈论的就是这样一个组织。但他们确实是有效的，而俄罗斯摧毁了这个组织。瓦格纳确实留下了一种影响，也就是所谓的俄军"瓦格纳化"——这发生在瓦格纳被解散之后：俄军广泛采纳瓦格纳战术，开始以非常大的规模与囚徒签订合同，组建了名为"风暴-Z"和"风暴-V"的突击连，开始把由囚徒组成、按突击群编组的突击连部署到各营和正规部队之中。它基本上把造就瓦格纳成功的那套东西拿来，推广到整个俄军。此后，亲眼看过瓦格纳怎么打仗的俄军，开始主要依靠突击群和突击支队作战，把它们当作可以不断补充的突击力量投向乌军。而且信不信由你，在 2023 和 2024 年的一段时间里，这些突击部队许多是囚徒成分；还有许多虽然不是囚徒，但人们签下薪酬更高的特殊合同，实质上就是承担充当突击群的风险。于是，俄罗斯国防部和军方实际上开始复制和模仿大量瓦格纳战术。俄空降兵也组建了突击群和突击支队。沿着这条脉络往后看——

[7680s - 7800s]

For years, do you see the Russian military actually fighting primarily with these assault formations? And over time, now you have fewer and fewer convicts. That groups, but nonetheless, thousands upon thousands of convicts were used by the Russian forces in assault units after Wagner. Prigozhin said that he lost killed killed in action twenty thousand people in just the battle of Bakhmut, which lasted somewhere I think from August twenty twenty two to May of twenty three. But the Russian military lost far more over the course of the years in terms of convicts they used. So I just want to put this postscript: that Wagner's organization may have died, but it it in some ways infected the Russian military, and these tactics spread across the Russian forces, which both led to some success, but also led the Russian military to over-specialize in using assault groups, which eventually led to diminishing returns. They specialized in small-scale combat action, and over time struggled to recover the ability to conduct larger-scale, more complex operations by focusing on fighting this way. I should mention for the listeners, for the audience, that is now past midnight, which is the only time we could do it. So there's two people in front of me; they're real warriors and just powering through. I really appreciate it. But quick bathroom break. All right, we're back, trying to power through. This might be a good moment to ask about the the very top, the leadership, the two commanders in chief, Vladimir Putin and Volodymyr Zelensky. What are, from a military perspective, the strengths and weaknesses of the two in terms of their role as commander in chief of their respective militaries? In my opinion, it appears as though Zelensky has offloaded a lot of the military responsibilities to the military, and he's not making tactical or operational level decisions. Generally speaking, that's a good thing. I don't think that that is happening in Russia. It's harder to say based.

【译】——多年以来，你是否看到俄军实际上主要就是靠这些突击编队作战？随着时间推移，如今囚徒成分越来越少，但尽管如此，在瓦格纳之后，俄军仍在突击部队中使用了成千上万的囚徒。普里戈任曾说，仅在巴赫穆特战役中他就损失了两万人阵亡——那场战役我记得大约从 2022 年 8 月持续到 2023 年 5 月。但以历年使用的囚徒总数而论，俄军的损失要大得多。所以我想补上这段后记：瓦格纳这个组织也许死了，但它在某种意义上"感染"了俄军，这些战术扩散到了俄军各部——这既带来了一些成功，也导致俄军在使用突击群上过度专精，最终收益递减。他们专门化于小规模战斗行动，久而久之，因为专注于这种打法，很难恢复实施更大规模、更复杂作战的能力。——我要向听众、向观众提一句：现在已经过了午夜，这是我们唯一能录制的时间。我面前这两位是真正的战士，硬撑着录完，我非常感激。稍作洗手间休息。——好，我们回来了，继续硬撑。这个节点或许适合聊聊最顶层——领导层，两位总司令：弗拉基米尔·普京和弗拉基米尔·泽连斯基。从军事角度看，两人作为各自军队的最高统帅，各有哪些长处与短板？——在我看来，泽连斯基似乎已把大量军事职责下放给军方，他不做战术或作战层面的决策。总体而言，这是好事。我不认为俄罗斯是这样的，不过基于现有报道比较难说……

[7800s - 7920s]

Off of how Putin is is reporting, at times it seems like he might be a little bit more involved than than Zelensky. But I get the impression that he's pretty removed from the military decision making process, which is is generally what you want to see. Historically, the more that a leader gets directly involved in that type of stuff, the the messier it becomes in either direction. In terms of the negatives, I would say well, they both have very different dynamics at play. Zelensky is is constantly appealing to an international audience, trying to solicit support from around the world. That's not really something that Putin has to do. So Putin much more focused on I'd say domestic and then kind of presenting a strong picture to the world that they're not on the verge of collapse or not on the verge of of of falling back anytime soon. Is Putin the same? Does he have any direct input into the military operation at the strategic, operational, or tactical level, as you understand? At strategic, I'm sure it does not look like from what I've seen that he has much input at the tactical or operational level. But I'd be interested in Mike's thoughts there as well. I think there was a period early on in 2022 when things were going very badly for the Russian military that he was actually contacting commanders, which are still very command senior commanders at the level of the grouping of troops, which is more the operational level formation above that of the combined arms army. But neither leader, in my view, really micromanages the fight. I think Zelensky is getting a much fairer, a more honest picture of the situation, has a much better assessment of Ukraine's position, and how Ukraine can best align its resources, can best advocate for resources with allies, and has more realistic goals. More importantly, more realistic goals for the military. If

【译】——基于关于普京的报道来看，有时他似乎比泽连斯基介入得略多一点。但我的印象是，他相当远离军事决策流程，而这通常正是你希望看到的。从历史上讲，领导人越是直接插手这类事务，局面就越是混乱，无论往哪个方向。至于短板，我想说，两人面对的态势非常不同。泽连斯基必须不断面向国际受众发声，争取全世界的支持；这其实不是普京需要做的事。所以普京更专注于——我想说——国内，同时向世界展示一种强硬的形象：俄罗斯没有濒临崩溃，短期内也不会溃退。——普京也是这样吗？据你了解，他是否在战略、作战或战术层面直接介入军事行动？——战略层面肯定有；但就我所见，他在战术或作战层面似乎没有太多介入。不过我也想听听迈克的看法。——我认为 2022 年早些时候有一段时期，俄军打得非常糟糕，他确实直接联系过指挥官——是高级指挥官，级别到了"军队集群"一级，也就是高于诸兵种合成集团军的作战层级的军团编成。但在我看来，两位领导人都不真正微观管理战事。我认为泽连斯基得到的情况汇报要公正、诚实得多，对乌克兰所处的位置有好得多的评估，更清楚乌克兰如何最合理地配置资源、如何最有效地向盟友争取资源，而且目标也更现实。更重要的是，给军方定的目标更现实。如果说——

[7920s - 8040s]

If you look at Putin, Russian political goals to me not only seem difficult to achieve, but for a long time I had argued that Russia has a fundamental mismatch in this war between its political goals and the military means available, and the fault of that lies squarely with Vladimir Putin because he started the war, and it's the job of political leadership to see. And certainly, if you can't see it four years in or five years in, should be asking hard questions of yourself. Can you speak to that mismatch? You can periodize this war in a number of ways, and and I've done so in my own writing. You can look at different operations, offensives, whatnot, or you can periodize it one big way, which is to say that you essentially have the first 30 days. This attempt to maintain that fails. The Russian attempt to take over Ukraine to replace Ukraine's government with one imposed by Russia, and this is really the contest for whether Ukraine will remain an independent state with its own national identity. And this invasion fails. This attempt fails decisively, and it cannot be recreated in the context of this war. And then everything else that follows, you know, and this I'm talking about from February 24 to March 25. Then everything else that follows is a war, you know, that is a a difficult, a tragic, prolonged conventional war defined by attrition, defined by exhaustion, and this war will define potentially the borders of the independent Ukrainian state. What will be the you know the economy? Does it work? And does the state will be economically sustainable? And whether or not Ukraine will ultimately have to make any concessions in terms of its strategic orientation, what organizations it can join or not join, like NATO or the European Union. But it's not a war to decide whether or not Ukraine will be independent or whether or not Ukraine will have its own national identity. That's that's already clear. That was the first 30 days.

【译】——再看普京：在我看来，俄罗斯的政治目标不仅难以实现，而且很久以来我一直在论证：这场战争中，俄罗斯的政治目标与可用的军事手段之间存在根本性错配，而这份责任完全在弗拉基米尔·普京身上，因为战争是他发动的，而看清这一点正是政治领导层的职责。而且可以肯定，如果打了四年、五年还看不清，就该扪心自问一些尖锐的问题了。——你能具体谈谈这种错配吗？——这场战争可以用多种方式划分阶段，我在自己的写作中就这么做过。你可以看不同的战役、攻势等等；也可以用一种大的划分法，即：头 30 天。那次企图——俄罗斯试图接管乌克兰、用一个由俄方强加的政府取代乌克兰政府——失败了。而这实质上是在争夺乌克兰能否保住一个拥有自身民族认同的独立国家地位。这次入侵失败了，这一企图决定性地失败了，而且在这场战争的框架内再也无法重来。此后的其他一切——我说的是从 2 月 24 日到 3 月 25 日——此后的一切都是另一场战争：一场艰难、悲惨、旷日持久的常规战争，以消耗战为定义，以疲惫为定义。这场战争将决定这个独立的乌克兰国家潜在的边界；经济会怎样？能不能运转？这个国家在经济上是否可持续？乌克兰最终是否不得不在其战略导向上作出让步——能加入或不能加入哪些组织，比如北约或欧盟。但它已不是一场决定乌克兰是否独立、是否拥有自身民族认同的战争——那一点早已明了，那是头 30 天就见分晓的。

[8040s - 8160s]

And so, to me, the war is in part Russia's attempt to impose its will on Ukraine. But it's become eminently clear for some time that Russia does not have the military means to achieve Putin's more maximalist objectives in this conflict. And if anything, in the past year, even if you don't agree with this assessment for the early years of this war, in the past year, it's become eminently clear that Russia is not coming closer to its goals. In fact, its military performance is getting worse, and that's been going on for some time. I would say the inflection point in this war for Russia certainly on the tactical level was back in summer fall of twenty twenty five, and so you can see that Ukraine had actually narrowed its position in negotiations fairly closely to its combat performance. Ukrainian political demands are ceasefire along the line of control, repatriation of lost people and stolen children. But it's not these outsized demands that Russian political leadership is making. You see, Russian demands is not being tied to Russian military performance. They seem much more tied to aspirational performance that Russia wished it had in this war, but actually doesn't. That's why they want Ukraine to concede the rest of Donbas because they're not able to take it, and they're not on a path to taking anytime soon. So even if we assess this war for most minimalist territorial war aims of Russia prioritizing the occupation of the rest of the Donbas, the last remaining some twenty plus percent, we have no way to even predict if they if they are able to take it in this war, given how poorly the Russian militaries perform right now. And so here I am, kind of making a judgment because if you see a country prosecuting a war with a clear mismatch between military means available and the political goals of the war, and the leader of the

【译】因此在我看来，这场战争部分上是俄罗斯试图把自身意志强加于乌克兰。但一段时间以来已极其清楚：俄罗斯并不具备实现普京在这场冲突中更最大化目标的军事手段。而且，即便你不认同我对战争前几年的这种评估，仅在过去一年，情况也已昭然若揭：俄罗斯并没有更接近自己的目标；事实上，它的军事表现还在恶化，而且已经持续了一段时间。我要说，这场战争对俄罗斯而言的拐点——至少在战术层面——是在 2025 年夏秋之交。由此你可以看到，乌克兰实际上已把谈判立场收窄到与其战场表现相当接近的程度：乌克兰的政治要求是沿实际控制线停火、遣返被掳走的人员和被偷走的孩子；而这可不是俄方政治领导层提出的那种漫天要价。你看，俄罗斯的要价并没有与其军事表现挂钩，倒更像是挂靠在他们幻想中的表现上——俄罗斯希望自己在这场战争中拥有的表现，而实际上并不具备。这就是为什么他们要乌克兰让出顿巴斯其余部分：因为他们拿不下来，也不在任何即将拿下的路径上。所以，即便我们以俄罗斯最克制版图目标的口径来评估这场战争——优先占领顿巴斯其余部分，即剩下的那约百分之二十几——以俄军目前这种糟糕的表现，我们也完全无法预测他们在这场战争中能否拿下来。所以我现在是在下一个判断，因为如果你看到一个国家在打一场军事手段与政治目标明显错配的战争，而这个国家的领导人——

[8160s - 8280s]

That country unwilling to revise down the political aims, willing to pay costs that would be ruinous for, for Russia. I mean, this war will be seen as a strategic failure for Russia with generational knock-on effects. Whatever Russia takes will not be worth the cost. That's very clear at this point. It's very difficult to see how Vladimir Putin goes down in history from this war, other than you know enigma. And then, even this many years into it, you now clearly see that Russia is not attaining its military objectives on any reasonable timelines. Is not revising the negotiating position, and there's not been a dramatic shift in how Russia's forces are fighting the war either. Can you linger on what Vladimir Putin's maximalist objectives might be? Which territories included in those maximalist objectives? Donbas, absolutely Donbas, as much of Kharkiv, Zaporizhzhia, and Dnipro Oblast as they could potentially occupy. Because you don't see Russian forces advancing Donbas; you see them advancing on a broad 1,200-kilometer front. Donbas may be the priority effort, but there's ongoing offensive operations by Russian troops for a long time. They were taking territory where they could, and so they were actually advancing more in Zaporizhzhia and Dnipro Oblast last year because it was easier to advance there, less urban terrain, fewer of the Ukrainian armies, better troops there, since the rest were focused on defending the Donbas, and also obviously seeking national concessions, right? Limits on the size of Ukrainian forces, limits on what kind of capabilities Ukraine can have, limits on Ukraine's strategic orientation or ability to join NATO, limits on whether or not Western forces can ever be deployed or based in Ukraine, other national concessions dealing with the status of Russian language, maybe the status of the Russian Orthodox Church, and so on, so forth. Those are still there, you know.

【译】——又不愿下调政治目标，还甘愿付出对俄罗斯而言毁灭性的代价。我的意思是，这场战争将被视为俄罗斯的战略失败，并带来代际级的连锁后果。无论俄罗斯占到什么，都不足以抵偿代价——在当下这一点非常清楚。很难想象弗拉基米尔·普京会因为这场战争在历史上留下什么形象，除了一个谜。而且即便打了这么多年，你现在清楚地看到：俄罗斯没有在任何合理的时间表上实现其军事目标，没有修正谈判立场，俄军的作战方式也没有发生重大转变。——能展开谈谈弗拉基米尔·普京的最大化目标可能是什么吗？这些最大化目标里包括哪些领土？——顿巴斯，绝对是顿巴斯，还有哈尔科夫、扎波罗热和第聂伯罗各州尽可能多的、他们有潜在可能占领的部分。因为你并没有看到俄军在顿巴斯推进；你看到的是他们在一条宽达 1200 公里的战线上推进。顿巴斯或许是主要努力方向，但俄军长期以来一直在多处实施进攻作战。他们能在哪里占地就在哪里占地，所以去年他们实际在扎波罗热州和第聂伯罗州推进得更多，因为那里更容易推进：城市地形更少，乌克兰的精锐部队在那里更少——因为其余部队都专注于防守顿巴斯。此外显然还有各种对乌国家性让步的要求，对吧？限制乌克兰军队的规模，限制乌克兰可以拥有何种能力，限制乌克兰的战略导向或加入北约的可能，限制西方部队能否在乌克兰部署或驻扎，还有其他涉及俄语地位、也许还有俄罗斯东正教会地位之类的国家性让步，等等。这些要求至今还在，你知道的。

[8280s - 8400s]

Amongst Russian demands negotiations, and also seeking recognition. To what extent they can get recognition for territory that they occupied and annexed? So, the reason I say all this is, I think it is a fair criticism. How Preston sees this matter, I think that Ukrainian goals in this war are not only far more reasonable, but will construct at least tied to what the military can achieve. There's an observable strategy that integrates military operations, stabilizing the front line, and liberating territory, trying to protect critical infrastructure, air and missile defense, to to reduce the impact of Russia's strike campaign, and prevent the Russian leadership from thinking that they have the ability to somehow bomb Ukraine into conceding these demands. And the third one is exhausting the Russian effort by striking the key components of the Russian economy, energy export, refining capacity, other targets. Now, more late lately, we have data centers, but essentially things that are relevant to the functioning of the Russian economy or the Russian government's ability to raise revenue. So, is it fair to say that wars come to an end when both sides' their demands match their military performance? To put it simply, it's when both sides see that negotiations and the negotiated settlement is a better alternative to fighting and what they can hope to expect to gain on the battlefield. I think that's a a good way of putting it. When military capabilities match what the the political concessions are looking like, and I think that's exactly why a lot of folks are not very optimistic about an end to this war anytime.

【译】——在俄方的谈判要价之中，还包括寻求承认：他们能在多大程度上让他们占领并吞并的领土获得承认？我之所以说这些，是因为我认为这是一个公允的批评。——普雷斯顿怎么看这个问题？——我认为，乌克兰在这场战争中的目标不仅合理得多，而且至少是与其军队所能达成的成果挂钩的。乌克兰有一条看得见的战略，把多个要素整合在一起：军事行动——稳定战线、收复领土；尽力保护关键基础设施，通过防空与反导降低俄军打击战役的影响，防止俄罗斯领导层以为自己有能力把乌克兰炸到屈服、接受这些要求；第三条是通过打击俄罗斯经济的关键组成部分来耗竭俄罗斯的战争努力——能源出口、炼油能力和其他目标，近来还包括数据中心——总之是与俄罗斯经济运转、与俄罗斯政府筹资能力切实相关的目标。——那可不可以说，当双方的要价与各自的军事表现相匹配时，战争就走到了终点？——简单说，就是当双方都看到：谈判与谈判达成的和解，优于继续打下去、优于他们能指望在战场上获得的东西。我认为这个说法很好——当军事能力与政治让步的幅度相匹配时。而我认为，这正是许多人对这场战争短期内结束不太乐观的原因。

[8400s - 8520s]

Soon, because as Mike mentioned, the demands from Russia are largely unchanged from the beginning of the war. Just this past week, Putin talked about how Zelensky did not have the authority to talk about the future of Zaporizhia and Kherson and Donetsk and Luhansk. He said those are historic Russian lands, so it's not even up to Zelensky to talk about territorial concessions. That's where Russia's mindset is, or Putin specifically, where his mindset is in the war is the territory that they haven't taken yet isn't even up for discussion. That's a foregone conclusion that they will own that, and as Mike mentioned, they are so far from taking that territory right now. I mean, part of that they would have to cross the Dnieper River. There's there's no one in the world that looks at this right now and thinks that Russia is anywhere near crossing a water feature to to take Kherson City and the rest of that oblast, let alone the little portions of terrain in Luhansk and and Donetsk. So, given that if there's a mismatch in demands and military performance, there's other factors, forces that come into play, like the state of the economy, the will to fight amongst the people, like very simply manpower. As you've spoken about, like in order to fight a war, you need humans to be doing that fighting. So, how does that come into play? How long can Ukraine last in this fight? How long can Russia last in this fight? Admittedly, the economy. I thought that the sanctions would have done a lot more damage at this point. I think a lot of people did, and it just hasn't. Russia has found a lot of ways to bypass that. I thought the Russian people would have been frustrated long ago with the the change in their status of living. That didn't materialize, so there's no doubt that the Russian economy is feeling some pressure to the extent that that will impact the end of the war or could drive the end of the war. It's a bit of a black box from my perspective. The other issue that that Russia is playing with right now is manpower. Look, overall, Russia has the manpower advantage.

【译】——短期内不乐观，因为正如迈克所说，俄罗斯的要价从战争一开始至今基本没变。就在过去这一周，普京还谈到泽连斯基无权谈论扎波罗热、赫尔松、顿涅茨克和卢甘斯克的未来。他说那些是历史上属于俄罗斯的土地，所以连领土让步都轮不到泽连斯基来谈。这就是俄罗斯的心态所在——具体说是普京的心态：在这场战争里，他们还没拿到的那部分领土甚至不在讨论范围之内，那些终将归他们所有，是板上钉钉的事。而正如迈克所说，他们现在离拿下那些领土还差得很远。我是说，其中一部分，他们还得渡过第聂伯河。当今世界上没有任何人会看一眼现状就认为俄罗斯接近于跨过这道水障去夺取赫尔松市及该州其余部分，更别提卢甘斯克和顿涅茨克的那些小块地域了。——那么，既然要价与军事表现之间存在错配，就还有其他因素、其他力量会起作用：经济状况、民众的战斗意志，还有最简单的人力。正如你说过的，要打仗，就得有人去打。那么这些因素如何起作用？乌克兰还能撑多久？俄罗斯还能撑多久？——先说经济。坦白讲，我原以为制裁到这个时候已经造成大得多的伤害。我想很多人当初都这么以为，但并没有。俄罗斯找到了很多绕过制裁的办法。我原以为俄罗斯民众早就该对生活水准的变化感到不满了，结果这也没有成为现实。毫无疑问，俄罗斯经济正感受到一定压力，但压力会不会影响战争结局、能不能推动战争结束，在我看来有点像黑箱。俄罗斯现在手里玩的另一个问题是人力。听着，总体而言，俄罗斯握有人力优势。

[8520s - 8640s]

It's not close. They have more people than Ukraine, and it's not close. Ukraine has done a good job of while they are mobilizing and conscripting people into the force, they are doing what they can to minimize soldiers at the front in dangerous positions. They're handing a lot of this off to to robots when possible. Russia is continuing to push men forward, trying to take terrain, not with robots and not with drones, but with soldiers, and that's costing a lot of lives. Russia, in certain regions, has started to really struggle in filling their recruitment demands, and we're seeing what. In fact, I think Mike wrote a piece about this recently, but there was kind of a shadow mobilization, if you will, inside of Russia where they're starting to coerce more and more people into joining the military. Again, they have far more people, but what does it look like when you start having to pull in more people that don't want to fight? Ukraine has already had to deal with that. They've already had to go through a number of people, a number of different groups within society that maybe didn't volunteer right out the gate. Or I just read an article earlier today about a soldier who now is doing an incredible job in the Ukrainian military. They grabbed him at the border; he was trying to leave the country, and now he's kind of found a purpose as a as a drone mechanic. Ukraine has done a lot of that. Russia is starting to get into that, and that's a big unknown: is how will the country react when they start having to tap into other portions of the population? I'm not overly optimistic because we've seen a number of things at this point that I thought would have pushed the Russian population over the edge. The casualties, for one, shocking. At about the three month mark, I thought that that would be enough for the people in Moscow to rise up and say we're not going to do this. And my God, here we are, thirty plus thousand casualties a month, and there's just there's no one in sight. So I'm not optimistic that should there be a mobilization or a push to pull in more people that have not been fighting, that it'll change anything drastic.

【译】而且优势还不小。他们的人口比乌克兰多，而且差距不小。乌克兰做得不错的一点是：尽管也在动员、征召人员入伍，他们在尽一切可能减少处在前线危险位置上的士兵，只要有可能，就把这类任务交给机器人。而俄罗斯仍在不断把人往前推，靠士兵——而不是机器人、不是无人机——去夺取土地，这正在付出大量生命代价。俄罗斯在某些地区已经开始真正难以满足征兵指标，我们正看到——事实上我想迈克最近就写过一篇关于这个的文章——俄罗斯内部出现了一种可称之为"影子动员"的做法：开始胁迫越来越多的人参军。再强调一次，他们人口远更多，但当你不得不拉进越来越多不想打仗的人时，局面会是什么样？乌克兰已经不得不应对过这个问题：他们已经经历过若干批人、社会中若干原本未必第一时间志愿入伍的群体。我今天早些时候刚读到一篇文章，讲一名如今在乌军中表现出色的士兵——当初他在边境被抓，当时正试图离境，而现在他当无人机机械师，算是找到了自己的使命。乌克兰做了大量这样的事。俄罗斯正开始走到这一步，而这是个大大的未知数：当他们不得不动用人口中的其他部分时，这个国家会作何反应？我不算乐观，因为到目前为止我们已经见过许多我以为会把俄罗斯民众逼到临界点的事情。伤亡就是一例，触目惊心。大概在开战三个月的时候，我以为那样的伤亡足以让莫斯科的民众起来说：我们不干了。结果天哪，如今已经到每月三万多人伤亡的程度，却还是看不到任何动静。所以我不乐观：即便出现动员、或开始征召此前从未参战的人群，也不会改变什么大局。

[8640s - 8760s]

Inside of Russia, but that dynamic is changing. I think, from a resource perspective, both of these are fairly large countries, and both of them have considerable external support. Ukraine has demonstrated immense resolve and will to fight, but clear without Western support, Western capital, Western military capabilities, it would not been able to sustain the war for this long. Russia too has benefited significantly from China. Chinese economy access to machine tools, components to China, to a lesser extent, North Korea, and much lesser extent Iran. But North Korea shipped so much. You know, one point in the war, North Korea was providing forty percent of Russian artillery ammunition in this war, and shipping artillery. And actually, North Korean troops, if you remember, fought on the Russian side in Kursk during the battle for Kursk. So both sides benefit considerably from external support. That, and this, what enables Russia to continue the war. I think, from an economic point of view, it's difficult to predict. It's clear Russia has the resources to continue to fight into twenty twenty seven. But key economic elites in Russia, early this year in the spring, began to publicly state that this war and this level of government spending is not sustainable. Russia is spending forty four percent of the government budget on the war, and maybe somewhere around nine to ten percent of GDP. The Russian economy has structural issues, and so much of the money is going towards the military effort and production of military goods, which are not economically valuable. That the economy is slowly consuming itself, so they have issues. For Ukraine, the main challenge is that the war continues.

【译】——（剧变会发生）在俄罗斯国内。但这一态势正在变化。我认为，从资源角度看，这是两个相当大的国家，而且都得到可观的外部支持。乌克兰展现出了巨大的决心与战斗意志，但很清楚：没有西方的支持、西方资本、西方军事能力，它不可能把战争维持这么久。俄罗斯同样显著受益于中国——得以进入中国经济、获取中国的机床和零部件；较小程度上受益于朝鲜，更小程度上受益于伊朗。但朝鲜发货量大得惊人：战争中的某个时点，朝鲜提供了俄军在这场战争中所用炮弹的百分之四十，还在运送火炮。而且如果你记得的话，在库尔斯克战役期间，朝鲜部队实际上在库尔斯克为俄方一侧作战。所以双方都从外部支持中获益颇多。这一点，再加上以下这点——是什么让俄罗斯能够把战争打下去：从经济角度看，很难预测。清楚的是，俄罗斯拥有把战争打到 2027 年的资源。但俄罗斯的关键经济精英今年早些时候、也就是春天，开始公开表示：这场战争和这种水平的政府支出不可持续。俄罗斯把政府预算的百分之四十四花在战争上，大约占 GDP 的九到十个百分点。俄罗斯经济存在结构性问题：如此多的钱流向军事努力和军用产品生产，而这些产品没有经济价值，以至于经济在慢慢消耗自身。所以他们是有问题的。至于乌克兰，主要挑战在于战争还在继续——

[8760s - 8880s]

To damage the Ukrainian economy and also drive people from the country, since a big part of the Russian military campaign is to try to hurt the economic viability of Ukraine and sustainability of life in Ukrainian cities. And so, even though Ukraine is doing well on the battlefield, continuing the war naturally comes a cost. I don't know any country that hosts a conventional war in its territory that was the better off for it. It's very clear. And Ukraine's main challenge is it depends so heavily on financial support, military support, intelligence support from the West as well. That these are things have to constantly be renewed, and increasingly, you also see that Ukraine is subject to some extent to the mercurial nature or capricious nature of policy of of some countries, like the United States. That we should we should speak frankly about it, right? There's there's genuine concern, I think, in Ukraine that folks here want to do business with Russia, and they want to do it no matter the implications for the allies, and no matter the implications for Ukraine and for this critical conflict that's going to shape future security in Europe. How long can Ukraine last without U.S. support? We should mention that there's no direct financial aid, as I understand from the United States, but there is, like you mentioned, intelligence and otherwise actual ammunition and weapons. The reality is the United States still provides considerable military equipment, ammunition, other things Ukraine needs. Just Europeans are paying for it. Okay, the United States is also offering quite a bit again through foreign military sales. Europeans are paying for that. There's a fair amount of intelligence support that I can't speak to, but you should consider it probably still important to the effort. So.

【译】——持续损害乌克兰经济，也持续把人赶出这个国家，因为俄军战役很大一部分就是试图打击乌克兰的经济生存能力、打击乌克兰城市生活的可持续性。所以，即便乌克兰在战场上表现不错，战争延续自然有代价。我不知道有哪个在本国领土上经历常规战争的国家因此过得更好——这一点非常清楚。而乌克兰的主要挑战在于：它在财政支持、军事支持乃至情报支持上都极度依赖西方，而这些支持必须不断续期。而且你还日益看到，乌克兰在一定程度上受制于某些国家政策的反复无常、朝令夕改，比如美国。这一点我们应该坦率地讲，对吧？我认为乌克兰确实存在真实的担忧：这边有些人想和俄罗斯做生意，而且不顾对盟友的影响、不顾对乌克兰的影响、不顾对这场将塑造欧洲未来安全的关键冲突的影响，非要做这生意不可。——没有美国支持，乌克兰能撑多久？——我们应该说明一点：据我理解，美国现在没有直接的财政援助，但正如你提到的，有情报支持，还有实际的弹药和武器。现实是，美国仍在提供相当多的军事装备、弹药和乌克兰需要的其他东西，只是由欧洲人出钱。好。美国还通过对外军售再提供不少，也是欧洲人付账。还有相当数量的情报支持，具体情况我不能谈，但应该认为它对战争努力大概仍然重要。所以——

[8880s - 9000s]

Ukraine may not be dependent nearly on the United States as it was earlier in the war. Where we are providing not only a lot of the the funding, but artillery ammunition, equipment, what have you, but in key areas, it does still depend on the United States or depend on the United States. Certainly not to try to hamper Ukraine's military effort, where the U.S. still has considerable power and influence. If you can evaluate what has Trump done as our commander in chief in the context of helping bring this war to a resolution, to be blunt, I don't know that there's a lot that's been, from my opinion, that's been good so far. I think we have ceded our position in the world was looking to us, and I think still will in a lot of ways. Should we take that mantle back up? I think the world is looking to us to take lead to take point. That doesn't necessarily mean that we're shipping the most equipment or we're spending the most money, but kind of leading the process and what this will look like going forward. And I think we stepped away from that early in the administration. I get the impression that maybe the president thought this would be easier to resolve, and it has been. Maybe lost some focus. We've lost leverage over Ukraine as well, which is kind of a cold thing to say. But when we were providing weapons to Ukraine, we had the ability to kind of you know navigate the the peace process with more power, more leverage. We did away with that. That hurt Ukraine, but it also gave us less ability to kind of dictate the direction for things to go. We've not seen the sanctions layered on Russia that I think could have been, and unfortunately, I think there's been just some doubt thrown on the the structure of the NATO alliance. Will the alliance hold? And I think there's probably just enough there for Putin to irrationally, but possibly still think that it would be worthwhile to poke and prod to see if there's an opening there. So, personally, I don't think there's been a lot of good. I think we've kicked the can down the road. I think we.

【译】——乌克兰对美国的依赖可能已经不像战争早期那么深。早期我们不仅提供大量资金，还提供炮弹、装备等等；但在若干关键领域，它仍然依赖美国。当然还有另一层：美国仍拥有相当大的权力和影响力，至少可以不去阻碍乌克兰的军事努力。——你能否评估一下，特朗普作为我们的最高统帅，在帮助推动这场战争走向解决方面做得如何？——坦率地说，依我之见，到目前为止没看到多少做得好的地方。我认为我们让出了自己的位置——世界曾在看着我们，而且我认为在很多方面仍然在看着我们。我们该不该把那副担子重新挑起来？我认为世界期待我们牵头、打头阵。这不一定意味着我们运送最多的装备、花最多的钱，而是主导这个进程、主导事情往后的发展方向。而我认为本届政府上任之初就从这个角色上退开了。我的印象是，总统也许以为这件事会更容易解决，而事实证明并非如此，也许因此失去了一些专注。我们也失去了对乌克兰的筹码——这话有点冷酷，但当我们向乌克兰提供武器时，我们有能力以更大的权力、更大的筹码去驾驭和平进程。我们把这条路自己堵死了。那伤害了乌克兰，也削弱了我们为事情定方向的能力。我们没有看到本可以层层加码到俄罗斯身上的制裁，而且不幸的是，我认为北约联盟的架构被投下了一些疑云：联盟还靠得住吗？而我认为，那里面的裂缝也许刚好够让普京——不理性地、但或许仍然——觉得值得去戳一戳、探一探，看有没有可乘之机。所以就我个人而言，我认为没多少好的方面，我们是在把问题往后踢。我认为我们——

[9000s - 9120s]

We've seen we have we've opened the door for Europe to take lead here. In some ways, they've started to. There's still a lot to be done, but I'm I'm not overly optimistic. I think we've we've made some mistakes here in ways that not just difficult for Ukraine, but I think for the United States. The engagement with it has been pretty fitful, Lex. They they seem to be coming back to it every three four months. We're heavily distracted by the Iran War, and it almost seems that the Trump administration had first settled on trying to coerce Ukraine, seeing Ukraine as the problem, then began to recognize that Russia is the problem, but doing nothing to compel Russia to change its negotiating position. The meeting in Anchorage, Alaska, seemed to begin to bring Russia out of the isolation that we and other Western countries had agreed on, and we can see the knock-on effects of that. And then, more recently, we had gone back to trying to compel Ukraine once again, and and I just don't think that the way we've engaged this process. Is the right approach? I agree with Preston. The only thing I might say, I think there needs to be a balance to all this. The only positive thing I would say is that even if it was probably for the wrong reasons, prioritizing attaining an end to the war and pushing all the parties involved towards making clear what their requirements are to end the war and some kind of process and essentially an alignment of positions on that topic was helpful because the previous administration had no plan. As long as it takes is a slogan, not a plan. If anyone knew what the Biden administration's plan was to either win the war or end the war, I don't know. And our European.

【译】——我们已经为欧洲敞开了让它牵头的大门。在某些方面，欧洲已经开始这么做了。还有很多要做的，但我谈不上乐观。我认为我们犯了一些错误，其代价不仅让乌克兰难受，我认为也让美国难受。——对这场战争的介入一直是断断续续的，莱克斯。他们似乎每隔三四个月才回来管一次。我们被伊朗战争严重分心，而且特朗普政府几乎是先认定要胁迫乌克兰、把乌克兰当成问题所在，之后才开始认识到俄罗斯才是问题，却不采取任何行动迫使俄罗斯改变谈判立场。阿拉斯加安克雷奇的会晤似乎开始把俄罗斯从我们和其他西方国家商定的孤立中拉了出来，其连锁效应我们如今看得到。而最近，我们又回到了再次施压乌克兰的老路上。我只是不认为我们介入这个进程的方式是正确的路径。——我同意普雷斯顿。我唯一想说的是，这一切需要有个平衡。我能说出的唯一一点积极之处是：即便出发点可能是错的，但把"达成战争终结"列为优先事项，推动各方明确说出结束战争的必要条件、建立某种进程、并在这个议题上实质上统一各方立场，这本身是有益的——因为上届政府根本没有计划。"需要多久就支持多久"是一句口号，不是计划。如果有人知道拜登政府赢得战争或结束战争的计划是什么，我不知道。而我们的欧洲——

[9120s - 9240s]

Allies, well, they had the opportunity for quite a few years to come up with some kind of plan or negotiating process, and they did not. Their their consensus of role was to support Ukraine; that was the common denominator, and to sustain the war. But given that this is a war principally fought in Europe, they too did not take lead on a peace plan for those years, until the Trump administration showed up. And I'm, I'm using this as an opportunity, I think, for some constructive and hopefully polite criticism, that of course, ideally, you should have an alignment of positions between the United States, and European allies in managing European security. Okay. However, it is not as though the Trump administration came into office and European allies had a plan or vision, and the Trump administration had a worse one or threw that plan and vision out. So, then European allies were forced to play catch up and maintain relevance and whatnot. Now, all that being said, I think the Trump administration's approach, overall, has not been a good one. They haven't done a great job consulting the key eyes involved in this. They've been capricious and and anyways unpredictable in their behavior, both towards allies and towards Ukraine. Um, it's clear that parts of the administration wants to normalize relations and do business with Russia and sees the whole war as kind of an impediment to that. So their motivations, I think, objectively or some extent suspect. And the way they've engaged with it has been kind of from a let's make a deal without looking at does this deal make sense and is this going to be a lasting agreement or is it just going to be a piece of paper with a word ceasefire on it that won't frankly will not be worth the paper it's on. So there are real issues you can objectively take with the way negotiations been handled. One thing I would add there is well.

【译】——盟友们呢？他们有好几年的时间可以拿出某种计划或谈判进程，但他们没有。他们共识中的角色就是支持乌克兰——这是最大公约数——以及维持战争。但考虑到这主要是一场在欧洲打的战争，那些年里他们同样没有在和平计划上牵头，直到特朗普政府上台。我把这当作一个机会，提出一些建设性的、但愿也算礼貌的批评：当然，理想状态下，美国与欧洲盟友在管理欧洲安全上应该立场一致。好。然而，现实并不是说特朗普政府一上台、把欧洲盟友已有的计划或愿景推翻、换成一个更糟的，从而逼得欧洲盟友手忙脚乱去追赶、去维持存在感——情况不是这样。话虽如此，我认为特朗普政府的做法总体上并不好。他们没有做好与关键盟友的磋商；他们对盟友、对乌克兰的行为反复无常、总之难以预测。而且很清楚，政府内部有一部分人想要与俄罗斯关系正常化、与俄罗斯做生意，把整场战争视为这件事的绊脚石。所以我认为，他们的动机客观上、在某种程度上是可疑的。他们介入的方式基本上是"咱们做个交易吧"，却不问这笔交易是否讲得通、这是否会是一份持久的协议，还是只是一张写着"停火"二字的纸——坦率说，连那张纸都不值。所以，对谈判的处理方式，确实存在可以客观指摘的实际问题。——我想补充的一点是——

[9240s - 9360s]

Is I think it's important to look at all of this. We're Americans. It's important to look at this through the American lens, and and it's not just a matter of were these decisions good or bad for Ukraine, because at the end of the day, we should be thinking about are they good or bad for the United States and our interests. Ukraine has raised their hand a couple times and said, "How can we help?" Especially when it came to the war in Iran and some of these interceptors, there is so much opportunity there in terms of collaboration, joint production, standing up facilities. We don't have to give money to Ukraine. We don't have to give equipment to Ukraine, but there is a mutually beneficial relationship to be had there, where we can help them produce, and maybe we get some rights for development because we need a lot of that stuff too. And I just feel like again, I think the best way to put is we've left a lot on the table. And certainly not used our leverage, and have been very, very resistant to using any of it to compel Russia. The approach has been unhelpful and hasn't really used the power in the role we have. And I think part of the problem was how we started because when when this began, as an I found Trump illustration, for some reason they decided to immediately maneuver the United States out of the leading role of a coalition supporting Ukraine, trying to achieve a favorable outcome in this war, to a neutral party who will now referee and arbitrate the outcome and try to make a deal, pretending as though the previous history of how this war began, a whole role, and it didn't happen. Like, and it was a very strange approach to take because it gave up our influence and leverage over both our European allies, who didn't trust our intentions, that could see that we basically were no longer on the same team, and our partner in Ukraine, who immediately began to think that we were about to sell them out because we turned around and abandoned our role, which was critical to supporting them, into one that now placed them on the equal footing with Russia. Which, to be clear, Russia even today laughs at the idea that we are trying to be this neutral party. They do not view us that way. They view us as as firmly in Ukraine.

【译】——我认为重要的是怎么看这一切。我们是美国人，重要的是透过美国的视角来看，而这不只是"这些决定对乌克兰是好是坏"的问题，因为归根结底，我们该想的是它们对美国、对我们的利益是好是坏。乌克兰有好几次主动举手说："我们能帮上什么忙？"尤其是在伊朗战争和那些拦截弹的问题上，那里有大量的合作机会：协作、联合生产、共建设施。我们不必给乌克兰钱，也不必给乌克兰装备，但那里完全存在一种互利关系：我们可以帮他们生产，也许我们还换来一些研发权益，因为那些东西我们自己也非常需要。而我再次感觉——我想最好的说法就是：我们把太多东西留在了桌面上没有拿。我们当然没有动用自己的筹码，而且一直非常、非常抗拒动用任何筹码去迫使俄罗斯让步。这种做法于事无补，没有真正运用我们所处角色所赋予的权力。而且我认为，问题的一部分出在我们起步的方式上：这一切开始时，作为特朗普政府，他们不知为何决定立刻把美国从"支持乌克兰、争取有利结局的联盟领导者"的位置上挪开，变成一个中立第三方，来给结局当裁判、做仲裁、撮合交易，还假装这场战争如何开端的那段历史——整个历史——不曾发生过。这是一个非常奇怪的路径选择，因为它放弃了我们对两边的势力和筹码：一边是欧洲盟友——他们不信任我们的意图，看得出我们基本上已不再与他们是同一队；另一边是我们的伙伴乌克兰——他们立即开始认为我们就要把他们出卖了，因为我们转身抛弃了对他们的存续至关重要的支持者角色，把自己换成了把乌克兰与俄罗斯置于同等地位的角色。而要说清楚的是：直到今天，俄罗斯都在嘲笑我们试图充当这种中立方的想法。他们根本不这么看我们。他们把我们视为坚定地站在乌克兰一边的——

[9360s - 9480s]

Understandably so. So these moves to kind of separate—I understand on paper it may have been—well, now we're neutral and we're not directly aligned with one side or the other. That that didn't didn't go over in Russia. Didn't carry. At this late hour, this is the most challenging question. What are the possible paths to peace in the war in Ukraine? Can you speak to the different trajectories? What are the things you hope to see? I don't see a willingness on the Russian side right now to negotiate. There are negotiations taking place, and there's another push, another effort. Maybe some sort of partial compartmentalized ceasefire will be achieved. For example, a ceasefire on energy strikes between the two sides, or a ceasefire halting strikes against shipping in the Black Sea, which have escalated this year. I think that's possible. I think the problem we have is that the Russian position remains that fighting's their preferred alternative to negotiation and to a revision of their current demands. And I think, from Putin's perspective, he is likely to wait to see the outcome of two things: the strike campaign over the course of the winter, right, and to see if that places Ukraine in a weaker bargaining position because Ukraine right now has considerable leverage. Ukraine's done much better this year at the front line than Russia, and Russia does not have great prospects for advancing along pretty much any part of the front. Can you explain why that is, and why you've already mentioned a couple of times that winter is a very difficult time for Ukraine? The winter is the hardest not for troops at the front line, is the hardest for the civilian population. But a country is not just the map or the front; it is the economy, it is the cities, the people. That's actually what you're fighting for, right? And and so that's who the winter affects the most. The second thing Putin may be waiting for is the outcome of certain elections.

【译】一方，这也完全可以理解。所以那些切割性的动作——我明白，纸面上或许像是"现在我们中立了，不再直接站在任何一方"——在俄罗斯那里行不通，没有买账。——到了这么晚的时刻，这是最难的一个问题：乌克兰战争可能的和平路径是什么？能谈谈不同的走向吗？你们希望看到的是什么？——我目前看不到俄方有谈判的意愿。谈判在进行，还有新一轮推动、新一轮努力，也许能达成某种局部的、分板块的停火。比如双方停止对能源设施的打击，或者停止对黑海航运的打击——这方面的袭击今年升级了。我认为这是可能的。我认为问题在于，俄方的立场依然是：打仗比谈判、比修改他们当前的要价更合他们的意。而且我认为，在普京看来，他很可能要等着看两件事的结果：一是整个冬季的打击战役，看它会不会把乌克兰逼到更弱的谈判位置，因为乌克兰现在握有相当的筹码——乌克兰今年在战线上的表现远好于俄罗斯，而俄罗斯在战线几乎任何地段都没有多大的推进前景。——你能解释一下这是为什么吗？还有，你已经几次提到冬天对乌克兰是非常艰难的时期。——冬天最难的不是前线部队，最难的是平民人口。因为一个国家不只是地图或前线，它是经济、是城市、是人民——那才是你真正为之而战的东西，对吧？所以冬天打击最重的正是这些人。普京可能在等的第二件事，是某些选举的结果——

[9480s - 9600s]

In Europe in the spring, like France's election, to see if the overall situation could be more favorable to Russia. Right now, I don't see any serious effort other than Ukraine strikes to compel Russia, right, or to meaningfully exhaust it economically. And so far, they have been impactful, but at the end of the day, it's clear that Russia still has the resources in the world to continue the war until 2027, and so does Ukraine. And Europeans, if they have to, are likely to come up with more funding for Ukraine next year. I think so. And we have not cut off military support; the things we are sending and providing that Ukraine depends on. We have made threats recently, unfortunately, to suspend it, but we haven't really cut cut these things off. So, even though negotiations continue, as they did last year, that's why I have a bit of a pessimistic view, and I could be wrong. I I hope I'm wrong. Those of us who have been working on this for a long time would like to see it end in the right way. Would like to see an acceptable outcome, if not a favorable one, to Ukraine. And also, a deal would like to end it in a way that it doesn't just lead to a third continuation of war. That's the pessimistic prospect I want to raise. A lot of folks think of this as let's just get to a deal, let's get to a ceasefire. Why won't Ukraine? Why won't Russia just agree? Because ceasefires can often just become rearmament periods to renew hostilities. Ukraine is very vulnerable. Some months after a ceasefire, eventually it'll have to demobilize all the men at the front line who want to go back to their lives to their families. Russia's force is largely contracted. Russia may not demobilize its force, and then Ukraine could be very vulnerable after a ceasefire is signed six to eight months into it, if there are no genuine security guarantees from the United States or any.

【译】——比如春天欧洲的选举，例如法国的选举，看整体局势会不会对俄罗斯更有利。就目前而言，除了乌克兰的打击行动之外，我看不到任何认真迫使俄罗斯让步的努力，也看不到能在经济上实质耗竭它的努力。到目前为止，那些打击是有成效的，但归根结底，现实很清楚：俄罗斯仍有资源把这场战争打到 2027 年，乌克兰也同样撑得住。而欧洲人若有必要，明年很可能还会为乌克兰拿出更多资金。——我也这么认为。而且我们没有切断军事支持，没有切断那些我们正在输送、提供、乌克兰所依赖的东西。不幸的是，我们最近威胁过要暂停，但还没有真正切断。所以，尽管谈判在继续——就像去年那样——这就是我对局势有些悲观的原因。我可能错，我希望我错。我们这些长久以来一直在处理这个问题的人，希望看到它以正确的方式结束：希望看到对乌克兰而言可以接受、哪怕不是有利的结局；也希望它的结束方式不只是通向这场战争的第三次延续。这就是我想提出的悲观前景。很多人把这想成"那就达成协议吧、那就停火吧，为什么乌克兰不肯？为什么俄罗斯不干脆同意？"——因为停火往往只是重新武装、重启战端的间歇期。乌克兰非常脆弱。停火后若干个月，它最终将不得不让前线所有想回归生活、回到家人身边的士兵复员。而俄罗斯的部队以合同兵为主，俄罗斯可能不复员。这样，如果得不到来自美国或其他任何——

[9600s - 9720s]

All they got was a piece of paper saying ceasefire. Russia had the opportunity to rearm, and the war is renewed. Except this time, Russia is in a much more advantaged position, given how things developed after the ceasefire. And for those not familiar with this war, it starts in 2014, and there were two failed ceasefires that never led to a political settlement: the first Minsk and the second Minsk agreement. And so, naturally, those of us who are familiar with this history both want to see a ceasefire, but also worry that a ceasefire is just a piece of paper with words on it, but doesn't lead to a lasting peace. And this is my concern that we will will potentially see a third iteration of this war. That how this war ends takes us to the next one, and there are plenty of conflicts like this: wars between India and Pakistan, Arab-Israeli conflict, Armenia-Azerbaijan. Well, there are multiple wars in that series. You're implying there's a low probability of success in the near term about the negotiation efforts. If if it turns out that they are successful, what does that look like? What do you think? What is the the inkling of light as we search for some optimism that they are successful? They get at least a partial ceasefire in place, compartmentalizing this war. Ideally, beginning with strikes against critical infrastructure by both sides, maybe also interdiction strikes in the Black Sea. Okay, and then maybe we start to see an agreement on a ceasefire for some period of days, to then show both sides' commitment to the fact that they want to engage in this political process. And you usually have to start somewhere, and having a ceasefire lasting some period of days, a week, two weeks, a month, is that beginning. Right now, I don't see Russia there willing to agree to something like this, because if they were, there's nothing that's been stopping them. That proposal's been on the table for a long time now.

【译】——国家的真正安全保证，他们得到的就只是一张写着"停火"的纸。俄罗斯趁机重新武装，然后战争重启——只不过这一次，鉴于停火后事态的发展，俄罗斯将处在有利得多的位置上。对于不熟悉这场战争的人：它始于 2014 年，此前已有两次停火都未能导向政治解决——第一次明斯克协议和第二次明斯克协议。所以自然而然，我们这些熟悉这段历史的人，既想看到停火，又担心停火只是一张写着字的纸，通向不了持久和平。而这就是我的担忧：我们很可能会看到这场战争的第三个回合。这场战争如何结束，把我们带向下一场战争，而这样的先例比比皆是：印巴之间的历次战争、阿以冲突、亚美尼亚与阿塞拜疆——那个系列里打了好几场战争。——你这是在暗示，短期内谈判努力成功的概率很低。那如果事实证明它们成功了，会是什么样子？你怎么看？在我们寻找一点乐观理由的时候，那一线曙光是什么？——至少达成分板块的部分停火，把这场战争切分开处理：理想情况下从双方停止打击关键基础设施开始，也许再加上停止黑海上的拦截性打击。好，然后也许我们开始看到一项为期若干天的停火协议，以此展示双方确实想投入这个政治进程的诚意。总得从某处开始，而一段持续若干天、一周、两周或一个月的停火，就是那个起点。就现在而言，我看不到俄罗斯愿意同意这样的东西——因为如果他们愿意，早就没有什么能拦住他们了：这个提议在桌上已经摆了很久了。

[9720s - 9840s]

Now they said their position is they don't want to agree to a ceasefire. They wish to settle all these complex, difficult issues, and only then will they offer a ceasefire. But the thrust of that position is there; they don't see a ceasefire and negotiations right now as a better alternative to fighting. At the end of the day, and that's because Putin's perception of the situation, from my point of view, is different from ours, and also I think differs to some extent from objective reality. Or at least I suspect that that's one of the inputs into his calculus. If we look at longer-term future, what does Ukraine look like after Zelensky? What does Russia look like after Putin? This war will leave lasting damage on Ukraine and on Russia. This fundamentally a tragic war, and even though I think we will judge Ukraine ultimately as having been successful in defending its independence, the cost will be great. And I do imagine that better future because I do think that ultimately this will pass. It's far from the first destructive European war, and unfortunately, in the history of Ukraine and Russia, it's far from the first destructive war that's that's been visited on these countries and on the people who live in them. So, in that regard, it's it's doubly tragic, and so many battles have been fought before in these same areas. It's it's a tragic history to see that in in 2020s we still have a large scale conventional war taking place here. I think one outstanding questions, and to me this is kind of an implied question, what you're asking about the future, is Ukraine going to be viable as as a state and as a nation after this war? I think so. Obviously, depends somewhat on.

【译】如今他们说，他们的立场是不想同意停火：他们想先解决所有这些复杂棘手的议题，之后才肯给出停火。但这个立场的实质在于：他们目前不把停火与谈判视为优于继续作战的选项。归根结底，这是因为，在我看来，普京对局势的认知与我们的不同，而且我认为在一定程度上也偏离了客观现实——或者至少我怀疑，这是他盘算中的输入之一。——如果放眼更远的未来：泽连斯基之后的乌克兰会是什么样？普京之后的俄罗斯会是什么样？——这场战争将给乌克兰和俄罗斯都留下持久的创伤。这从根本上是一场悲剧性的战争。尽管我认为，我们最终会把乌克兰评判为成功保卫了自身独立，但代价将是巨大的。我确实能想象那个更好的未来，因为我确实认为这一切终将过去。这远不是欧洲第一场毁灭性的战争；而不幸的是，在乌克兰和俄罗斯的历史上，这远不是降临在这两个国家、降临在生活于其间的人民身上的第一场毁灭性战争。就此而言，这是双重的悲剧：在同样的这些土地上，此前已经打过太多战役。看着 2020 年代的今天这里仍在进行一场大规模常规战争，这是一段悲惨的历史。我认为一个悬而未决的问题——对我来说，这差不多是你问未来时隐含的问题——是战后乌克兰作为一个国家、一个民族还能否存续？我认为可以。显然，这在某种程度上取决于——

[9840s - 9960s]

How the war ends, but more importantly, the kind of support Ukraine can hope to have from European countries. But if the rest of the nations in Europe, or at least the key ones, see Ukraine as European and as essential to European security and as a member of that community, they will see to it. Right? They will work with Ukraine to ensure that it's successful. It will have to depend on them for some time. The question about Russia is a challenging one because Russia is not a problem of one person, right? Russia is a large country with a lot of people in it. People matter because they make decisions. Vladimir Putin cares far more about Ukraine than other Russian elites. In fact, many of them, I think, are mystified by this war that he's launched. But nonetheless, some percentage of the population supports it, acquiesce to it, and hundreds upon hundreds of thousands of Russian men sign contracts to fight in it. This is the first large-scale conventional war that Russia has fought, where very few of the men were mobilized, and many essentially took money and contracts to fight in them. So it's not just Putin's war. The question we are, in some respects, dealing with is one of Russia's failed imperial transition, right? And the fact that Russia has not become a post-imperial nation state in Europe. And I want to make a broader point here that, to me, this is, in some respects, a a war of I hate to frame this way, but I think it's useful Soviet succession because it's resultant of that history that goes back to the dissolution of the Soviet Union, which historians that I know argue was both an event we remember it as happening and the dates that happened, but also a process, and that process is really one of imperial collapse, not just of the Soviet Union but the Russian Empire that lay under it as a foundation.

【译】——战争如何结束，但更重要的，是乌克兰能指望从欧洲国家得到何种支持。如果欧洲其他国家——至少是关键国家——把乌克兰视为欧洲的一部分、视为欧洲安全不可或缺的一环、视为这个共同体的一员，他们就会设法成全此事，对吧？他们会与乌克兰合作，确保它成功。乌克兰在一段时间内将不得不依赖他们。关于俄罗斯的问题则更具挑战性，因为俄罗斯不是一个人的问题，对吧？俄罗斯是一个大国，里面有许许多多的人。人是重要的，因为决策由人做出。弗拉基米尔·普京对乌克兰的在意程度远甚于其他俄罗斯精英。事实上，我认为他们中的许多人对这场由他发动的战争感到困惑不解。但尽管如此，仍有一部分民众支持它、默许它，而成千上万——几十万几十万——的俄罗斯男性签订合同去打这场仗。这是俄罗斯打的第一场大规模常规战争，其中极少有人是被动员入伍的，许多本质上是拿了钱、签了合同去参战。所以这不只是普京一个人的战争。我们在某些方面面对的问题，是俄罗斯帝国转型失败的问题，对吧？以及俄罗斯至今没有成为欧洲范围内一个后帝国民族国家的事实。在这里我想提出一个更宏观的观点：在我看来，这在某种意义上是一场——我不喜欢这样框定，但我认为这样看有用——"苏联继承战争"，因为它是那段历史的产物：那段历史要回溯到苏联解体。我认识的一些历史学家认为，苏联解体既是一个事件——我们记得它发生、记得那些日期——同时也是一个进程；而这个进程实质上是一场帝国的崩塌，不只是苏联的崩塌，还有作为其地基、藏于其下的俄罗斯帝国的崩塌。

[9960s - 10080s]

And this was fundamentally an imperialistic war, by a state and by an elite, by a leader that couldn't see another way of being and another vision for Russia. And so, one of the challenges we have is this failed transition from Russia. It's not just that Russia doesn't become democratic; is that whatever the political system may be, that the state has not stopped being a revisionist, revengeous, imperial state in Europe. But I don't see this as deterministic or something that is certain or forever. The truth is that Europe was filled with countries that were imperial powers that also did not surrender their empires very easily. Sherman famously said, "War is hell." I'd like to talk to you about the nature of war, having studied it for so long. What do those words mean to you? Feels like a sickness we can't kick. For all the the ways we've gotten better as people, as humans, throughout our evolution, the the one thing we keep coming back to is we we kill each other over disputes, and it's it's pretty crazy that we're still doing that when you really think about it. There's an aspect of war that's fascinating to me because it brings out the along that worst, it brings out the best, and we see some wonderful stories, and we're seeing that right now in Ukraine. But it is when you really, when I really step back and think about it, it is depressing that this is still the way that we resolve differences. It's crazy that we get to a point of who can kill more people to to come to a conclusion. So it's unfortunately no no end in sight, I would say, but it's a human condition. What gives you hope for the future of human civilization? We've been talking about some dark topics. You ever sit back, look up at the sky, both you have families.

【译】而这场战争从根本上是一场帝国主义战争，发动它的是一个看不到另一种存在方式、看不到俄罗斯另一种图景的国家、精英和领导人。因此，我们面临的挑战之一，就是俄罗斯这场失败的转型。问题不只是俄罗斯没有走向民主；而是无论其政治制度为何，这个国家在欧洲范围内始终没有停止做一个修正主义的、复仇主义的、帝国式的国家。但我并不认为这是命定的、必然的或永恒的。事实是，欧洲曾遍布那样的国家——它们都曾是帝国主义强权，也都曾极不情愿地交出自己的帝国。谢尔曼有句名言："战争就是地狱。"我想和你们谈谈战争的本质，你们研究它这么多年，这句话对你们意味着什么？——感觉像一种我们戒不掉的病。尽管作为人、作为人类，我们在整个演化过程中已在方方面面变得更好，但我们不断回到同一件事上：为了争端自相残杀。认真想想，我们至今还在这么做，这相当疯狂。战争有一面让我着迷：它激发出最坏的东西，也激发出最好的东西，我们看到过一些极好的故事，此刻在乌克兰就能看到。但当我真正退后一步去想，令人沮丧的是，这仍然是我们解决分歧的方式。我们竟然走到以"谁杀的人更多"来一决高下的地步，这很疯狂。所以很不幸，我看不到尽头，但这就是人类的境况。——什么让你对人类文明的未来抱有希望？我们聊的一直是些沉重的话题。你们有没有偶尔靠在椅背上、仰望天空——你们两位都有家庭——

[10080s - 10200s]

Look up at the sky and say, "I think we're gonna be. I think we're gonna be all right. We're gonna make it as as as a species. I'll give you two real quick here. We just my wife and I just watched the the Stanford Prison Experiment on on Netflix. You know I'm talking about the show that just came out. And at the end of that, at at at the end of it, you've got all these people that had nothing in common, and they just spent a bunch of time together, kind of trapped. They all became friends, and they came out the other side. And there was no political debates or anything like that. It was just cool to see so many different Americans in one spot come out the other side, friendly, having a good time. That was great. That was a pick me up. In the context of what we've been talking about today with war, and specifically in Ukraine, there is a new innovation on the robotics front that rolled out this past week, where they saved a soldier with a drone flying 30 kilometers away. So they put a soldier. It's a company called Skyfall, and they have essentially a essentially a casket underneath a drone. They flew this thing up to the front because they couldn't get this soldier out or two of them. They couldn't get them out any other way. They couldn't walk them out. They couldn't get the UGV ground robots in there to get them. CODE 9.2, the regiment I mentioned earlier. It's them, and they're one of the most innovative units. They flew these in on in one of them. The guy's buddies brought him out, packed him in, and they flew out. The other one, the guy crawled into the the container himself. And then once they are in that pod, they have the ability to talk back with the crew, so they're not alone, and they can. Hey, we're getting there. Just so you know, we're taking off. Here we go. A few more minutes. You're doing great. Can't wait to see you. And they're talking to him the whole way back. And the the final the the video they shared with it, one of the guys was in the hospital being treated the day after with his kid right there on his lap. And I thought that is so cool. So much money and technology and effort, and it wasn't just one drone went out.

【译】仰望天空然后说："我觉得我们会没事的，我们作为一个物种能挺过去。"——我快速给你们讲两个。第一个：我和我妻子刚在 Netflix 上看了《斯坦福监狱实验》，就是刚播出的那部剧。在结尾处，你看到所有这些原本毫无共同点的人，一起被困了那么一段时间，结果全都成了朋友，一起走了出来，中间没有任何政治争论之类的东西。看到那么多形形色色的美国人聚在一处，出来时和和气气、相处愉快——这很棒，很提气。第二个，与我们今天谈的战争、特别是乌克兰有关：机器人战线上一项新事物本周刚刚亮相——他们用无人机飞了 30 公里，救回了一名士兵。事情是这样的：有一家公司叫 SkyFall，他们做的基本上就是在无人机底下挂一个"棺材"式的吊舱。他们把这东西飞到前线，因为这名士兵——其实是两名——没法用其他任何方式救出来：没法让他们走着出来，也没法派 UGV 地面机器人进去接。就是「CODE 9.2」，我前面提到的那支部队——是他们干的，他们是无人机领域最有创新力的部队之一。他们用吊舱飞了进去：一个人的战友把他抬出来、装进去，然后飞了回来；另一个人是自己爬进吊舱的。人一旦进到吊舱里，就能和机组人员通话，所以不是孤身一人，可以听到："嘿，快到了。跟你说一声，我们起飞了。好嘞，还有几分钟。你表现得很棒，等不及见你了。"返程一路都有人和他说话。他们分享的视频最后一幕，其中一人第二天正在医院接受治疗，孩子就趴在他腿上。我觉得这太棒了：那么多的资金、技术和心血，而且出动的远不止一架无人机——

[10200s - 10320s]

And you push a button and it goes. There was shaping operations and coordination, and infantry and armor, and everything went into the role to get this one guy, 24 year old, I think, just go get him. Loved it. Can I can I give you another another anecdote? Also from CODE 9.2, ironically the same unit. Yes, it gives me hope. So, spend a lot of time studying war, dealing with war, and so you get to see, I think, some of the worst of humanity and also some of the best humanity, often in the same spaces. And if you travel the front line, you will see that, right? You will see basically the worst that humanity has to offer and the best, sometimes in the same day. Another anecdote from CODE 9.2, you know, just fantastic video, a challenging one, but a great one where they have two soldiers that are wounded. This is before Skyfall's system is available. This is some months back. Two soldiers are wounded. They're being evacuated by UGV, an uncrewed ground vehicle, and they're both in it, kind of like a think of it as a, it's like a small truck, and it gets hit by a Russian drone and it's disabled, and they're stuck out there. Okay, they're both wounded. They're stuck in this UGV, and the company commander, without waiting, sees them on video in a pickup truck racing to them, pulls up, backs up to them, gets out. He's a really big guy. You can see in the video, not wearing any body armor, didn't even bother to throw on his plate carrier. Goes and grabs one guy, throws him in the pickup. Goes and grabs the second guy, throws him in the pickup, and speeds out of there, and they all make it out. Obviously, incredibly dangerous. There are many moments like this that you can see in the war that they do give you hope. And also, I mean, I'll tell you the last part. This may seem silly. It's a grim enterprise. The thing that gets everyone through it is a dark sense.

【译】——不是那种"一按按钮就飞出去"的事。背后有预先塑造态势的行动、有协同，步兵和装甲全部投入，一切为了把这一个 24 岁——我记得是 24 岁——的小伙子接回来。就一句话：去把他接回来。我太喜欢这个了。——我能再讲一个轶事吗？同样来自「CODE 9.2」，巧了，还是这支部队。——请讲，这给我希望。——所以说，长年研究战争、与战争打交道，你会看到人性中最坏的一面，也会看到最好的一面，而且往往在同一片空间里。你若走访前线，就会看到这一点，对吧？你会看到人类所能给出的最坏与最好，有时就在同一天。再讲一个「CODE 9.2」的故事：一段很棒的视频——看得揪心，但了不起。两名士兵负伤了，这是在 SkyFall 那套系统可用之前，是几个月前的事。两名士兵负伤，正由 UGV（无人地面车辆）后送，两人都在车里——可以把它想成一辆小卡车——结果被一架俄军无人机击中，车辆瘫痪，他们被困在那里。好，两人都负着伤，困在这辆 UGV 里。连长没有等待，从视频里看到他们，立刻开着皮卡冲了过去，停稳、倒车到位、跳下车。他块头很大，你在视频里能看到：没穿任何护甲，连插板背心都懒得套上。走过去抱起一个人，扔进皮卡；再去抱起第二个，扔进皮卡，然后一脚油门冲了出去——所有人都活着出来了。显然，这危险至极。这场战争里有许多这样的时刻，它们确实给你希望。另外，我告诉你们最后一部分，也许听起来有点傻：这是一门残酷的行当，支撑所有人熬过去的，是一种阴暗的——

[10320s - 10429s]

Of humor, which you need to have in this profession as well, people people who who have been fighting for a long time, still you can see haven't lost their humanity at every level. Yeah, the humor, the the wide smile, yeah, the ability to, yeah, show love towards each other. It's great. That's a that's a man. That's such a complicated thing about war. It really does show some of the most beautiful aspects of human nature. It does. Sometimes we need tragedy to like really bring out the best in us. It's a stark contrast when you see it, right? It's contrasted much. It's in sharper relief than if you are going day to day in your normal life. Well, Preston, first of all, I want to say thank you. You gave me this gift. Absolutely, man. Then about that, I'm I'm gonna have to drink soon. And all of us, like ships in the night, were literally met at night and made this happen. This is a huge honor to finally meet the both of you. Thank you so much for everything you do in the world, and thank you so much for this conversation. This was awesome. Thank you, Lex. It's been great. Thanks for hosting us and for inviting us. Thank you for listening to this conversation with Preston Stewart and Michael Kofman. To support this podcast, please check out our sponsors in the description, where you can also find links to contact me, ask questions, give feedback, and so on. Thank you for listening, and hope to see you next time.

【译】——幽默感，干这一行你也得有这种东西。那些打了很久仗的人，你仍能看到他们从上到下没有丢掉人性。——对，那种幽默、那种咧开的大笑，对，那种彼此表达爱意的能力。这很棒。——这才是男子汉。这就是战争如此复杂的地方：它确实也展现出人性中最美好的一些面向。——确实。有时候，我们需要悲剧才能真正激发出自己最好的一面。亲眼看到时，反差极其强烈，对吧？它比日复一日的平常生活里呈现出鲜明得多的轮廓。——好，普雷斯顿，首先我要说声谢谢。你送了我这件礼物。——必须的，哥们儿。说到这个，我很快就得去喝一杯了。而我们所有人，就像夜航中交会的船，真的是在深夜里相会、促成了这一切。终于见到你们两位，是我莫大的荣幸。非常感谢你们为这个世界所做的一切，也非常感谢这次对话。这太棒了。——谢谢你，莱克斯。很棒。谢谢你的款待和邀请。——感谢收听本期与普雷斯顿·斯图尔特和迈克尔·科夫曼的对话。要支持本播客，请查看节目说明中的赞助商信息；那里也有联系我的链接，可以提问、反馈等等。感谢收听，期待下次再见。
